S S -arithmetic (co)homology and p p -adic automorphic forms
Guillem Tarrach

Abstract
. We study the S S -arithmetic (co)homology of reductive groups over number fields with coefficients in (duals of) certain locally algebraic and locally analytic representations for finite sets of primes S S . We use our results to construct eigenvarieties associated to parabolic subgroups at places in S S and certain classes of supercuspidal and algebraic representations of their Levi factors. We show that these agree with eigenvarieties constructed using overconvergent homology and that for definite unitary groups they are closely related to the Bernstein eigenvarieties constructed by Breuil–Ding in [ BD21 ] .



1.
Introduction

Let
p
be a prime number.
One notable feature of the theory of
p
-adic automorphic forms is that it has more than one incarnation, depending on what space of
p
-adic automorphic forms one is working with.
The most widely applicable constructions of such spaces (for example, completed cohomology and overconvergent cohomology) are defined in different ways using the cohomology of arithmetic groups (such as
\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
).
These groups contain important arithmetic information: if
\mathbf{G}
is a reductive group over a number field
F
, the cohomology of an arithmetic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(F)
with coefficients in a finite-dimensional representation of
\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})
can be computed in terms of automorphic representations of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.

The goal of this article is to study a new construction involving instead the homology of
S
-arithmetic groups (such as
\text{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}[1/N])
for a positive integer
N
) for finite sets of primes
S
of
F
.
More precisely, we will study the homology of such groups with coefficients in certain (usually infinite-dimensional) representations that play an important role in the representation theory of the local groups at primes in
S
,
and show that these also contain important arithmetic information.
For example, we prove that one may compute the
S
-arithmetic homology of some smooth or locally algebraic complex representations of these local groups in terms of automorphic representations as in the arithmetic case.
Similarly, the homology
p
-adic locally analytic representations is related to
p
-adic automorphic forms.
One can therefore use
S
-arithmetic homology to give a construction of
p
-adic automorphic forms that works in a more general setting than previous constructions in the literature.
This approach is better suited to study
p
-adic automorphic forms by using the representation theory of
p
-adic groups than, say, overconvergent cohomology.

1.1.
Automorphic representations and
S
-arithmetic cohomology of locally algebraic representations

Let us first explain the relation to classical automorphic representations.
As usual, it is more convenient to work with cohomology from the adelic point of view (as opposed to working directly with
S
-arithmetic groups), so let us fix a level structure
K^{S}
away from
S
, i.e. an open compact subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty})
, and assume it is neat.
We will write
H^{*}(K^{S},{-})
and
H_{*}(K^{S},{-})
for
S
-arithmetic cohomology and homology of level
K^{S}
– see
Section

5.1
for a precise definition.
They are isomorphic to a direct sum of the (co)homologies of
S
-arithmetic subgroups of
\mathbf{G}(F)
.
It is well-known that the cohomology of such groups with coefficients in finite-dimensional representations of
\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})
is finite-dimensional and can also be computed in terms of automorphic representations (cf.
[
BW00
, Chapter XIV]
), although in this case the only representations involved are those which are Steinberg at places in
S
.
The following theorem shows that when the coefficients are certain locally algebraic representations, a similar result holds with different conditions on the automorphic representations involved.

Theorem 1.1
.

For each
v\in S
, let
\mathbf{P}_{v}
be a parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}_{v}:=F_{v}\times_{F}\mathbf{G}
with Levi subgroup
\mathbf{M}_{v}
.
For all
v\in S
, let
\omega_{v}
be a supercuspidal representation of
\mathbf{M}_{v}(F_{v})
that is compactly induced from a finite-dimensional representation of an open compact-mod-center subgroup,
and let
W
be an irreducible finite-dimensional algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})
over
\mathbb{C}
.
Consider the
S
-arithmetic cohomology

H^{*}\left(K^{S},\left(W\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bigboxtimes_{v\in S}\Ind_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{G}(F_{v})}(\omega_{v})^{\text{sm}}\right)^{\prime}\right),

where
\Ind_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{G}(F_{v})}({-})^{\text{sm}}
denotes smooth parabolic induction and
({-})^{\prime}
denotes the abstract dual.
This cohomology space is finite-dimensional and admits a decomposition into
cuspidal
and
Eisenstein
direct summands, the first of which can be described in terms of cuspidal automorphic representations
\pi=\bigotimes_{v}^{\prime}\pi_{v}
for
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
satisfying the following conditions with respect to the data of
K^{S}
,
(\mathbf{P}_{v},\mathbf{M}_{v},\omega_{v})_{v\in S}
and
W
:

(i)

\pi^{S\infty}:=\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\not\in S\\
v\nmid\infty\end{subarray}}^{\prime}\pi_{v}
has non-zero
K^{S}
-invariant vectors,

(ii)

\pi_{\infty}:=\bigotimes_{v\mid\infty}\pi_{v}
is cohomological of weight
W
,

(iii)

for all
v\in S
,
\omega_{v}(\delta_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}^{-1})
is a subrepresentation of the Jacquet module of
\pi_{v}
with respect to the parabolic opposite to
\mathbf{P}_{v}
, where
\delta_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}
is the modulus character of
\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})
.

See
Theorem

5.20
for a more precise statement.
The condition on the supercuspidal representations
\omega_{v}
in
Theorem

1.1
is expected to be satisfied for all supercuspidal representations of reductive groups in this setting, and this is known for example when
\mathbf{M}_{v}
is a product of terms of the following forms:

•

A general linear group (cf.
[
BK93
]
) or an inner form of it (cf.
[
SS08
]
),

•

A unitary, symplectic or special orthogonal group, if
v
does not lie above 2 (cf.
[
Ste08
]
),

•

A tamely ramified group, if
v
does not divide the order of its Weyl group (cf.
[
Fin21
]
).

In fact, an analogue of
Theorem

1.1
can be proven even if the
\omega_{v}
are not supercuspidal, but we will only be interested in this case.
Thus, for the rest of the introduction we will assume that the hypotheses of
Theorem

1.1
are satisfied.
By using well-known results of Zelevinsky
[
Zel80
]
, the following theorem can also be deduced from
Theorem

1.1
.

Theorem 1.2
.

If for all
v\in S
,
\mathbf{G}_{v}
is isomorphic to
\text{GL}_{n/F_{v}}
, then for all finite-length smooth (or locally algebraic) representations
\pi
of
\prod_{v\in S}\mathbf{G}_{v}(F_{v})
, the cohomology
H^{*}(K^{S},(W\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi)^{\prime})
is finite-dimensional.

Both
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
have analogues for homology instead of cohomology, where one does not take duals in the coefficients.
Our methods work more naturally for homology, and this is the reason why the duals appear in these statements.

1.2.
S
-arithmetic cohomology of locally analytic representations

In order to study
p
-adic automorphic forms, we are led to consider similar cohomology groups as we vary some of the supercuspidal data
p
-adically. In order to make sense of this, we have to make a few changes. Fixing an algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and an isomorphism
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbb{C}
, we may change the field of coefficients from
\mathbb{C}
to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, and in fact to some finite Galois extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
which we may assume is large enough to split
\mathbf{M}_{v}
for all
v\mid p
.
Let
\mathbf{N}_{v}
be the unipotent radical of
\mathbf{P}_{v}
.
If
\Sigma\subseteq S
is a subset consisting only of places dividing
p
, then we will replace the smooth induction by locally (
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-)analytic induction at the places in
\Sigma
.
We may also view
W
as an algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\subseteq\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.
The representation
W
is then a tensor product of irreducible algebraic representations
W_{v}
for
v\mid p
, and we will write
U
for the tensor product of those corresponding to places outside
\Sigma
.
If
\delta_{v}\colon\mathbf{M}_{v}(F_{v})\longrightarrow E^{\times}
is a continuous character for all
v\in\Sigma
, then we may consider the
K^{S}
-cohomology of the
continuous
dual of

(1.3)

\displaystyle U\otimes_{E}\left(\widehat{\bigboxtimes}_{v\in\Sigma}\Ind_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{G}(F_{v})}(W_{v}^{\mathbf{N}_{v}}\otimes\omega_{v}\otimes\delta_{v})^{\text{la}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\left(\bigboxtimes_{v\in S\smallsetminus\Sigma}\Ind_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{G}(F_{v})}(\omega_{v})^{\text{sm}}\right),

where now
\Ind_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{G}(F_{v})}({-})^{\text{la}}
denotes locally (
\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-analytic induction.
In order to shorten notation, let us set
V_{\delta}=(\bigboxtimes_{v\in\Sigma}W_{v}^{\mathbf{N}_{v}}\otimes\omega_{v}\otimes\delta_{v})\otimes(\bigboxtimes_{v\in S\smallsetminus\Sigma}\omega_{v})
, and
\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\delta})
for
Equation

1.3
.
If
U
is trivial, the groups
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
act smoothly on this representation for
v\in S\smallsetminus\Sigma
and locally analytically when
v\in\Sigma
.
For brevity we will refer to it as a locally
\Sigma
-analytic representation of
G:=\prod_{v\in S}\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
.
By duality, studying the cohomology of the dual of
\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\delta})
is equivalent to studying the homology of
\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\delta})
. The same methods that prove the finite dimensionality in
Theorem

1.1
show that this is finite-dimensional, and can be used to give a criterion for its vanishing.
It is also interesting to study the
S
-arithmetic (co)homology of more general locally
\Sigma
-analytic representations of
G
.
A good source of such representations are the Orlik–Strauch functors
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}
.
We show that the
S
-arithmetic homology of many of the representations in the image of these functors is also finite-dimensional.
Under the assumptions of
Theorem

1.2
, we can also show finite-dimensionality for all such representations when they are of finite length.
We can relate (co)homology with locally algebraic and locally analytic coefficients via the following theorem.

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume that for all
v\in\Sigma
, the maximal split torus in the center of
\mathbf{M}_{v}
acts numerically non-critically on
U\otimes V_{1}
in the sense of
Definition

5.23
. Then, the inclusion of locally algebraic induction into locally
\Sigma
-analytic induction induces an isomorphism

H_{*}(K^{S},W\otimes\bigboxtimes_{v\in S}\Ind_{\mathbf{P}_{v}(F_{v})}^{\mathbf{G}(F_{v})}(\omega_{v})^{\text{sm}})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{1}))

(and similarly for the cohomology of their duals).

In terms of
p
-adic automorphic forms, this theorem gives a numerical criterion for a
p
-adic automorphic form to be classical.

Theorem

1.4
can therefore be seen as a classicality theorem.
Finally, we show that the homology groups behave well as we allow the characters
\delta
to vary in rigid-analytic families,
and use this to construct eigenvarieties.
More precisely, we prove the following result.

Theorem 1.5
.

Assume that
E
is sufficiently large. Fix
W
and
\omega=(\omega_{v})_{v\in S}
as above. There exists a rigid-analytic space
\mathcal{W}_{\omega}
defined over
E
parametrising locally analytic twists of
(\omega_{v})_{v\in\Sigma}
and a rigid-analytic space
\mathscr{Y}_{K^{s},W,\omega}^{\Sigma}
over
\mathcal{W}_{\omega}
whose fibre along a twist
(\omega_{v}\otimes\delta_{v})_{v\in\Sigma}
is canonically in bijection with the set of systems of eigenvalues for the action of a certain Hecke algebra on
H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\delta}))
.

1.3.
Relation to previous work

We will compare our constructions of eigenvarieties to previous constructions in the literature.
Let us assume that
S
is the set of all primes of
F
dividing
p
since this is the usual setting in which these constructions are carried out.
Let us distinguish between two types of eigenvarieties: “trianguline” or “finite slope” eigenvarieties, i.e. those that correspond to the case when all
\mathbf{P}_{v}
are Borel subgroups, and “paraboline” or “parabolic” eigenvarieties, which are the rest.
The former correspond to
p
-adic automorphic forms which are finite-slope for all Hecke operators at places in
S
, whereas in the latter you allow infinite slope at some operators.

First, we will compare our construction with that of overconvergent (co)homology.
For the trianguline case, a very general source is
[
Han17
]
, which encompasses previous constructions such as
[
Che04
]
or
[
Urb11
]
.
We prove that our eigenvarieties in these cases are isomorphic to these (and that they do not depend on the supercuspidal and algebraic data).
For the paraboline case, these were carried out by Loeffler
[
Loe11
]
when
\mathbf{G}
is compact at infinity and by Barrera Salazar–Williams
[
SW21
]
when
\mathbf{G}_{v}
is unramified for all
v\in S
.
We prove that the former are isomorphic to ours, and that the latter are isomorphic to a variant of it, where the supercuspidal data is replaced by a different smooth representation
\cInd_{J}^{G}(1)
(here
J
is a certain subgroup of
G
and
1
denotes the trivial representation of
J
), and extend their construction to the case where the group is not unramified.
We remark that
Theorem

1.1
suggests that that one should indeed include the supercuspidal data.

Secondly, we will also compare our construction with those based on completed cohomology.
In the trianguline case, these were carried out by Emerton
[
Eme06b
]
.
In the paraboline case, these have been constructed by Hill–Loeffler
[
HL10
]
and more recently by Breuil–Ding in
[
BD21
]
, who have called them
Bernstein eigenvarieties
.
We will focus on the comparison with Breuil–Ding, who work in the case where
\mathbf{G}_{v}\simeq\text{GL}_{n/F_{v}}
for all
v\in S
and
\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R})
is compact.
We prove in this setting that the reduced varieties underlying Bernstein eigenvarieties and our eigenvarieties are (essentially) isomorphic.
In
[
BD21
]
Breuil–Ding prove several important results about their eigenvarieties, their local geometry, and their connection to Galois representations.
As a consequence of the comparison, all of these results may also be applied to our eigenvarieties.

The comparison of our eigenvarieties with overconvergent cohomology follows naturally from our methods along with a comparison of two different finite slope functors. However, the comparison with completed cohomology requires more work, and is a consequence of an interpolation argument using
[
Han17
]
and Bergdall–Chojecki’s adjunction theorem for Emerton’s locally analytic Jacquet functor
[
BC18
]
applied to numerically non-critical points, which are dense in the considered eigenvarieties.

To the author’s knowledge, the first to study the
S
-arithmetic cohomology of locally analytic representations of reductive groups were Kohlhaase–Schraen in their article
[
KS12
]
.
The reader that is familiar with their work will notice that our work is heavily inspired by theirs, and indeed, our strategy for proving the above theorems is also inspired by theirs.
The ideas of Kohlhaase–Schraen have also inspired work by Gehrmann, Rosaria Pati and Rosso
[
Geh21
]

[
GR22
]

[
GP21
]
, who have explored the applications of
p
-arithmetic cohomology of locally analytic representation to the study of automorphic
\mathcal{L}
-invariants.

1.4.
Methods

As mentioned above, our strategy for proving
Theorem

1.1
and its analogue for locally
\Sigma
-analytic representations are based on the work of Kohlhaase–Schraen
[
KS12
]
, who proved finite-dimensionality and a vanishing criterion for locally analytic induction in a special case, namely when
F=\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{R})
is compact,
S=\{p\}
,
\mathbf{G}_{p}
is split, semisimple and adjoint and
\mathbf{P}_{p}
is a Borel subgroup.
Let us give an outline of this strategy adapted to our setting.
By our compact induction assumption on
\omega_{v}
,
W_{v}^{\mathbf{N}_{v}}\otimes\omega_{v}\otimes\delta_{v}
is isomorphic to a compact induction
\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}_{v}}^{\mathbf{M}_{v}(F_{v})}(\sigma_{v})
for each
v\in S
, where
\mathpzc{M}_{v}
is a (not too small) compact-mod-center subgroup of
\mathbf{M}_{v}(F_{v})
and
\sigma_{v}
is finite-dimensional.
The main idea is that one can relate the
S
-arithmetic homology of locally
\Sigma
-analytic induction to (a twisted version of) overconvergent homology
H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma,U})
.
Here,
J
is again a certain compact open subgroup of
G
and
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma,U}
is a locally
\Sigma
-analytic representation of
J
(and in fact, of a larger monoid).
Shapiro’s lemma shows that there is an isomorphism between
H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma,U})
and the
S
-arithmetic homology
H_{*}(K^{S},\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma,U}))
.
The representation
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma,U})
of
G
is also naturally a module for a Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}_{S,E}^{-}
, which can also be made to act trivially (in some sense) on
\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U^{\Sigma},V_{\delta})
and on the coefficient field
E
. We write
E(1)
for the field
E
equipped with this trivial action of
\mathcal{H}_{S,E}^{-}
.
The key technical result is the following.

Theorem 1.6
.

There is an isomorphism in the derived category of
\mathcal{H}_{S,E}^{-}[G]
-modules

E(1)\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}_{S,E}^{-}}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma,U})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\delta}).

One can formally deduce from this that a similar statement holds after replacing these representations of
G
by their
S
-arithmetic homology. A rough way of stating this is to say that the
S
-arithmetic homology of
\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\delta})
can be identified with (derived) eigenquotients of 1 for the action of a Hecke algebra at
S
on this twisted version of overconvergent homology.
In most cases it is possible to “untwist” this action, and the identification is with systems of eigenvalues in the usual overconvergent homology spaces that can be read off from the action of the compact-mod-center groups
\mathpzc{M}_{v}
from which we are compactly inducing the
\sigma_{v}
.
For example, if
\mathbf{G}_{v}
splits over
F_{v}
and
\mathbf{P}_{v}
is a Borel subgroup for all
v
, then
\mathcal{H}_{S,E}^{-}
has a basis consisting of Hecke operators associated to antidominant cocharacters of some fixed maximal split tori of the
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
; in this case one can take
\mathpzc{M}_{v}
to be the group of
F_{v}
-points of these tori, and if
\sigma_{v}
is irreducible (i.e. a character), a choice of uniformiser
\varpi_{v}
of
F_{v}
gives one such untwisting, and the eigenvalue of the Hecke operator corresponding to a cocharacter
\beta
is
\sigma_{v}(\beta(\varpi_{v}))
.

We use this identification to prove
Theorem

1.1
, as well as finite-dimensionality in the locally
\Sigma
-analytic case, the vanishing criterion we alluded to above, and the properties for representations in the image of Orlik–Strauch functors.

Theorem

1.4
can be deduced without much difficulty from these properties and the properties of the BGG category
\mathcal{O}
.
This is an example of one of the advantages that the
S
-arithmetic approach has over overconvergent (co)homology.
In the latter setting, the analogue of
Theorem

1.4
is proven in
[
Urb11
]
and in
[
SW21
]
by constructing an
ad hoc
version for locally analytic representations of
J
of BGG resolutions in the category
\mathcal{O}
.
In the
S
-arithmetic setting, however, where we can work with representations of the whole group
G
, the existence of such a resolution of locally analytic representations can be deduced
directly
from the BGG resolution in
\mathcal{O}
by applying the Orlik–Strauch functors to it.

Regarding
S
-arithmetic homology in families, the obvious reformulation of
Theorem

1.6
when the character
\delta
takes values in affinoid algebras remains true (and is also true for more general families of compactly induced representations than the
\sigma_{v}\otimes\delta_{v}
).
This again allows us to relate our
S
-arithmetic homology to overconvergent homology, but it is not as straightforward to deduce finiteness theorems from this relation, at least without assuming extra hypotheses (such as the existence of slope decompositions).
However, we prove a spectral theorem for compact operators on orthonormalisable Banach algebras to show these statements, and also to show that it is possible to glue these
S
-arithmetic homology groups for different affinoid algebras.
This allows us to construct eigenvarieties directly over the character space of the group
\prod_{v\in\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{v}(F_{v})
and not via Fredholm hypersurfaces over weight space, in a similar way to the constructions that use completed cohomology and Emerton’s locally analytic Jacquet functor (cf.
[
Eme06a
]
,
[
Eme06b
]
).
In fact, this spectral theorem can be seen as a variant of the spectral theorem underlying the construction of locally analytic Jacquet modules.
It is often the case when studying eigenvarieties that one needs to rely on Fredholm hypersurfaces in order to prove meaningful properties, and this situation is no different.
To do this, we also generalise the usual construction of eigenvarieties using Fredholm hypersurfaces and overconvergent homology to our more general setting and relate it to our other construction.
It is important to note that in order to make this work we have to work with the untwisted (i.e. the usual) version of overconvergent homology mentioned above.

1.5.
Organisation of the article

In the first section,
Section

2
, we will collect most of the results on the structure of
p
-adic reductive groups that we will need in the rest of the article (especially in
Section

4
) putting special emphasis on rigid-analytic aspects.
Next, in
Section

3
we will write down some of the properties of the Tor groups that will become ubiquitous in the rest of the article.
Especially, we will prove a vanishing criterion that underlies part of the proof of
Theorem

1.6
.

Section

4
contains the rest of the local part of the argument: we will define the relevant locally (
\Sigma
-)analytically induced representations and prove
Theorem

1.6
.
In
Section

5
we will move on to study
S
-arithmetic homology and its relation to arithmetic homology.
In it, we prove
Theorem

1.1
,
Theorem

1.2
and
Theorem

1.4
among other results on these
S
-arithmetic (co)homology groups.
Finally, in
Section

6
we construct eigenvarieties, study some of their properties (in particular, proving
Theorem

1.5
), and compare them to previous constructions.

1.6.

I am especially grateful to Jack Thorne for suggesting these topics and for his continuous and invaluable help during the development of this article.

1.7.
Notation

If
C_{\bullet}
is a chain complex, we write
H_{*}(C_{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{i}(C_{\bullet})
and similarly for cohomology.
By “module over a non-commutative ring”, we always mean left module unless explicitly specified.
In any case, the only rings in this article that are non-commutative are group rings of non-abelian groups (in particular, all Hecke algebras are commutative), for which we may consider right modules as left modules and vice versa in the usual way.
If
V
is a locally convex vector space over a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we will write
V^{\prime}
for its continuous dual, which we always equip with its strong topology.
All topological tensor products are projective tensor products.
By locally analytic, we always mean locally
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic.
We will usually use the same symbols to denote representations of a group and the underlying vector spaces or modules.

All rigid-analytic spaces appearing in this article are Tate rigid-analytic spaces.
If
A
is an affinoid algebra over a finite extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we will write
\text{Sp}(A)
for the corresponding affinoid rigid
E
-analytic space. If
X
is a rigid
E
-analytic space, we will write
\mathcal{O}(X)
to denote the algebra of global
E
-analytic functions on
X
.

If
\mathbf{H}
is an algebraic group over
E
, we will write
H:=\mathbf{H}(E)
for its groups of
E
-points, and similarly for all other letters.
We will write
X^{*}(\mathbf{H})
(resp.
X_{*}(\mathbf{H})
) for the group of
E
-rational characters (resp. cocharacters) of
\mathbf{H}
, or
X^{*}_{E}(\mathbf{H})
(resp.
X_{*}^{E}(\mathbf{H})
) if we want to emphasise the field
E
.
If
F
is a field extension of
E
, we will write
\mathbf{H}_{F}
or
F\times_{E}\mathbf{H}
for the base change of
\mathbf{H}
from
E
to
F
.
Similarly, if
F
is a subfield of
E
we will write
\Res_{E/F}\mathbf{H}
for the restriction of scalars.

2.
The structure of
p
-adic reductive groups

In this section we will gather some facts about reductive groups over
p
-adic local fields that will be used throughout the article.
Throughout this section, except in
Section

2.9
, we let
L
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with normalised valuation
v\colon L^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.
We normalise the absolute value
|{-}|
of
L
so that it sends a uniformiser to
q^{-1}
, where
q
is the cardinality of the residue field of
L
.

2.1.
Basic notation

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a connected reductive group over
L
,
\mathbf{S}
a maximal
L
-split torus of
\mathbf{G}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{Z}}
(resp.
\bm{\mathfrak{N}}
) its centraliser (resp. normaliser) in
\mathbf{G}
, and
\mathfrak{Z}=\bm{\mathfrak{Z}}(L)
(resp.
\mathfrak{N}=\bm{\mathfrak{N}}(L)
).
Let
\Phi=\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S})
be the relative root system of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{S}
and
W
be its Weyl group, which is naturally isomorphic to
\mathfrak{N}/\mathfrak{Z}
.
If
\alpha\in\Phi
is a root, we will write
s_{\alpha}\in W
for the corresponding simple reflection and
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}
for the corresponding root subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
.
We fix an ordering of the roots of
\Phi
and let
\Phi^{+}
(resp.
\Phi^{-}
) be the subset of positive (resp. negative) roots and
\Delta\subseteq\Phi^{+}
the corresponding root basis.
Write
\Phi^{\text{ndiv}}
for the set of non-divisible roots in
\Phi
, i.e. the set of
\alpha\in\Phi
such that
\frac{1}{2}\alpha\not\in\Phi
, and
\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}=\Phi^{\text{ndiv}}\cap\Phi^{+}
(resp.
\Phi^{\text{ndiv},-}=\Phi^{\text{ndiv}}\cap\Phi^{-}
).

Fix a subset
\Delta_{M}\subseteq\Delta
.
The subgroup
\mathbf{P}
of
\mathbf{G}
generated by
\bm{\mathfrak{Z}}
and the root subgroups
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}
, where
\alpha\in\Phi
runs through the positive roots and linear combinations of elements of
\Delta_{M}
, is a parabolic subgroup containing
\mathbf{S}
, and all parabolic subgroups of
\mathbf{G}
arise this way (for possibly different choices of
\mathbf{S}
and orderings of the root system).
Let
\mathbf{A}
be the identity component of
\bigcap_{\alpha\in\Delta_{M}}\ker(\alpha)\subseteq\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{M}
its centraliser in
\mathbf{G}
. Then,
\mathbf{A}
is the maximal split torus in the center of
\mathbf{M}
, which is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{P}
containing
\mathbf{S}
.
If
\mathbf{N}
denotes the unipotent radical of
\mathbf{P}
, then
\mathbf{P}
admits a Levi decomposition
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{M}\ltimes\mathbf{N}
.
We will write
\overline{\mathbf{P}}
for the opposite parabolic subgroup to
\mathbf{P}
and
\overline{\mathbf{N}}
for its unipotent radical.

The root subsystem
\Phi_{M}
of
\Phi
consisting of linear combinations of elements of
\Delta_{M}
is isomorphic to the root system of
\mathbf{M}
with respect to
\mathbf{S}
, and
\Delta_{M}
forms a root basis for the induced ordering of
\Phi_{M}
.
Set
\Phi^{+}_{M}=\Phi^{+}\cap\Phi_{M}
, and define
\Phi^{-}_{M},\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}_{M}
and
\Phi^{\text{ndiv},-}_{M}
similarly.
The Weyl group of
\Phi_{M}
is isomorphic to the subgroup
W_{M}
of
W
generated by the simple reflections
s_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\Delta_{M}
.

The inclusion
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\bm{\mathfrak{Z}}
induces an inclusion
X^{*}(\bm{\mathfrak{Z}})\subseteq X^{*}(\mathbf{S})
of finite index.
There is a homomorphism
\text{ord}\colon\mathfrak{Z}\longrightarrow X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
sending
t
to the cocharacter
\text{ord}(t)
defined by
\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle=v(\alpha(t))
for
\alpha\in X^{*}(\bm{\mathfrak{Z}})
. The kernel
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
of this map is the maximal compact open subgroup of
\mathfrak{Z}
, and
S_{0}=S\cap\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
and
A_{0}=A\cap\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
are the maximal compact subgroups of
S
and
A
respectively.
The quotient
A/A_{0}
is a free abelian group whose rank is the rank of
\mathbf{A}
. We fix once and for all a section
A/A_{0}\longrightarrow A
of the natural projection, and call its image
\Lambda
.

Most of the results of this section are consequences of standard facts from Bruhat–Tits theory
[
BT72
]
,
[
BT84
]
.
We refer the unfamiliar reader to
[
Tit79
]
for a survey of the topic, and we also note that many of the properties we will use are contained in the first two sections of
[
Mor99
]
.
Let
\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{B}(\mathbf{G},L)
be the (enlarged) Bruhat–Tits building of
\mathbf{G}
over
L
. Recall that this is the product of the Bruhat–Tits buildings of the adjoint group
\mathbf{G}^{\text{ad}}
of
\mathbf{G}
(that is, the reduced building of
\mathbf{G}
) and of the cocenter
\mathbf{G}/\mathbf{G}^{\text{der}}
of
\mathbf{G}
, where
\mathbf{G}^{\text{der}}
is the derived subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
.
Let
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{A}(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{S},L)
be the apartment of
\mathscr{B}
corresponding to
\mathbf{S}
; this is an affine space under the vector space
X_{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
with an action of
\mathfrak{N}/\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
.
Let us also write
\mathscr{A}_{M}
for the apartment corresponding to
\mathbf{S}
of the Bruhat–Tits building of
\mathbf{M}
.
There is a natural map
\mathscr{A}\longrightarrow\mathscr{A}_{M}
which is an isomorphism of affine spaces (cf.
[
BT72
, 7.6.4]
,
[
BT84
, 4.2.18]
).
Let
\mathscr{F}_{M}
be a facet of
\mathscr{A}_{M}
and
M_{0}
be its pointwise stabiliser in
M
.
The preimage of
\mathscr{F}_{M}
in
\mathscr{A}
is a union of facets.
Fix a maximal facet
\mathscr{F}
in this preimage, a chamber
\mathscr{C}
whose closure
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
contains
\mathscr{F}
and a special point
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.
Let
K
be the stabiliser in
G
of
x_{0}
, and write
J
for the pointwise stabiliser of
\mathscr{F}
. In particular,
M_{0}=J\cap M
(by
[
Mor99
, Lemma 1.13]
), and
J\cap\mathfrak{Z}=K\cap\mathfrak{Z}=M_{0}\cap\mathfrak{Z}=\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
.
We also let
I
be the pointwise stabiliser in
G
of
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathfrak{M}
the normaliser of
M_{0}
in
M
.

There exists a maximal torus
\mathbf{T}
in
\bm{\mathfrak{Z}}
defined over
L
containing
\mathbf{S}
which splits over a finite Galois extension
L^{\prime}/L
. For example, if
\mathbf{G}
is quasi-split we may take
\mathbf{T}=\bm{\mathfrak{Z}}
. We normalise the valuation and absolute value of
L^{\prime}
so that it extends those of
L
, and we also denote it by
v
and
|{-}|
. Let
\Phi^{\prime}
be the root system of the pair
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{T})
and define
\Phi^{\prime}_{M}
in the same way as
\Phi_{M}
.
We have restriction maps
X^{*}_{L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{T})\longrightarrow X^{*}_{L}(\mathbf{S})
and
\Phi^{\prime}\longrightarrow\Phi\cup\{0\}
.
Given
\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}
, we will also write
\mathbf{U}_{\beta}
for the corresponding root subgroup defined over
L^{\prime}
. We may embed
\mathscr{B}
into the points in the Bruhat–Tits building of
\mathbf{G}
over
L^{\prime}
which are fixed under the natural action of
\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)
, and similarly for the apartment
\mathscr{A}
of the former and the apartment
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
of the latter corresponding to
\mathbf{T}
. These embeddings are bijections if
L^{\prime}/L
is tamely ramified (cf.
[
PY02
]
).

2.2.
Root subgroups and Iwahori decompositions

Recall that we may associate to an affine function
\psi
on
\mathscr{A}
whose vector part is a root
\alpha\in\Phi
a subgroup
U_{\psi}
of
U_{\alpha}
as follows.
Given
u\in U_{\alpha}
, then
U_{-\alpha}uU_{-\alpha}\cap\mathfrak{N}
consists of a single element
m(u)
, whose action on
\mathscr{A}
is a reflection around a hyperplane that is perpendicular to
\alpha
.
If
\Psi(u,\alpha)
denotes the affine function with vector part
\alpha
that vanishes on this hyperplane, then
U_{\psi}
is the subgroup of
U_{\alpha}
consisting of the identity and those
u\in U_{\alpha}
such that
\Psi(u,\alpha)\geq\psi
.
Recall also that
\psi
is called an affine root if
U_{\psi}
is not contained in
U_{\psi+\varepsilon}\cdot U_{2\alpha}
(where
U_{2\alpha}=1
if
2\alpha\not\in\Phi
) for any
\varepsilon>0
.

If
\alpha\in\Phi
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we will write
\psi_{\alpha,r}
for the affine function on
\mathscr{A}
with vector part
\alpha
and
\psi_{\alpha,r}(x_{0})=r
, and
U_{\alpha,r}=U_{\psi_{\alpha,r}}
.
This is a decreasing family of neighbourhoods of the identity in
U_{\alpha}
.
The group
\mathfrak{N}
acts on
\mathscr{A}
, and for each
n\in\mathfrak{N}
and
\psi
as above we have
nU_{\psi}n^{-1}=U_{\psi\circ n^{-1}}
.
An element
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
acts on the apartment
\mathscr{A}(\mathbf{G}^{\text{ad}},\mathbf{S},L)
by translation by
-\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle
in the direction of
\alpha\in\Phi^{+,\text{ndiv}}
, so in particular we have
tU_{\alpha,r}t^{-1}=U_{\alpha,r+\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle}
. If
\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle>0
, then
tU_{\alpha,r}t^{-1}
is strictly contained in
U_{\alpha,r}
.
More generally, we can write any element
n
of
\mathfrak{N}
as a product
n=wt
, where
w\in\mathfrak{N}
fixes the special point
x_{0}
and
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
(see
Lemma

2.26
below), and then
nU_{\alpha,r}n^{-1}=U_{n\alpha,r+\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle}
.

Recall that we say that an open subgroup
H\subseteq G
admits an Iwahori decomposition with respect to
\mathbf{P}
if the multiplication map

(2.1)

\displaystyle(H\cap\overline{N})\times(H\cap M)\times(H\cap N)\longrightarrow H

is an isomorphism of
p
-adic locally analytic manifolds (note that
H
is automatically a
p
-adic locally analytic manifold).
As the multiplication map
\overline{\mathbf{N}}\times\mathbf{M}\times\mathbf{N}\longrightarrow\mathbf{G}
is an isomorphism of algebraic varieties onto an open subset of
\mathbf{G}
, the map
Equation

2.1
is an isomorphism of
p
-adic locally analytic manifolds if and only if it is surjective.
The subgroups that we will encounter admitting Iwahori decompositions will admit Iwahori decompositions of a special kind (which is rather common when the subgroups arise from Bruhat–Tits theory), which to avoid repetition we will call
rooted
.
Recall from
[
BT72
, Definition 6.4.3]
that a function
f\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called convex if for any finite family
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
of elements of
\Phi
such that
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\in\Phi
we have
f(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i})\leq\sum_{i}f(\alpha_{i})
. For example, if
\Omega
is a bounded subset of
\mathscr{A}
, then the function defined by
f(\alpha)=-\inf\psi_{\alpha,0}(\Omega)
is convex. We say that an open subgroup
H
admits a
rooted Iwahori decomposition
if it admits an Iwahori decomposition and, moreover, there exists a convex function
f\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

(2.2)

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}U_{f,\alpha}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H\cap N,

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}U_{f,\alpha}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H\cap\overline{N}

are isomorphisms of
p
-adic locally analytic manifolds for some, or equivalently any (by
[
BT72
, Proposition 6.4.9 (ii)]
) ordering of the terms in the products, where
U_{f,\alpha}:=U_{\alpha,f(\alpha)}\cdot U_{2\alpha,f(2\alpha)}
is a subgroup of
U_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv}}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
(where
U_{2\alpha,f(2\alpha)}=\{1\}
if
2\alpha\not\in\Phi
). As above, the condition that the maps in
Equation

2.2
be isomorphisms of
p
-adic locally analytic manifolds is equivalent to them being surjective.

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume that
H
admits a rooted Iwahori decomposition with respect to a standard parabolic
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
. Then,
H
admits a rooted Iwahori decomposition with respect to
\mathbf{P}
.

Proof.
Let
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
, resp.
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}
) be the standard Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
(resp. the unipotent radical of
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
, resp. the unipotent radical of the opposite parabolic
\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{\prime}
). Let
\Phi_{M^{\prime}}\subseteq\Phi_{M}
be the root system of
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
, and choose a convex function
f
realising the rooted Iwahori decomposition. We first show that the multiplication map

\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}U_{f,\alpha}\longrightarrow H\cap N

is surjective, and hence an isomorphism of
p
-adic locally analytic manifolds, for any ordering of the factors in the product such where the first roots are
\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
.
Any element
x
of
H\cap N\subseteq H\cap N^{\prime}
can be written as a product
\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M^{\prime}}}x_{\alpha}=(\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}x_{\alpha})\cdot(\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{M}^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M^{\prime}}}x_{\alpha})
with
x_{\alpha}\in U_{f,\alpha}
for all
\alpha
.
Both
x
and the first term in the last expression lie in
N
, while the second term lies in
M
. As
M
and
N
have trivial intersection, this means that the second term is 1, so
x
must lie in
\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}x_{\alpha}
as claimed. The same argument shows the corresponding statement for
H\cap\overline{N}
.

If
\Sigma
is a subset of
\Phi^{\text{ndiv}}
, write
U_{f,\Sigma}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma}U_{f,\alpha}
to simplify notation.
To finish the proof, we must show that the map

(H\cap\overline{N})\times(H\cap M)\times(H\cap N)\longrightarrow H

is surjective. This follows from the Iwahori decomposition of
H
with respect to
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
by writing

\displaystyle(H\cap\overline{N}^{\prime})\times(H\cap M^{\prime})\times(H\cap N^{\prime})

\displaystyle\simeq U_{f,\Phi^{\text{ndiv},-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}\times U_{f,\Phi_{M}^{\text{ndiv},-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M^{\prime}}}\times(H\cap M^{\prime})\times U_{f,\Phi_{M}^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M^{\prime}}}\times U_{f,\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}

\displaystyle\subseteq(H\cap\overline{N})\times(H\cap M)\times(H\cap N).\qed

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume that
H
admits a rooted Iwahori decomposition and let
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle\leq 0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
,

(ii)

t(H\cap\overline{N})t^{-1}\subseteq H\cap\overline{N}
,

(iii)

t^{-1}(H\cap N)t\subseteq H\cap N
.

Similarly, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle<0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
,

(ii)

\bigcap_{n\geq 0}t^{n}(H\cap\overline{N})t^{-n}=\{1\}
,

(iii)

\bigcap_{n\geq 0}t^{-n}(H\cap N)t^{n}=\{1\}
.

Proof.
If
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
and
\alpha\in\Phi
, then
tU_{\alpha,r}t^{-1}=U_{\alpha,r+\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle}
, and if
\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle\neq 0
then these are not equal to
U_{\alpha,r}
. Thus, if
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
and
\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}
, for any convex function
f
one has
tU_{f,-\alpha}t^{-1}\subseteq U_{f,-\alpha}
if and only if
\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle\leq 0
, if and only if
t^{-1}U_{f,\alpha}t\subseteq U_{f,\alpha}
. Using that
H
admits a rooted Iwahori decomposition, we see that in the first case above, (ii) and (iii) are equivalent to (i).
The second case follows similarly.
∎

In the same way as in
[
RTW14
, Remark 3.23]
, if
f\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}
is a convex function, we may define affinoid subspaces
\mathbf{U}_{f,\alpha}
of (the rigid-analytification of)
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}
such that each
U_{f,\alpha}
can be naturally identified the set of
L
-points of
\mathbf{U}_{f,\alpha}
.
Let us recall this construction.
We have fixed maximal torus
\mathbf{T}
defined over
L
containing
\mathbf{S}
which splits over a finite extension
L^{\prime}
of
L
.
There exists an isomorphism of algebraic groups

(2.5)

\displaystyle L^{\prime}\times_{L}\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}\\
\beta|_{\mathbf{S}}=\alpha\end{subarray}}\mathbf{U}_{\beta}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}\\
\beta|_{\mathbf{S}}=2\alpha\end{subarray}}\mathbf{U}_{\beta}

giving an identification

(2.6)

\displaystyle U_{f,\alpha}=\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}\\
\beta|_{\mathbf{S}}=\alpha\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,f(\alpha)}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}\\
\beta|_{\mathbf{S}}=2\alpha\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,f(2\alpha)}\right)^{\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)}.

Here, the indexing on the groups
U_{\beta}
is given by the image
x^{\prime}_{0}
of
x_{0}
in
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
and the valuation
v
of
L^{\prime}
extending that of
L
. The subgroups
U_{\beta,r}
for
\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}
are naturally the set of
L^{\prime}
-points of rigid
L^{\prime}
-analytic spaces
\mathbf{U}_{\beta,r}
:
a choice of special vertex
y_{0}
in
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
determines isomorphisms from the additive group over
L^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{U}_{\beta}
, and we take
\mathbf{U}_{\beta,r}
to be the preimage under these isomorphisms of the closed ball of radius
q^{-r-\beta(y_{0}-x^{\prime}_{0})}
centered at the origin.
This definition is independent of the choice of
y_{0}
.
The product

(2.7)

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}\\
\beta|_{\mathbf{S}}=\alpha\end{subarray}}\mathbf{U}_{\beta,f(\alpha)}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\Phi^{\prime}\\
\beta|_{\mathbf{S}}=2\alpha\end{subarray}}\mathbf{U}_{\beta,f(2\alpha)}.

is stable under the action of
\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)
on
L^{\prime}\times_{L}\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}
(it’s enough to check this on points over finite extensions of
L
by the argument used in the proof of
Lemma

2.25
below) and the affinoid
\mathbf{U}_{f,\alpha}
is defined as its quotient by this action.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv}}
and
f,g\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be convex functions with
f(\alpha)<g(\alpha)
and
f(2\alpha)<g(2\alpha)
if
2\alpha\in\Phi
. Then, the map of
L
-affinoid algebras
\mathcal{O}(\mathbf{U}_{f,\alpha})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(\mathbf{U}_{g,\alpha})
induced by the inclusion
\mathbf{U}_{g,\alpha}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbf{U}_{f,\alpha}
is compact as a map of
L
-Banach spaces.

Proof.
Let
R_{f}
be the
L^{\prime}
-algebra of rigid
L^{\prime}
-analytic functions on the affinoid space
Equation

2.7
for some fixed
\alpha
. The inclusion
\mathbf{U}_{g,\alpha}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbf{U}_{f,\alpha}
then corresponds to the bottom row of the diagram of
L
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R_{f})^{\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R_{g})^{\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)}.}

The map in the top row is compact, since it corresponds to a product of inclusions of closed balls with different radii. In this case, the rightmost vertical arrow admits a section as a map of
L
-Banach spaces by
[
Sch02
, Proposition 10.5]
, so the bottom arrow factors through the top arrow, and therefore will also be compact.
∎

2.3.
The groups
K
,
J
and
\mathfrak{M}

Lemma 2.9
.

The subgroup
J
admits a rooted Iwahori decomposition with respect to
\mathbf{P}
.

Proof.
Let
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
be the parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
associated to
\mathscr{F}
by
[
Mor99
, Theorem 2.1]
, whose definition we now recall.
We can associate to the facet
\mathscr{F}
the subroot system
\Phi_{\mathscr{F}}
of
\Phi
consisting of the vector parts of affine roots of
G
that vanish at
\mathscr{F}
.
Consider the reductive subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
containing
\mathbf{S}
whose root system is the closure
{}^{c}\Phi_{\mathscr{F}}
of
\Phi_{\mathscr{F}}
in
\Phi
. If
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
is its connected center, then
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
is the standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
whose standard Levi subgroup is the centraliser
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{G}
.
The condition that the facet
\mathscr{F}
is maximal in the preimage of
\mathscr{F}_{M}
in
\mathscr{A}
ensures that
\Phi_{\mathscr{F}}
, and hence also
{}^{c}\Phi_{\mathscr{F}}
, is contained in
\Phi_{M}
. In particular,
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
. By
[
Mor99
, Theorem 2.1]
and its proof,
J
admits a rooted Iwahori decomposition with respect to
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
, and hence also with respect to
\mathbf{P}
by
Lemma

2.3
.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

In fact,
[
Mor99
, Theorem 2.1]
shows that
J\cap\overline{N}
and
J\cap N
are contained in the pro-
p
radical of the parahoric subgroup of
J
corresponding to
\mathscr{F}
(see
[
HV15
, 3.6 Definition and 3.7 Proposition]
), which in turn is contained in (the Iwahori subgroup of)
I
.
In particular,
J\cap\overline{N}\subseteq K\cap\overline{N}
and
J\cap N\subseteq K\cap N
.
However, it is not necessarily true that
J\cap M\subseteq K\cap M
.

The group
K
will not in general admit an Iwahori decomposition, but the following result still holds.

Lemma 2.11
.

The multiplication map
(K\cap M)\times(K\cap N)\longrightarrow K\cap P
is an isomorphism of
p
-adic locally analytic manifolds.

Proof.
This is proven in
[
HV15
, Theorem 6.5]
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

The group
M_{0}
is the intersection of its normaliser
\mathfrak{M}
and the subgroup
{}^{0}M
of
M
consisting of elements
m
such that
v(\chi(m))=0
for all
\chi\in X^{*}(\mathbf{M})
, since these are the groups fixing pointwise the image of
\mathscr{F}_{M}
in the buildings of
\mathbf{M}^{\text{ad}}
and
\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}
respectively.
As
X^{*}(\mathbf{M})
can be naturally identified with a finite index subgroup of
X^{*}(\mathbf{A})
, we deduce from the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}/M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M/{}^{\circ}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(\mathbf{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Hom}(X^{*}(\mathbf{A}),\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Hom}(X^{*}(\mathbf{M}),\mathbb{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

that
\mathfrak{M}/M_{0}
is a free abelian group of rank equal to the rank of the torus
\mathbf{A}
and that
AM_{0}
is a subgroup of
\mathfrak{M}
of finite index. Note that this inclusion may be strict, for example this can happen when
\mathbf{M}
is
\text{GSp}_{4}
.
Inclusion
\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
induces an isomorphism
(\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathfrak{M})/(\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathfrak{M}/M_{0}
(this follows, for example, from
[
BT72
, 7.4.15]
).

Consider the submonoids of
M
defined by

\displaystyle M^{-}

\displaystyle:=\{m\in M:m(J\cap\overline{N})m^{-1}\subseteq J\cap\overline{N}\text{ and }m^{-1}(J\cap N)m\subseteq J\cap N\}

and
M^{+}=(M^{-})^{-1}
.
For any subgroup
H
of
M
, we will also write
H^{-}
for
H\cap M^{-}
and
H^{+}=H\cap M^{+}
.

Lemma

2.4
and
Lemma

2.9
imply that

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}^{-}

\displaystyle=\{t\in\mathfrak{Z}:\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle\leq 0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}\}.

Similarly,

(2.14)

\displaystyle A^{-}

\displaystyle=\{a\in A:v(\alpha(a))\leq 0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta\smallsetminus\Delta_{M}\}

since
v(\alpha(a))=\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(a)\rangle
for all
\alpha
and
\alpha(z)=1
for all
\alpha\in\Phi_{M}
.
Define also

\displaystyle A^{--}

\displaystyle=\{a\in A:v(\alpha(a))<0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta\smallsetminus\Delta_{M}\}.

This subsemigroup of
A
will usually not contain the identity element, but it is always non-empty.
Note that if
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
, then

\mu^{-1}(J\cap P)\mu=M_{0}\mu^{-1}(J\cap N)\mu\subseteq M_{0}(J\cap N)=J\cap P.

The following two lemmas are straight-forward generalisations of results of Schneider–Stuhler,
[
SS91
, Lemma 4.10 and Proposition 4.7]
.

Lemma 2.15
.

(i)If
m\in M^{-}
and
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
, then
JmJ\mu J=Jm\mu J
.

(ii)

Let
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
. If
x,\widetilde{x}
run through coset representatives of
(J\cap\overline{N})/\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}
and
(J\cap\overline{N})/\widetilde{\mu}(J\cap\overline{N})\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}
respectively, then
J\mu\widetilde{\mu}J=\coprod_{x,\widetilde{x}}x\mu\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mu}J
.

Proof.
Statement (i) follows from the fact that

\displaystyle mJ\mu J

\displaystyle=m(J\cap\overline{N})\mu\cdot\mu^{-1}(J\cap P)\mu\cdot J

\displaystyle=m(J\cap\overline{N})m^{-1}\cdot m\mu J\subseteq Jm\mu J.

For (ii), note that
J\mu J=(J\cap\overline{N})\mu J=\coprod_{x}x\mu J
and similarly for
J\widetilde{\mu}J
, so by part (i) it is clear that
J\mu\widetilde{\mu}J=\bigcup_{x,\widetilde{x}}x\mu\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mu}J
, so it’s enough to check that the cosets in the right-hand side are pairwise disjoint.
For a fixed
x
, it is clear that the cosets
x\mu\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mu}J
are pairwise disjoint for the various
\widetilde{x}
.
Thus, it’s enough to show that if
x\mu J\widetilde{\mu}J\cap\mu J\widetilde{\mu}J\neq\emptyset
then
x\in\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}
.
As
\mu J\widetilde{\mu}J=\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\widetilde{\mu}J
, there exist
\overline{n}_{0},\overline{n}_{1}\in\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}
such that
x\overline{n}_{0}\mu\widetilde{\mu}J=\overline{n}_{1}\mu\widetilde{\mu}J
.
Since
x\overline{n}_{0}
and
\overline{n}_{1}
are in
J\cap\overline{N}
, we deduce from the Iwahori decomposition that
x\overline{n}_{0}\cdot\mu\widetilde{\mu}(J\cap\overline{N})\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}\mu^{-1}=\overline{n}_{1}\cdot\mu\widetilde{\mu}(J\cap\overline{N})\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}\mu^{-1}
. In particular,
x\in\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}
as required.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

As
J\cap\overline{N}\subseteq K\cap\overline{N}
, the argument in the proof of (i) shows that for
m\in M^{-}
and
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
,

KmJ\cdot J\mu J\subseteq K\cdot m(J\cap\overline{N})m^{-1}\cdot m\mu\cdot\mu^{-1}(J\cap P)\mu\cdot J\subseteq Km\mu J.

In particular, it follows from
Lemma

2.15
(ii) that the subsets
J\mathfrak{M}^{-}J
and
JA^{-}J
of
G
(or more generally,
J\mathpzc{M}^{-}J
for any finite index subgroup
\mathpzc{M}
of
\mathfrak{M}
) are submonoids of
G
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
g,\widetilde{g}\in J
,
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
such that
g\mu JP\cap\widetilde{g}\mu JP\neq\emptyset
. Then,
g\mu J=\widetilde{g}\mu J
.

Proof.
We may assume that
\widetilde{g}=1
. Write
g=\overline{n}p
with
\overline{n}\in J\cap\overline{N}
and
p\in J\cap P
. Since
\mu^{-1}(J\cap P)\mu\subseteq J\cap P
, we have

g\mu J=\overline{n}p\mu J=\overline{n}\mu(\mu^{-1}p\mu)J=\overline{n}\mu J.

Hence,
\overline{n}\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}P\cap\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}P\neq\emptyset
. As
\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}\subseteq J\cap\overline{N}
, it follows from the injectivity of multiplication
\overline{N}\times P\longrightarrow G
that
\overline{n}\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}\cap\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}\neq\emptyset
. In other words,
\overline{n}\in\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}
, so
g\mu J=\overline{n}\mu J=\mu J
.
∎

Lemma 2.18
.

\mathfrak{M}
is generated as a monoid by
\mathfrak{M}^{-}
and
A^{+}
.
In particular,
\mathfrak{M}
is generated as a group by
\mathfrak{M}^{-}
.

Proof.
Let
\mu
be an element of
\mathfrak{M}
. Conjugation by
\mu
preserves both
N
and
\overline{N}
. The group
\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}
is contained in the subgroup generated by
U_{\alpha,r_{\alpha}}
with
\alpha\in\Phi^{-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
for some
r_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
, and similarly for
\mu^{-1}(J\cap N)\mu
. We may choose
s
sufficiently large so that for all
\alpha
,
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha,r_{\alpha}+s}
is contained in
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha,f(\alpha)}
, where
f
is a convex function giving the rooted Iwahori decomposition of
J
.
Choose
a\in A^{-}
satisfying
v(\alpha(a))\leq-s
for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
. Then, we have
a\mu(J\cap\overline{N})\mu^{-1}a^{-1}\subseteq J\cap\overline{N}
and
\mu^{-1}a^{-1}(J\cap N)a\mu\subseteq J\cap N
. In other words,
a\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
.
∎

2.4.
Congruence subgroups of
J

Next, we will introduce certain families of subgroups of
J
similar to those considered in
[
SS97
, §I.2]
. Let
s\geq 0
. Consider the subset
T^{\prime}_{s}
of
\mathbf{T}(L^{\prime})
consisting of elements
t
such that
v(\chi(t)-1)\geq s
for all
\chi\in X^{*}_{L^{\prime}}(\mathbf{T})
.
Choose an ordering of
\Phi^{\prime}
compatible with that of
\Phi
(so that the positive roots of
\Phi^{\prime}
restrict to elements of
\Phi^{+}\cup\{0\}
) and let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
be the associated Borel subgroup of
\mathbf{G}_{L^{\prime}}
containing
\mathbf{T}_{L^{\prime}}
over
L^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{U}^{\prime}
its unipotent radical and
\overline{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}
the unipotent radical of the opposite Borel.
Let
N^{\prime}_{s}
(resp.
\overline{N}^{\prime}_{s}
, resp.
U^{\prime}_{s}
, resp
\overline{U}^{\prime}_{s}
) be the subgroup generated by
\mathbf{U}_{\psi}
where
\psi
runs over all affine functions of
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
such that
\inf\psi|_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{F})\geq s
and the image in
X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
of the vector part of
\psi
(resp. the image in
X^{*}(\mathbf{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
of the vector part of
\psi
, resp. the vector part of
\psi
, resp. the vector part of
\psi
) lies in
\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
(resp.
\Phi^{-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
, resp.
(\Phi^{\prime})^{+}
, resp.
(\Phi^{\prime})^{-}
).
Write also
U^{\prime}_{M,s}=U^{\prime}_{s}\cap\mathbf{M}(L^{\prime})
and
\overline{U}^{\prime}_{M,s}=\overline{U}^{\prime}_{s}\cap\mathbf{M}(L^{\prime})
.
The image of
\mathscr{F}_{M}
(resp.
\mathscr{F}
) in
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}_{M}
(resp.
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
) is contained in a minimal union of facets, let
M^{\prime}_{0}
(resp.
J^{\prime}_{0}
) be the pointwise stabiliser of this union.
When
s>0
, we let
M_{s}^{\prime}
(resp.
J^{\prime}_{s}
) be the subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(L^{\prime})
generated by by
T^{\prime}_{s}
,
U^{\prime}_{M,s}
and
\overline{U}^{\prime}_{M,s}
(resp.
T^{\prime}_{s}
,
U^{\prime}_{s}
and
\overline{U}^{\prime}_{s}
).
When
s=0
, we let
M_{s}^{\prime}
(resp.
J^{\prime}_{s}
) be the stabiliser in
\mathbf{M}(L^{\prime})
(resp.
\mathbf{G}(L^{\prime})
) of the image of
\mathscr{F}_{M}
(resp.
\mathscr{F}
) in
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}_{M}
(resp.
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
).
The groups
N^{\prime}_{s},\overline{N}^{\prime}_{s},M_{s}^{\prime}
and
J^{\prime}_{s}
are all stable under the action of
\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)
.
Define
N_{s},\overline{N}_{s},M_{s}
and
J_{s}
as the subgroups of
\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)
-fixed points of the respective subgroups of
\mathbf{G}(L^{\prime})
.
Note that when
s=0
the notation for
M_{s}
agrees with our previous use of
M_{0}
.
Finally, let
J_{0,s}
be the subgroup of
J
generated by
N_{s}
,
M_{0}
(as defined in the previous sections) and
J\cap\overline{N}
and let
J_{1,s}
be the subgroup of
J
generated by
N_{s}
,
M_{s}
and
J\cap\overline{N}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
f\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the convex function defined by
f(\alpha)=-\inf\psi_{\alpha,0}(\mathscr{F})
. Then, the multiplication maps

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}U_{f+s,\alpha}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow N_{s},

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}U_{f+s,\alpha}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\overline{N}_{s}

are isomorphisms of locally
L
-analytic manifolds for any ordering of the terms in the products.

Proof.
We will only prove the statement for
N_{s}
since the other case is analogous. As usual, it is enough to show that the map above is surjective.
Let
f^{\prime}\colon\Phi^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the function defined by
f^{\prime}(\beta)=f(\beta|_{\mathbf{S}})
is
\beta|_{\mathbf{S}}\neq 0
and
f^{\prime}(\beta)=0
otherwise.
It is a convex function of
\Phi^{\prime}
.
Thus, for an affine root
\psi
on
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
with vector part
\beta
we have
U_{\psi}\subseteq U_{\beta,f^{\prime}(\beta)+s}
if and only if
\inf\psi|_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{F})\geq s
, so
N^{\prime}_{s}
is generated by
U_{\beta,f^{\prime}(\beta)+s}
as
\beta
runs through
\Phi^{\prime+}\smallsetminus\Phi^{\prime}_{M}
.
Hence, by
[
BT72
, 6.1.6]
multiplication
\prod_{\beta\in\Phi^{\prime+}\smallsetminus\Phi^{\prime}_{M}}U_{f^{\prime}+s,\beta}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}_{s}
is a bijection. Taking
\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)
-invariants, the result follows from
Equation

2.6
.
∎

In particular,
N_{0}=J\cap N
and
\overline{N}_{0}=J\cap\overline{N}
.
Moreover,
J_{0}=J_{0,0}=J_{1,0}=J
.
Similar decompositions to the ones in
Lemma

2.19
can be given for
M_{s}
when
s>0
.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
s\geq 0
.

(i)

M_{s}
is a normal subgroup of
\mathfrak{M}
.

(ii)

J_{s}
is a normal subgroup of
J
.

(iii)

J_{1,s}
is a normal subgroup of
J_{0,s}
.

(iv)

If
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
, then
\mu\overline{N}_{s}\mu^{-1}\subseteq\overline{N}_{s}
and
\mu^{-1}N_{s}\mu\subseteq N_{s}
.

(v)

(M_{s})_{s}
is a basis of neighbourhoods of the identity in
M_{0}
by open subgroups.

Proof.
Statement (v) follows from the corresponding fact for
M_{s}^{\prime}
.
We know the other statements for
s=0
, so we will focus on the case of positive
s
.
First, we note that the construction of
M_{s}
essentially coincides with that of the groups
U_{F}^{(e)}
in
[
SS97
, §I.2]
(except that instead of
H_{e+}
one should use
H_{e}
in the definition) and the arguments leading to the comments after
[
SS97
, Proposition I.2.6]
apply also in this case to show that
\mathfrak{M}
normalises
M_{s}
, which proves (i).
For the remaining statements, we make the following observations for
s,\widetilde{s}\geq 0
,
\psi|_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{F})\geq s
,
\widetilde{\psi}|_{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{F})\geq\widetilde{s}
,
u\in U_{\psi},\widetilde{u}\in U_{\widetilde{\psi}}
and
t\in T^{\prime}_{\widetilde{s}}
:

(a)

u\widetilde{u}u^{-1}
lies in the subgroup generated by
U_{\widetilde{\psi}}
and
J^{\prime}_{s+\widetilde{s}}
,

(b)

if the vector part of
\psi
and
\widetilde{\psi}
have images in
\Phi_{M}\cup\Phi^{-}\cup\{0\}
and
\Phi^{-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
respectively, then
u\widetilde{u}u^{-1}\in\overline{N}^{\prime}_{0}
,

(c)

utu^{-1}
is contained in the subgroup generated by
U_{\psi+\widetilde{s}}
and
T^{\prime}_{\widetilde{s}}
,

Indeed,
Item

(a)

follows from
[
Tit79
, 1.4.2]
,
Item

(b)
follows from the same reference and the fact that
\overline{\mathbf{N}}
is normalised by
\overline{\mathbf{P}}
, and
Item

(c)

follows from
[
BT72
, Example 6.4.16 (b)]
.
Facts
Item

(a)
and
Item

(c)
together imply that conjugation by an element of
U^{\prime}_{0}
or
\overline{U}^{\prime}_{0}
maps the groups
U^{\prime}_{s},\overline{U}^{\prime}_{s}
and
T^{\prime}_{s}
into
J^{\prime}_{s}
.
It follows from this that
\overline{N}_{0}
and
N_{0}
normalise
J_{s}
and, together with
Item

(a)
and
Item

(b)
, that
\overline{N}_{0}
and
N_{s}
normalise
J_{1,s}
.

According to
[
BT72
, 7.4.4]
,
M_{0}
is generated by
\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0}
and the subgroups
U_{\psi}
where
\psi
runs over all affine roots of
\mathscr{A}_{M}
such that
\psi(\mathscr{F}_{M})\geq 0
.
Again, it follows from
Item

(a)
,
Item

(b)
and
Item

(c)
as above that such
U_{\psi}
normalise
J_{s}
and
J_{1,s}
.
Therefore, taking into account (i), statements (ii) and (iii) will follow if we show that
\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0}
normalises both
\overline{N}_{s}
and
N_{s}
.
In fact, by the same arguments, (iv) will also follow if we show that conjugation by
\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{-}
(resp.
\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathfrak{M}^{+}
) preserves
\overline{N}_{s}
(resp.
N_{s}
).
This is indeed true: it is a consequence of the explicit description of the action of
\mathfrak{N}
on root subgroups and the corresponding statement for
s=0
.
∎

Proposition 2.21
.

Let
s\geq 0
.
The groups
J_{s},J_{1,s}
and
J_{0,s}
admit rooted Iwahori decompositions

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{s}\times M_{s}\times N_{s}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}J_{s},

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{0}\times M_{s}\times N_{s}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}J_{1,s},

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{0}\times M_{0}\times N_{s}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}J_{0,s}.

Proof.
The case
s=0
was proven in
Lemma

2.9
, so assume
s>0
.
To obtain the rooted Iwahori decomposition for
J_{1,s}
(resp.
J_{s}
) we can apply
[
BT72
, 6.4.9 and 6.4.48]
to obtain a similar decomposition for a minimal parabolic for
J_{1,s}
(resp. for
J^{\prime}_{s}
, and then take
\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)
-invariants to obtain one for
J_{s}
), and apply
Lemma

2.3
and
Lemma

2.19
.
The decomposition for
J_{0,s}
is proved in the same way as
[
Mor99
, Theorem 2.1 (ii)]
, as we now show. As usual, it’s enough to show that multiplication
\overline{N}_{0}\times M_{0}\times N_{s}\longrightarrow J_{0,s}
is surjective. Let
U
be the pro-
p
radical of the parahoric subgroup of
J
corresponding to
\mathscr{F}
.
Recall from the comment below
Lemma

2.9
that
\overline{N}_{0}=U\cap\overline{N}
and
N_{0}=U\cap N
.
For each
x\in J_{0,s}
, there exists
m_{0}\in M_{0}
such that
y:=m_{0}^{-1}x\in U
.
If we show that
y=\overline{n}mn
with
\overline{n}\in U\cap\overline{N},m\in M_{0}
and
n\in N_{s}
, then
x=(m_{0}\overline{n}m_{0}^{-1})(m_{0}m)n
will be in the image of the multiplication map above.
In other words, it’s enough to show that
U\cap J_{0,s}
admits an Iwahori decomposition. Let
f\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the function defined by

f(\alpha)=\begin{cases}r_{\alpha}&\text{ if }\alpha\in\Phi^{-}\cup\Phi_{M},\\
r_{\alpha}+s&\text{ if }\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M},\end{cases}

where
r_{\alpha}=-\inf\psi_{\alpha,0}(\mathscr{F})
,
and define
f^{*}
as in
[
BT72
, 6.4.23]
. Then, the Iwahori decomposition for
U\cap J_{0,s}
with respect to a minimal parabolic follows from
Lemma

2.3
and
[
BT72
, 6.4.9 and 6.4.48]
applied to
f^{*}
, and the decomposition with respect to
P
follows by
Lemma

2.3
again.
∎

We have the following version of
Lemma

2.15
for
J_{0,s}
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
. Then
J_{0,s}\mu\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}=\coprod_{x,\widetilde{x}}x\mu\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}
, where
x,\widetilde{x}
run through coset representatives of
\overline{N}_{0}/\mu\overline{N}_{0}\mu^{-1}
and
\overline{N}_{0}/\widetilde{\mu}\overline{N}_{0}\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}
respectively.

Proof.
As in the proof of
Lemma

2.15
we have
J_{0,s}\mu\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}=\cup_{x,\widetilde{x}}x\mu\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}
, and the cosets in the union are disjoint because the (bigger) corresponding
J
-cosets are.
∎

In particular,
J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s}
is a submonoid of
G
. From
Proposition

2.21
, it is easy to check that the inclusion
M_{0}\longrightarrow J_{0,s}
induces an isomorphism
M_{0}/M_{s}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}J_{0,s}/J_{1,s}
.
We will need the following refined version of this fact.

Corollary 2.23
.

The inclusion
\mathfrak{M}^{-}\longrightarrow J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s}
induces a monoid isomorphism

\mathfrak{M}^{-}/M_{s}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s}/J_{1,s}.

Proof.
It follows from
Lemma

2.20
(iv) and
Proposition

2.21
that the multiplication map
\overline{N}_{0}\times\mathfrak{M}^{-}\times N_{s}\longrightarrow J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s}
is bijective.
In order to prove the theorem, we must show
that if
\overline{n},\overline{n}^{\prime},\overline{n}^{\prime\prime}\in\overline{N}_{0},\mu,\mu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
and
n,n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}\in N_{s}
satisfy
\overline{n}\mu n\overline{n}^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}n^{\prime}=\overline{n}^{\prime\prime}\mu^{\prime\prime}n^{\prime\prime}
, then

\mu^{\prime\prime}M_{s}=\mu\mu^{\prime}M_{s}
.
This can be seen from
Lemma

2.20
(i) and (iv) since

n\overline{n}^{\prime}\in J_{1,s}
.
∎

Using
Lemma

2.19
and the paragraph above
Lemma

2.8
, we may view
\overline{N}_{s}
as the set of
L
-points of the rigid
L
-analytic space

\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},-}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}\mathbf{U}_{f+s,\alpha},

and similarly for
N_{s}
.
In fact, the Iwahori decompositions from
Proposition

2.21
allow us to canonically define rigid
L
-analytic spaces whose sets of
L
-points are
J_{s}
and
M_{s}
when
s>0
.
In order to do this, define
\overline{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}_{s},\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{s},\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}_{s}
and
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}_{s}
in the same way as
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}
and
\mathbf{N}_{s}
, and let
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}_{s}
be the open affinoid subspace of (the rigid
L^{\prime}
-analytification of)
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
defined by the same inequalities as
T^{\prime}_{s}
.
Define
\mathbf{J}^{\prime}_{s}=\overline{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}_{s}\times\mathbf{T}^{\prime}_{s}\times\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{s}
, so that we have a natural open immersion
\mathbf{J}^{\prime}_{s}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbf{G}_{L^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{J}^{\prime}_{s}(L^{\prime})=J^{\prime}_{s}
by
[
BT72
, 6.4.9 and 6.4.48]
.
Then
\mathbf{J}^{\prime}_{s}
is a
\text{Gal}(L^{\prime}/L)
-invariant subspace of
\mathbf{G}_{L^{\prime}}
, so we may define
\mathbf{J}_{s}
as its quotient by this action, and define
\mathbf{M}_{s}
similarly.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
. For any finite extension
L^{\prime\prime}
of
L
,
\mu\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}(L^{\prime\prime})\mu^{-1}\subseteq\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}(L^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.
By enlarging
L^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime}
and
L
if necessary, we may assume that
L^{\prime\prime}=L^{\prime}=L
.
We have already proved this case in
Lemma

2.20
.
∎

Lemma 2.25
.

(i)Conjugation by an element of
J
induces a rigid
L
-analytic endomorphism of
\mathbf{J}_{s}
.

(ii)

There is a rigid
L
-analytic isomorphism

\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}\times\mathbf{M}_{s}\times\mathbf{N}_{s}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbf{J}_{s}.

(iii)

Let
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
. Then, conjugation by
\mu
induces a morphism of rigid
L
-analytic spaces
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}
.

Proof.
It’s enough to prove the corresponding statements for the rigid analytic spaces over
L^{\prime}
.
Statement (ii) follows from the definitions and the argument in
Lemma

2.3
.
For (iii), given
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
we have an inclusion
\mu\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}_{s}\mu^{-1}\subseteq\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}_{s}

on rigid-analytic points
by
Lemma

2.24
.
There exists some affinoid open
\mathcal{U}
of
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}
through which the inclusion and conjugation by
\mu
maps
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}_{s}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}
factor (since
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to affine space), and the previous sentence then implies that conjugation by
\mu
on
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}_{s}
factors through
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}_{s}
, as
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{\prime}_{s}
is an affinoid subspace of
\mathcal{U}
by
[
BGR84
, Corollary 8.1.2/4]
.
The same argument shows (i) by taking into account that the inclusion and conjugation by elements of
J
,
\mathbf{J}_{s}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbf{G}
, also factor through some affinoid open (because we can embed
\mathbf{G}
into affine space).
∎

2.5.
Bruhat–Tits decomposition

Let
\widetilde{W}=\mathfrak{N}/\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
. If
H
is a subgroup of
G
or of
M
containing
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
, we will write
\widetilde{W}^{H}
for the group
(\mathfrak{N}\cap H)/\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
.

Lemma 2.26
.

(i)The natural homomorphism
\widetilde{W}^{K}\longrightarrow W
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

The group
\widetilde{W}
is isomorphic to the semidirect product
\widetilde{W}^{K}\ltimes(\mathfrak{Z}/\mathfrak{Z}_{0})
.

(iii)

We have
\mathfrak{N}\cap J=\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0}
.
In particular, the inclusion
M_{0}\subseteq J
induces an isomorphism
\widetilde{W}^{M_{0}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\widetilde{W}^{J}
.

Proof.
The map in (i) is injective, and the fact that
x_{0}
is special implies that it is also surjective.
Part (ii) follows from part (i) together with the exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Z}/\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\longrightarrow\widetilde{W}\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 1

For (iii), note that the groups
\mathfrak{N}\cap J
and
\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0}
are the subgroups of
\mathfrak{N}
fixing the points of
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}_{M}
respectively. As
\mathfrak{N}
acts on
\mathscr{A}
by affine transformations and both facets generate the same affine subspace, both subgroups are the same.
∎

Consider the following right action of
\widetilde{W}
on
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}
. If
\mu\in\mathfrak{N}
and
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
, write
\mu=n\widetilde{t}
with
\widetilde{t}\in\mathfrak{Z}
and
n\in\mathfrak{N}\cap K
according to the decomposition in
Lemma

2.26
(ii), and define
(\mathfrak{Z}_{0}t)\cdot\mu:=\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\widetilde{t}\mu^{-1}t\mu=\mathfrak{Z}_{0}n^{-1}t\mu
.
The following proposition generalises
[
Her11
, Lemma 2.20]
.

Proposition 2.27
.

(i)Inclusion
\mathfrak{Z}\xhookrightarrow{}G
induces a bijection

\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}/\widetilde{W}^{M_{0}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}K\backslash G/J,

where the action of
\widetilde{W}^{M_{0}}
on
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}
is induced by that of
\widetilde{W}
.
In particular,
G=K\mathfrak{Z}J
.

(ii)

For all
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
, there exist
t_{1},...,t_{r}\in\mathfrak{Z}
such that
tJ\mu\subseteq\bigcup_{i}Kt_{i}\mu J
for all
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
.

(iii)

Let
\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}
be a subsemigroup of
\mathfrak{Z}
defined by
\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t)\rangle\leq c_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
and some
c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
. Then, for all
g\in G
, there exists
a\in A^{-}
such that
gJaJ\subseteq K\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}J
.

Proof.
The Bruhat–Tits decomposition
[
BT72
, 7.4.15]
for
\mathscr{F}
and
x_{0}
implies that inclusion
\mathfrak{N}\xhookrightarrow{}G
induces a bijection
\widetilde{W}^{K}\backslash\widetilde{W}/\widetilde{W}^{J}\simeq K\backslash G/J
. Now,
\widetilde{W}^{K}\backslash\widetilde{W}\simeq\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}
by
Lemma

2.26
(ii) and it is straightforward to check that the right action of
\widetilde{W}
by multiplication corresponds to the action on
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}
defined above.
Thus, (i) follows from
Lemma

2.26
(iii).

Recall that
I
is the pointwise stabiliser of
\mathscr{C}
in
G
.
As explained in
[
Her11
, Proof of Lemma 2.20]
, one can deduce from
[
BT72
, Théorème 6.5]
that
(G,I,\mathfrak{N})
is a generalised
(B,N)
-pair, and it follows from the axioms of generalised
(B,N)
-pairs that if
\widetilde{n}\in\mathfrak{N}
, there exist
n_{1},...,n_{r}\in\mathfrak{N}
such that for all
n\in\mathfrak{N}
,
nI\widetilde{n}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}Inn_{i}I
.
Applying this to
\widetilde{n}=t^{-1}
shows that there exist
n_{1},...,n_{r}\in\mathfrak{N}
such that

tJ\mu=t\overline{N}_{0}\mu\mu^{-1}(J\cap P)\mu\subseteq tI\mu J\subseteq\bigcup_{j}In_{j}\mu J\subseteq\bigcup_{j}Kn_{j}\mu J.

By
Lemma

2.26
(i) and (ii), there exist
t_{1},...,t_{r}\in\mathfrak{Z}
such that
Kn_{j}=Kt_{j}
for all
j
, so (ii) follows.

Finally, in (iii) the statement for
g
depends only on the double coset
KgJ
, so by (i) we may assume without loss of generality that
g=t\in\mathfrak{Z}
. Let
t_{1},...,t_{r}
be as in (ii).
If
v_{p}(\alpha(a))
is small enough for
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
, then
\langle\alpha,\text{ord}(t_{j}a)\rangle\leq c_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
, so
t_{j}a\in\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}
and
tJa\subseteq\bigcup_{j}Kt_{j}aJ
.
∎

Corollary 2.28
.

If
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathfrak{M}
and
t\in\mathfrak{Z}
satisfy
Kt\mu J=Kt\widetilde{\mu}J
, then
\mu M_{0}=\widetilde{\mu}M_{0}
.

Proof.
We may assume without loss of generality that
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathfrak{M}
.
By the proof of
Proposition

2.27
(i) and
Lemma

2.26
(iii), we have

(\mathfrak{N}\cap K)t\mu(\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0})=(\mathfrak{N}\cap K)t\widetilde{\mu}(\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0}).

If
t\mu=kt\widetilde{\mu}m
with
k\in\mathfrak{N}\cap K
and
m\in\mathfrak{N}\cap M_{0}
, then
k=t\mu m^{-1}\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}t^{-1}\in K\cap M
and
\mu\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}=t^{-1}kt\widetilde{\mu}m\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}\in\mathfrak{M}\cap(t^{-1}(K\cap M)t\cdot\widetilde{\mu}M_{0}\widetilde{\mu}^{-1})
.
Thus, it suffices to show that the set in the right-hand side is contained in
M_{0}
.
The subgroup
{}^{0}M
of
M
defined in
Remark

2.12
contains all the compact subgroups of
M
, and hence also
t^{-1}(K\cap M)t\cdot\widetilde{\mu}M_{0}\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}
.
Therefore, the result follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{M}\cap{}^{0}M=M_{0}
.
∎

2.6.
Hecke algebras

Given a locally profinite group
H
, a compact open subgroup
H_{0}
and a commutative ring
R
, we will write
\mathcal{H}(H,H_{0})_{R}
for the convolution
R
-algebra of
H_{0}
-biinvariant compactly supported functions
f\colon H\longrightarrow R
. Explicitly, multiplication is given by

(f*\widetilde{f})(h)=\sum_{x\in H/H_{0}}f(x)\widetilde{f}(x^{-1}h).

Given
h\in H
, write
[H_{0}hH_{0}]
for the characteristic function on the double coset
H_{0}hH_{0}
.
These form an
R
-basis of
\mathcal{H}(H,H_{0})_{R}
. If
H^{-}
is a submonoid of
H
containing
H_{0}
, we will write
\mathcal{H}(H^{-},H_{0})_{R}
for the subalgebra of functions whose support is contained in
H^{-}
.
As usual, taking
H_{0}
-(co)invariants of
H^{-}
-modules gives modules for
\mathcal{H}(H^{-},H_{0})_{R}
in the following way:

Lemma 2.29
.

(i)If
\mathcal{M}
is a right
R[H^{-}]
-module, then the
H_{0}
-coinvariants
\mathcal{M}_{H_{0}}
have a natural right action of
\mathcal{H}(H^{-},H_{0})_{R}
via

[m][H_{0}\mu H_{0}]=\left[\sum_{x\mu H_{0}\in H_{0}\mu H_{0}/H_{0}}mx\mu\right],

where
[{-}]
denotes the canonical map
\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{H_{0}}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{M}
is a left
R[H^{-}]
-module, then the
H_{0}
-invariants
\mathcal{M}^{H_{0}}
have a natural left action of
\mathcal{H}(H^{-},H_{0})_{R}
via

[H_{0}\mu H_{0}]m=\sum_{x\mu H_{0}\in H_{0}\mu H_{0}/H_{0}}x\mu m.

Proof.
This is well-known when
H^{-}
is a group, and the same arguments show that this holds for submonoids.
∎

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
and
s\geq 0
. Then, in
\mathcal{H}(G,J_{0,s})_{R}
one has

[J_{0,s}\mu J_{0,s}]*[J_{0,s}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}]=[J_{0,s}\mu\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}].

Proof.
For
g\in G
, one computes

\displaystyle([J_{0,s}\mu J_{0,s}]*[J_{0,s}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}])(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in J_{0,s}\mu J_{0,s}/J_{0,s}}[J_{0,s}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}](x^{-1}g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\overline{N}_{0}/\mu\overline{N}_{0}\mu^{-1}}[J_{0,s}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}]((x^{\prime}\mu)^{-1}g),

A necessary condition for this sum to be non-zero is that
g\in x^{\prime}\mu J_{0,s}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}
for some
x^{\prime}
, and this is contained in
J_{0,s}\mu J_{0,s}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}=J_{0,s}\mu\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}
.
Conversely, if
g\in J_{0,s}\mu\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}
, then by
Lemma

2.22
we may write
g=x\mu\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mu}y
, where
x,\widetilde{x}\in\overline{N}_{0}
and
y\in J_{0,s}
.
If
x^{\prime}\in\overline{N}_{0}
, then the summand corresponding to
x^{\prime}
is non-zero if and only if
\mu^{-1}(x^{\prime})^{-1}x\mu\widetilde{x}\widetilde{\mu}y\in J_{0,s}\widetilde{\mu}J_{0,s}
, if and only if
\mu^{-1}(x^{\prime})^{-1}x\mu
lies in
\overline{N}_{0}
, if and only if
x
and
x^{\prime}
belong to the same coset of
\mu\overline{N}_{0}\mu^{-1}
in
\overline{N}_{0}
.
Thus, all the terms in the sum are zero except one, which is 1.
∎

Proposition 2.31
.

Restriction to
M
induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{H}(J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s},J_{0,s})_{R}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathcal{H}(\mathfrak{M}^{-},M_{0})_{R}.

Proof.
Let
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{-}
.
The Iwahori decomposition of
J_{0,s}
implies
J_{0,s}\mu J_{0,s}=\overline{N}_{0}\mu M_{0}N_{s}
, so
J_{0,s}\mu J_{0,s}\cap M=\mu M_{0}
.
Thus, there is a bijection
\mathfrak{M}^{-}/M_{0}\longrightarrow J_{0,s}\backslash J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s}/J_{0,s}
and
[J_{0,s}\mu J_{0,s}]|_{M}=[M_{0}\mu M_{0}]
.
This implies that restriction to
M
gives an isomorphism of
R
-modules.
By
Lemma

2.30
, restriction is compatible with convolutions, and hence it is an isomorphism of
R
-algebras.
∎

Corollary 2.32
.

Restriction induces an
R
-algebra isomorphism

\mathcal{H}(J\mathfrak{M}^{-}J,J)_{R}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s},J_{0,s})_{R}.

Proof.
The composition
\mathcal{H}(J\mathfrak{M}^{-}J,J)_{R}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(J_{0,s}\mathfrak{M}^{-}J_{0,s},J_{0,s})_{R}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(\mathfrak{M}^{-},M_{s})_{R}
is an isomorphism (by
Proposition

2.31
applied to
s=0
), and so is the second map.
∎

Corollary 2.33
.

The algebra
\mathcal{H}(J\mathfrak{M}^{-}J,J)_{R}
is isomorphic to the monoid ring
R[\mathfrak{M}^{-}/M_{0}]
, in particular it is commutative.
In fact, it is isomorphic to a subring of
R[X_{1}^{\pm 1},...,X_{\rank(\mathbf{A})}^{\pm 1}]
.

Proof.
It’s enough to show this for
\mathcal{H}(\mathfrak{M}^{-},M_{0})_{R}
, which follows from
Remark

2.12
.
∎

Remark 2.34
.

All of these results remain true if one replaces
\mathfrak{M}
with a subgroup
\mathpzc{M}
containing
AM_{0}
.

2.7.
A lemma on abelianisations

If
H
is a closed subgroup of
M
, let us write
\overline{[H,H]}
for the closure its commutator subgroup.

Lemma 2.35
.

Let
H
be an open subgroup of
M
containing
AH_{0}
as a subgroup of finite index for some compact open subgroup
H_{0}
of
M
. Then, the map
H/\overline{[H,H]}\longrightarrow M/\overline{[M,M]}
has finite kernel and cokernel.

Proof.
Let
\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}
be the derived subgroup of
\mathbf{M}
and
\varphi\colon\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}
be the simply connected cover of
\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}
.
Then, the commutator map
\mathbf{M}\times\mathbf{M}\longrightarrow\mathbf{M}
factors through
\varphi
.
The simply connected group
\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}
is isomorphic to the product of its simple factors
\mathbf{M}_{i}
.
We claim that
\overline{[\mathbf{M}_{i}(L),\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)]}
has finite index in
\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)
.
If
\mathbf{M}_{i}
is
L
-isotropic, then it follows from the Kneser–Tits conjecture
[
Pla69
, Theorem B]
that each group
\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)
is perfect, and the index is 1.
If
\mathbf{M}_{i}
is
L
-anisotropic, then
\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)
is compact, so
\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)/\overline{[\mathbf{M}_{i}(L),\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)]}
is an abelian, compact
p
-adic locally analytic group.
Its Lie algebra is an abelian quotient of the Lie algebra of
\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)
, which is semisimple, so it must be trivial.
Thus, the quotient
\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)/\overline{[\mathbf{M}_{i}(L),\mathbf{M}_{i}(L)]}
is a finite union of points, as required.
This implies that the inclusion
\overline{[\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}(L),\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}(L)]}\subseteq\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}(L)
has finite index, and since

\varphi(\overline{[\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}(L),\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}(L)]})\subseteq\overline{[M,M]}\subseteq\varphi(\mathbf{M}^{\text{sc}}(L))
