the second inclusion has finite index as well.
As the kernel of
\varphi
is finite, its Galois cohomology in degree 1 is finite as well, so we conclude that
\overline{[M,M]}
has finite index in
\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)
.
Hence, the map
M/\overline{[M,M]}\longrightarrow M/\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)
has finite kernel and cokernel, and it suffices to prove the statement of the lemma with the source replaced by the target.

Next, note that we may replace
H
by
AH_{0}
.
Indeed, if the composition

AH_{0}/\overline{[H_{0},H_{0}]}\longrightarrow H/\overline{[H,H]}\longrightarrow M/\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)

has finite kernel and cokernel, then the first map has finite kernel and the second has finite cokernel. But
AH_{0}
has finite index in
H
, so the first map also has finite cokernel, and hence the second has finite kernel.
In fact, the same argument shows that we may replace
H_{0}
with any open compact subgroup of it.
In particular, we are free to replace
H_{0}
with a subgroup of the form
H_{1}H_{2}
where
H_{1}
is contained in the center of
M
,
H_{2}
is contained in
\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)
, and
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
have trivial intersection.

Let us first show that the cokernel is finite. It’s enough to check that the subgroup of
M
generated by
\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)
,
A
and
H_{1}
has finite index in
M
. The homomorphism
M/A\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)\longrightarrow(\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{A}\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}})(L)
has finite kernel and cokernel (since the intersection of
\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}
and the center of
\mathbf{M}
is finite, hence has finite Galois cohomology), so it’s enough to check that the image of
H_{1}
in the target has finite index. But the target is compact and the image of
H_{1}
in it is an open subgroup, so the result follows.
Let us now show that the kernel
(AH_{1}H_{2}\cap\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L))/\overline{[H_{2},H_{2}]}
is finite. Since

AH_{1}H_{2}\cap\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)=(AH_{1}\cap\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L))H_{2}

and
AH_{1}\cap\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(L)
is finite, we are reduced to showing that
\overline{[H_{2},H_{2}]}
has finite index in
H_{2}
. This follows from the same Lie algebra argument as in the first paragraph.
∎

2.8.
A lemma on roots

Write
{}^{M}W
for the set of minimal length coset representatives of cosets in
W_{M}\backslash W
.
Alternatively, an element
w
of
W
belongs to
{}^{M}W
if and only if

w^{-1}\Phi^{+}_{M}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
.
Write
\ell
for the length function on
W
.

Lemma 2.36
.

Let
s\in S^{-}
and
\lambda\in X^{*}(\mathbf{S})
be a dominant weight. The minimum of the quantities
v_{L}((w\lambda)(s))
for
w\in{}^{M}W,w\neq 1,
is achieved at
w=s_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\Delta\smallsetminus\Delta_{M}
.

Proof.
Let
w\in{}^{M}W
be any non-trivial element.
We claim that there exists
\alpha\in\Delta\smallsetminus\Delta_{M}
such that
\ell(s_{\alpha}w)<\ell(w)
.
Indeed, this is equivalent to
w^{-1}\alpha\in\Phi^{-}
, so if this is false we would have
w^{-1}(\Delta\smallsetminus\Delta_{M})\subseteq\Phi^{+}
, and hence
w^{-1}\Phi^{+}\subseteq\Phi^{+}
, so
\ell(w^{-1})=w^{-1}\Phi^{+}\cap\Phi^{-}=0
, which contradicts our assumption that
w
is non-trivial.
So let
\alpha\in\Delta\smallsetminus\Delta_{M}
be such a root.
Clearly,
s_{\alpha}\in{}^{M}W
, so it’s enough to show that
v_{L}((s_{\alpha}\lambda)(s))\leq v_{L}((w\lambda)(s))
.
As
s\in S^{-}
, by
Equation

2.13
it’s enough to check that
s_{\alpha}\lambda\geq w\lambda
, which follows from the fact that
s_{\alpha}\leq w
in the Bruhat order.
∎

2.9.
Direct products of groups

When we apply the results we have proven so far in
Section

4
, we will do so not for a reductive group over a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, but rather a product of reductive groups over finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
for different primes
\ell
. We will now introduce this setting.

Let
\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}
be prime numbers (each of which can appear more than once). For each
i
, let
L_{i}
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell_{i}}
and
\mathbf{G}_{i}
a reductive group over
L_{i}
. We make the same definitions as in
Section

2.1
and write them with subindex
i
. Thus, for example we have subgroups
\mathbf{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{i}\subseteq\bm{\mathfrak{Z}}_{i}\subseteq\mathbf{M}_{i}\subseteq\mathbf{P}_{i}\subseteq\mathbf{G}_{i}
,
and the pair
(\mathbf{G}_{i},\mathbf{S}_{i})
has root system
\Phi_{i}
with simple roots
\Delta_{i}
and Weyl group
W_{i}
. We set
G_{i}=\mathbf{G}_{i}(L_{i})
and
G=\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}
, and similarly for other groups. We will mostly view these simply as locally profinite groups, or sometimes as products of locally profinite groups and
p
-adic locally analytic groups (if some of the
\ell_{i}
are
p
). We will also set
\Phi=\coprod_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i}
, etc.

All of the results we have proven thus far remain valid for this setting after making the appropriate modifications to the definitions if necessary. For example, we say that a subgroup of the form
H=\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}
with
H_{i}\subseteq G_{i}
admits an Iwahori decomposition if the product
(H\cap\overline{N})\times(H\cap M)\times(H\cap N)\longrightarrow H
is a homeomorphism (or equivalently, surjective), and that it is rooted if there exists a convex function
f\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the multiplication map
\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{\text{ndiv},+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}}U_{f,\alpha}\longrightarrow H\cap N
is a homeomorphism (or equivalently, surjective) for any ordering of its factors and similarly for
\overline{N}
. Here, given
\alpha\in\Phi
belonging to
\Phi_{i}
, the group
U_{f,\alpha}
is the group
U_{f|_{\Phi_{i}},\alpha}\times\prod_{j\neq i}\{1\}
. Then, it is clear that
H
admits a (rooted) Iwahori decomposition if and only if each of the
H_{i}
does.

Because all of these results generalise immediately to this setting, we will not repeat them and instead quote directly the original results.
Let us also mention that if
H_{i,0}
is a compact open subgroup of
G_{i}
and
H_{i}^{-}
a submonoid containing
H_{i,0}
, then there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{H}(\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{i}^{-},\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{i,0})_{R}\simeq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}(H_{i}^{-},H_{i,0})_{R}
.

3.
A vanishing criterion for
Tor

The key technical result in
[
KS12
]
(as well as in this article) is the vanishing of certain Tor groups with respect to the monoid algebra of
\Lambda^{-}
.
To obtain them, Kohlhaase–Schraen use that, in their setting, this monoid algebra is isomorphic to a polynomial algebra, and they carry out an explicit computation.
In this section, we will give a more general (but still specifically tailored to our needs) criterion for the vanishing of the relevant Tor groups, which we obtain by reducing to the case of polynomial algebras.
Let us forget the notation from previous sections.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and
\Lambda
a free abelian group of finite rank, whose group law we write using multiplicative notation, and let
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda^{-}\subseteq\Lambda
be submonoids satisfying
\Lambda^{\prime}(\Lambda^{\prime})^{-1}=\Lambda
. If
\chi\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow R^{\times}
is a group homomorphism, we will write
R(\chi)
for the
R[\Lambda]
-module whose underlying
R
-module is
R
and where
\Lambda
acts via
\chi
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an
R[\Lambda^{-}]
-module and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(i)

The natural
R[\Lambda^{-}]
-module map
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda]}_{q}(R(\chi),R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\mathcal{M})
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

Inclusion
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda^{-}
induces an isomorphism
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{\prime}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})
.

(iii)

Assume that
\mathcal{M}
contains an
R[\Lambda^{-}]
-submodule
\mathcal{M}^{-}
such that for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}
such that
\lambda m\in\mathcal{M}^{-}
. Then, inclusion
\mathcal{M}^{-}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
induces an isomorphism

\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M}^{-})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})

Proof.
Note that
R[\Lambda]
is the localisation of
R[\Lambda^{-}]
at
S=\Lambda^{-}
. Hence,
S^{-1}\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})
is isomorphic to
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda]}_{q}(S^{-1}R(\chi),S^{-1}\mathcal{M})
. But
R(\chi)
is an
R[\Lambda]
-module, and hence so is

\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})
, and thus
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})\simeq\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda]}_{q}(R(\chi),R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\mathcal{M})
, which proves part (i).

By (i) applied to both
\Lambda^{-}
and
\Lambda
, statement (ii) will follow if we show that the natural map
R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{\prime}]}\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\mathcal{M}
is an isomorphism. It is clearly surjective, and its kernel is generated by elements of the form
\lambda\otimes m-1\otimes\lambda m
with
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
. Writing
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}(\mu^{\prime})^{-1}
with
\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}
we see that

\mu^{\prime}(\lambda\otimes m)=\lambda^{\prime}\otimes m=1\otimes\lambda^{\prime}m=\mu^{\prime}(1\otimes\lambda m)\text{ in }R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{\prime}]}\mathcal{M}.

As
\mu^{\prime}
is invertible in
R[\Lambda]
, this means that
\lambda\otimes m-1\otimes\lambda m=0
, so the above homomorphism is also injective.

For statement (iii) it’s enough to show by (i) that the natural map

R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\mathcal{M}^{-}\longrightarrow R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\mathcal{M}

is an isomorphism. As localisation is exact, it’s enough to show that
R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}(\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{-})
vanishes. This follows from our assumption on
\mathcal{M}^{-}
, since any element of the form
\mu\otimes m
can also be written as
\mu\lambda^{-1}\otimes\lambda m
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}
.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that
\mathcal{M}
admits a decomposition
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{+}\oplus\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
as
R
-modules satisfying

(a)

\lambda\mathcal{M}_{\mu}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\lambda\mu}
for
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda^{-}
,

(b)

for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
there exists
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}
such that
\lambda m\in\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
.

Then, the same conditions hold for
\Lambda^{\prime}
instead of
\Lambda^{-}
, and there is an isomorphism

\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})\simeq\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{\prime}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}).

Proof.
We first show that
Item

(a)
and
Item

(b)
remain valid if we replace
\Lambda^{-}
by
\Lambda^{\prime}
everywhere. This is immediate for
Item

(a)
. Given
m\in\mathcal{M}
, by
Item

(b)
there exist
\lambda,\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda^{-}
and
m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\lambda_{i}}
for
i=1,...,n
such that
\lambda m=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}
. We may write
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}(\mu^{\prime})^{-1}
with
\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{\prime}_{i}(\mu^{\prime}_{i})^{-1}
similarly. Write
\mu^{(i)}=\mu^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\mu^{\prime}_{i-1}\mu^{\prime}_{i+1}\cdots\mu^{\prime}_{n}
. Then,

(\lambda^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\mu^{\prime}_{n})m=\mu^{\prime}(\mu^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\mu^{\prime}_{n})\lambda m=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu^{\prime}\mu^{(i)}\cdot\mu^{\prime}_{i}m_{i}\in\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}_{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{(i)}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}}\subseteq\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{\mu}.

This proves the first statement. The second statement follows from applying
Lemma

3.1
(iii) to
\Lambda^{\prime}
and
\bigoplus_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\beta_{1},...,\beta_{r}\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be linearly independent
\mathbb{Z}
-linear maps, and consider
\Lambda^{-}:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda:\beta_{i}(\lambda)\leq 0
for all
i\}
. Then:

(i)

\Lambda^{-}
contains a free abelian monoid
\Lambda^{\prime}
of rank
d
such that
\Lambda^{\prime}(\Lambda^{\prime})^{-1}=\Lambda
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{M}
is an
R[\Lambda^{-}]
-module as in
Lemma

3.2
, then
\text{Tor}_{q}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})=0
for
q>0
.

Proof.
Part (i) is a consequence of
[
CLS11
, Theorem 11.1.9]
.
Indeed, let
\sigma
be the cone of
\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
defined by the same inequalities as
\Lambda^{-}
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be any cone of maximal dimension in the refinement
\Sigma^{\prime}
obtained by applying this theorem to the fan
\Sigma
consisting of
\sigma
and its faces.
Then,
\sigma^{\prime}
is a smooth cone, which means that
\Lambda^{\prime}:=\sigma^{\prime}\cap\Lambda
is a free abelian monoid as described, thus proving (i).
By
Lemma

3.2
, part (ii) is equivalent to
\text{Tor}_{q}^{R[\Lambda^{\prime}]}(R(\chi),\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda})=0
for
q>0
, which follows from
Lemma

3.4
below and
[
Bou06
, X.9.6, Proposition 5]
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
R
be a ring,
R[X_{1},...,X_{d}]
a polynomial ring in
d
variables with its usual
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}
grading. Let
\mathcal{M}=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}}\mathcal{M}_{m}
be a graded
R[X_{1},...,X_{d}]
-module. Let
a_{1},...,a_{d}\in R^{\times}
, and set
Y_{i}:=X_{i}-a_{i}
. Then, the sequence
(Y_{1},...,Y_{d})
of endomorphisms of
\mathcal{M}
is regular, i.e. if
1\leq j\leq d
, then the endomorphism of
\mathcal{M}/\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\Im(Y_{i})
induced by
Y_{j}
is injective.

Proof.
This statement is a generalisation of
[
KS12
, Theorem 2.6]
, and the same proof applies to this level of generality.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}
be a free abelian group of finite rank containing
\Lambda
as a finite index subgroup,
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
a submonoid of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
such that
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-})^{-1}=\widetilde{\Lambda}
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}\Lambda
.
Let

\Lambda^{-}=\Lambda\cap\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
.
Assume that the index
[\widetilde{\Lambda}:\Lambda]
is invertible in
R
.
Then, for any
R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]
-module
\mathcal{M}
, character
\chi\colon\widetilde{\Lambda}\longrightarrow R^{\times}
and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, the natural
R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]
-module homomorphism
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\text{Tor}^{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})
identifies the target with a direct summand of the source.

Proof.
We begin by observing that for any
R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]
-module
M
, the natural map

R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}M\longrightarrow R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]\otimes_{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}M

is an isomorphism.
Indeed, as
R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}M=R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]\otimes_{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}M
, it’s enough to prove that the natural map
R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]\longrightarrow R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]
is an isomorphism. This is a localisation of the
R[\Lambda^{-}]
-module inclusion
R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]\xhookrightarrow{}R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]
, and hence is injective, and surjectivity follows from the condition
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}\Lambda
.
Apply this to
\mathcal{M}
to see that, if
\mathcal{N}=R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]\otimes_{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}\mathcal{M}
, then by
Lemma

3.1
(i) it’s enough to show that the homomorphism

\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{N})\longrightarrow\text{Tor}^{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{N})

identifies the target with an
R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]
-module direct summand of the source.
Let
P_{\bullet}
be a resolution of
R(\chi)
by free
R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]
-modules (in particular by flat
R[\Lambda]
-modules).
It’s enough to show that the natural map
P_{\bullet}\otimes_{R[\Lambda]}\mathcal{N}\longrightarrow P_{\bullet}\otimes_{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]}\mathcal{N}
identifies the target with a direct summand of the source.
This holds since
P_{\bullet}\otimes_{R[\Lambda]}\mathcal{N}
is isomorphic to
(P_{\bullet}\otimes_{R[\Lambda]}R[\widetilde{\Lambda}])\otimes_{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]}\mathcal{N}
and the map
p\otimes\lambda\longmapsto\lambda p\colon P_{\bullet}\otimes_{R[\Lambda]}R[\widetilde{\Lambda}]\longrightarrow P_{\bullet}
admits the section
\frac{1}{[\widetilde{\Lambda}:\Lambda]}\sum_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}/\Lambda}\lambda p\otimes\lambda^{-1}\mapsfrom p
.
∎

Putting everything together, we obtain the main result of this section.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
R,\widetilde{\Lambda},\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-},\Lambda,\Lambda^{-},\mathcal{M}
and
\chi
be as in
Lemma

3.5
. Assume that the submonoid
\Lambda^{-}
of
\Lambda
is of the form described in
Lemma

3.3
(i) and that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
the conditions of
Lemma

3.2
for
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
. More specifically,
\mathcal{M}
admits a decomposition

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{+}\oplus\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
as
R
-modules satisfying

(a)

\lambda\mathcal{M}_{\mu}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\lambda\mu}
for
\lambda,\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
,

(b)

for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
there exists
\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
such that
\lambda m\in\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
.

Then,
\text{Tor}_{q}^{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}(R(\chi),\mathcal{M})=0
for
q>0
.

Proof.
By
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

3.5
it’s enough to show that
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda]}_{q}(R(\chi),R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\mathcal{M})
vanishes for
q>0
.
Recall from the proof of
Lemma

3.2
that there is a natural isomorphism

R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}R[\Lambda]\otimes_{R[\Lambda^{-}]}\mathcal{M}.

Write
\mathcal{N}
for the source of the isomorphism. Then,
\mathcal{N}
admits a direct sum decomposition
\mathcal{N}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}}\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}
, where
\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}
is the
R
-submodule generated by
\mu\otimes m
with
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mu^{-1}\lambda}
and
\mu\in\Lambda
such that
\mu^{-1}\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}
.
For every coset
L
in
\widetilde{\Lambda}/\Lambda
, the
R[\Lambda]
-submodule
\mathcal{N}_{L}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in L}\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}
satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

3.2
, and hence
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{N}_{L})=0
for
q>0
by
Lemma

3.3
. In particular,
\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda]}_{q}(R(\chi),\mathcal{N})=0
for
q>0
.
∎

4.
The locally analytic representations

In this section we will introduce the locally analytic representations whose homology we will be studying in the rest of the article and study some of their properties.
For example, we will prove
Theorem

1.6
.

4.1.
Locally
\Sigma
-analytic and compact induction

Our ultimate goal is to study the
S
-arithmetic (co)homology of
p
-adic representations of products of
p
-adic and
\ell
-adic groups for primes
\ell\neq p
.
We want the corresponding representations of
p
-adic groups to be locally analytic, and the corresponding representations of
\ell
-adic groups to be smooth.
For this reason, we introduce the following framework of locally
\Sigma
-analytic representations, which will allow us to work simultaneously with both types of representations.

Recall that a Hausdorff topological space
X
is called strictly paracompact if every open cover has a refinement by pairwise disjoint open subsets.
A locally
\Sigma
-analytic manifold is a pair
X=(X_{\Sigma},X^{\Sigma})
where
X_{\Sigma}
is a (strictly) paracompact
p
-adic locally analytic manifold and
X^{\Sigma}
is a Hausdorff topological space that can be written as a disjoint union of compact, totally disconnected subspaces.
We will usually write
X=X_{\Sigma}\times X^{\Sigma}
.
A morphism of locally
\Sigma
-analytic manifolds
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
consists of a pair of a morphism
f_{\Sigma}\colon X_{\Sigma}\longrightarrow Y_{\Sigma}
of locally analytic manifolds and a continuous map
f^{\Sigma}\colon X^{\Sigma}\longrightarrow Y^{\Sigma}
.
We may similarly define a locally
\Sigma
-analytic group as a pair of a
p
-adic locally analytic group and a locally profinite group with the property that their quotients by some (or equivalently, any) compact open subgroups are countable (these spaces are then automatically strictly paracompact).
We will start by defining locally
\Sigma
-analytic functions on a locally
\Sigma
-analytic manifold
X
, which stated roughly are those that are locally analytic in
X_{\Sigma}
and smooth in
X^{\Sigma}
.
If
Y
is an open subset of
X_{\Sigma}
equipped with an isomorphism
\mathbb{Y}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}Y
, where
\mathbb{Y}
is a rigid
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic closed ball, and
U
is an open compact subset of
X
, we will say that a function from
Y\times U
to a (
p
-adic) Banach space
W
is
\mathbb{Y}\times U
-analytic if it factors through projection to
Y
and is given by a rigid
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic function of
\mathbb{Y}
, i.e. by an element of
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{Y})\cotimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}W
.
If
V
is a Hausdorff locally convex
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-vector space, we define the space of locally
\Sigma
-analytic functions
X\longrightarrow V
as the direct limit

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}(X,V)=\varinjlim_{\{(\mathbb{X}_{i},U_{j},W_{i})_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\}}\prod_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{X}_{i})\cotimes_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}W_{i}

running over all collections of triples
(\mathbb{X}_{i},U_{j},W_{i})_{(i,j)\in I\times J}
, where
(\mathbb{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
is an analytic partition of
X_{\Sigma}
in the sense of
[
Eme17
, Section 2.1]
,
(U_{j})_{j\in J}
is a partition of
X^{\Sigma}
into pairwise disjoint open compact subspaces, and
W_{i}\xhookrightarrow{}V
is a BH-subspace of
V
for each
i\in I
(i.e. an injective continuous map from a Banach space into
V
).
We equip
\mathcal{C}^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}(X,V)
with the locally convex direct limit topology.
We identify an element
f
of
\mathcal{C}^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}(X,V)
with a continuous function
X\longrightarrow V
: if
f
belongs to an element of the product in
Equation

4.1
, say
f=(\sum_{m}f_{i,j,m}\otimes w_{i,j,m})_{i,j}
, and
x\in X
belongs to
\mathbb{X}_{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times U_{j}
, then
f(x)=\sum_{m}f_{i,j,m}(x)w_{i,j,m}
.
If
X^{\Sigma}
is compact, then there is a topological isomorphism
\mathcal{C}^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}(X,V)\simeq\mathcal{C}^{\text{sm}}(X^{\Sigma},\mathcal{C}^{\text{la}}(X_{\Sigma},V))
.

Now, let
G=G_{\Sigma}\times G^{\Sigma}
be a locally
\Sigma
-analytic group.
If
V
is endowed with a continuous action of
G
, we will say that this action is locally
\Sigma
-analytic if for all
v\in V
the orbit map
g\longmapsto gv
is locally
\Sigma
-analytic.
We do not impose any conditions on the topology of
V
such as being of compact type.

If
H\subseteq G
is a closed (locally
\Sigma
-analytic) subgroup and
V
is a locally
\Sigma
-analytic representation of
H
on a locally convex
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-vector space, we write
\Ind_{H}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
for the locally analytic induction

\Ind_{H}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}=\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(G,V):f(gh)=h^{-1}f(g)\text{ for all }g\in G,h\in H\},

and equip it with the topology it inherits as a closed subspace of
\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(G,V)
. The group
G
acts on
\Ind_{H}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
by left translations:
(gf)(g^{\prime})=f(g^{-1}g^{\prime})
for
g,g^{\prime}\in G
and

f\in\Ind_{H}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
.
Given a subset
\Omega\subseteq G
, we will write
\Ind_{H}^{G}(V)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}(\Omega)
for the subspace of
\Ind_{H}^{G}(V)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}
consisting of functions
f
whose support
\supp(f)
is contained in
\Omega
.

We are also interested in the compact induction

\displaystyle\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)=\{

\displaystyle f\colon G\longrightarrow V:f(gh)=h^{-1}f(g)\text{ for all }g\in G,h\in H,

\displaystyle\supp(f)/H\text{ is compact }\}

with its action of
G
by left translations.
Again, for
\Omega\subseteq G
, we will write
\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)(\Omega)
for the subspace of
\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)
consisting of functions whose support is contained in
\Omega
.
We equip
\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)
with its locally convex direct limit topology obtained from the isomorphism

\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)\simeq\varinjlim_{\Omega}\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)(\Omega),

where
\Omega
runs through finite unions of left cosets of
H
in
G
.
If
V
has its finest locally convex topology (for example, if
V
is finite-dimensional and Hausdorff), then the same is true of
\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)
.
Given
g\in G
and
v\in V
, we write
[g,v]
for the element of
\cInd_{H}^{G}(V)
satisfying
\supp([g,v])=gH
and
[g,v](g)=v
.

The following lemma guarantees that our definition of
\Sigma
-analytic induction agrees with the example in the introduction.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that
G_{\Sigma}=G_{\Sigma,1}\times G_{\Sigma,2}
and
G^{\Sigma}=G^{\Sigma}_{1}\times G^{\Sigma}_{2}
. For
i=1,2
, let
G_{i}=G_{\Sigma,i}\times G^{\Sigma}_{i}
and let
H_{\Sigma,i},H^{\Sigma}_{i}
be closed subgroups of
G_{\Sigma,i}
,
H^{\Sigma}_{i}
,
H_{i}=H_{\Sigma,i}\times H^{\Sigma}_{i}
, and
V_{i}
be a locally
\Sigma
-analytic representation of
H_{i}
on a Hausdorff locally convex
E
-vector space of compact type, where
E
is some finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

(i)

If
H_{i}
is open in
G_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then there is a topological isomorphism

\cInd_{H_{1}}^{G_{1}}(V_{1})\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\cInd_{H_{2}}^{G_{2}}(V_{2})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\cInd_{H_{1}\times H_{2}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}V_{2}).

(ii)

If
G_{i}/H_{i}
is compact for
i=1,2
, then there is a topological isomorphism

\Ind_{H_{1}}^{G_{1}}(V_{1})^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\Ind_{H_{2}}^{G_{2}}(V_{2})^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\Ind_{H_{1}\times H_{2}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}V_{2})^{\Sigma-\text{la}}.

Proof.
Let us start with the case of compact induction.
The morphism we seek sends
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
to the map
(g_{1},g_{2})\longmapsto f_{1}(g_{1})\otimes f_{2}(g_{2})
.
We must check that this is indeed a topological isomorphism.
As
H_{i}
is open, a (right)
H
-equivariant function
G_{i}\longrightarrow V_{i}
has compact support if and only if its support is a finite union of left cosets of
H_{i}
.
Write each
V_{i}
as a countable direct limit
V_{i}=\varinjlim_{n}V_{i,n}
of Banach spaces with compact injective transition maps and choose a countable increasing system
(\Omega_{i,n})_{n}
of finite sets of left
H_{i}
-cosets whose union is
G_{i}
.
Then,

\cInd_{H_{i}}^{G_{i}}(V_{i})\simeq\varinjlim_{\Omega}\cInd_{H_{i}}^{G_{i}}(V_{i})(\Omega)\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\cInd_{H_{i}}^{G_{i}}(V_{i,n})(\Omega_{i,n})

and the transition maps are injective and compact, and we have a similar expression for the compact induction to
G_{1}\times G_{2}
.
Thus,
[
Eme17
, Proposition 1.1.32 (i)]
implies that

\displaystyle\cInd_{H_{1}}^{G_{1}}(V_{1})\cotimes_{E}\cInd_{H_{2}}^{G_{2}}(V_{2})

\displaystyle\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\cInd_{H_{1}}^{G_{1}}(V_{1,n})(\Omega_{1,n})\cotimes_{E}\cInd_{H_{2}}^{G_{2}}(V_{2,n})(\Omega_{2,n})

\displaystyle\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\cInd_{H_{1}\times H_{2}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V_{1,n}\cotimes_{E}V_{2,n})(\Omega_{1,n}\times\Omega_{2,n})

\displaystyle\simeq\cInd_{H_{1}\times H_{2}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V_{1}\cotimes_{E}V_{2}),

as required (for the second isomorphism note that, for example,
\cInd_{H_{1}}^{G_{1}}(V_{1,n})(\Omega_{1,n})
is isomorphic to a direct sum of
|\Omega_{1,n}/H_{1}|
copies of
V_{1,n}
).
This proves (i).

Next, let us deal with (ii). The map above is defined by the same formula as in the case of compact induction.
Choose for
i=1,2
a section
s_{i}\colon G_{i}/H_{i}\longrightarrow G_{i}
of locally
\Sigma
-analytic manifolds, i.e. sections
G_{\Sigma,i}/H_{\Sigma,i}\longrightarrow G_{\Sigma,i}
of locally analytic manifolds and
G^{\Sigma}_{i}/H^{\Sigma}_{i}\longrightarrow G^{\Sigma}_{i}
of topological spaces.
These sections induce a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{H_{1}}^{G_{1}}(V_{1})^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\Ind_{H_{2}}^{G_{2}}(V_{2})^{\Sigma-\text{la}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{H_{1}\times H_{2}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V_{1}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}V_{2})^{\Sigma-\text{la}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(G_{1}/H_{1},V_{1})\cotimes_{E}\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(G_{2}/H_{2},V_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}((G_{1}\times G_{2})/(H_{1}\times H_{2}),V_{1}\cotimes_{E}V_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

and it’s enough to show that the bottom map is a topological vector space isomorphism.
Choose for each
i
a cofinal system
((\mathbb{X}_{i,n,k},U_{i,n,j})_{k,j})_{n}
of pairs of analytic partitions of
G_{\Sigma,i}/H_{\Sigma,i}
, where
(\mathbb{X}_{i,n+1,k})_{k}
is a relatively compact refinement of
(\mathbb{X}_{i,n,k})_{k}
, and disjoint partitions of
G^{\Sigma}_{i}/H^{\Sigma}_{i}
into open compact subspaces.
Then,
[
Eme17
, Proposition 1.1.32 (i)]
shows that

\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(G_{i}/H_{i},V_{i})\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\prod_{k,j}\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{X}_{i,n,k})\cotimes_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}V_{i,n},

and the transition maps are injective and compact.
In particular, applying
[
Eme17
, Proposition 1.1.32 (i)]
again shows that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(G_{1}/H_{1},V_{1})\cotimes_{E}\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(G_{2}/H_{2},V_{2})\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\prod_{k,j}\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{X}_{1,n,k}\times\mathbb{X}_{2,n,k})\cotimes_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(V_{1,n}\cotimes_{E}V_{2,n}).

As the quotients
G_{i}/H_{i}
are compact,
((\mathbb{X}_{1,n,k}\times\mathbb{X}_{2,n,k})_{k})_{n}
is a cofinal system of analytic partitions of
(G_{\Sigma,1}\times G_{\Sigma,2})/(H_{\Sigma,1}\times H_{\Sigma,2})
and
((U_{1,n,j}\times U_{2,n,j})_{j})_{n}
is a cofinal system of partitions of
(G^{\Sigma}_{1}\times G^{\Sigma}_{2})/(H^{\Sigma}_{1}\times H^{\Sigma}_{2})
into open compact subspaces. In particular, the right-hand side of
Equation

4.3
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}((G_{1}\times G_{2})/(H_{1}\times H_{2}),V_{1}\cotimes_{E}V_{2})
, which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume that
H_{0}
is an open subgroup of
G
.
If the action of
H_{0}
on a vector space
V
extends to an action of a submonoid
H^{+}
of
G
containing
H_{0}
, then there is a natural right action of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(H^{-},H_{0})
on
\cInd_{H_{0}}^{G}(V)
, where
H^{-}=(H^{+})^{-1}
, such that for
\mu\in H^{-}
,

[g,v][H_{0}\mu H_{0}]=\sum_{x\mu\in H_{0}\mu H_{0}/H_{0}}[gx\mu,(x\mu)^{-1}v].

This action commutes with the action of
G
.

Proof.
We may view the action of
H^{+}
on
V
as a right action of
H^{-}
.
Consider the space
E[G]\otimes_{E}V
; it is a
(G,H^{-})
-bimodule with
H^{-}
acting via
h\cdot(g\otimes v)=gh\otimes h^{-1}v
and
\cInd_{H_{0}}^{G}(V)
can be identified with its right
H_{0}
-coinvariants (an element
g\otimes v
of the latter corresponding to
[g,v]
).
The action of the Hecke algebra is that described in
Lemma

2.29
, and
G
-equivariance is immediate from this formula.
∎

4.2.
Parabolic and parahoric induction

We now retake the notation from
Section

2
, more specifically
Section

2.9
.
Thus, we let
\ell_{1},...,\ell_{n}
be prime numbers, for each
i

L_{i}
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell_{i}}
and
\mathbf{G}_{i}
is a reductive group over
L_{i}
, etc.
Fix a subset
\Sigma
of
\{1,...,n\}
such that for all
i\in\Sigma
,
\ell_{i}=p
, and set
G_{\Sigma}=\prod_{i\in\Sigma}G_{i}
and
G^{\Sigma}=\prod_{i\not\in\Sigma}G_{i}
, and similarly for the subgroups of
G
defined in
Section

2.9
.
Hence,
G=G_{\Sigma}\times G^{\Sigma}
is a locally
\Sigma
-analytic manifold and similarly for its subgroups
P
,
M
, etc.
Analogously, we also have similar decompositions
\Phi=\Phi_{\Sigma}\coprod\Phi^{\Sigma},\Delta=\Delta_{\Sigma}\coprod\Delta^{\Sigma}
, etc.
Recall from
Section

2.9
that we will usually reference results from
Section

2
that were stated (and proven) for each of the
G_{i}
but also hold for
G
when we want to use them in the latter case.
Let
\mathpzc{M}
be a subgroup of
\mathfrak{M}
containing
AM_{0}
. Many of our results on
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{M}^{-}
from
Section

2
(for example,
Lemma

2.15
and
Lemma

2.17
) also hold for
\mathpzc{M}
and
\mathpzc{M}^{-}
for obvious reasons, and we will also use them without comment in this situation.

Let
R
be a Noetherian Banach
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-algebra and
\sigma
be a locally
\Sigma
-analytic representation of
\mathpzc{M}
on some finitely generated
R
-module, which we also write as
\sigma
, equipped with its unique Banach
R
-module topology. We will write
V=\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{M}(\sigma)
for the compact induction of
\sigma
to
M
.
As explained in the previous section, we equip
V
with the direct limit locally convex topology induced from the expression

(4.5)

V\simeq\varinjlim_{\Omega}\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{M}(\sigma)(\Omega),

where
\Omega
runs over finite unions of cosets of
\mathpzc{M}
in
M
, and the terms in the limit, which are isomorphic as
R
-modules to direct sums of finitely many copies of
\sigma
, are given their unique Banach
R
-module topologies.
We extend the action of
M
on
V
to a locally
\Sigma
-analytic action of
P
by letting
N
act trivially, and we extend
\sigma
to
J\cap P
similarly.
Restriction of functions induces a topological isomorphism

\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathcal{C}^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}(\overline{N}_{0},\sigma)

as in the proof of
Lemma

4.2
.
Likewise, restriction of functions induces topological isomorphisms

\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP)\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathcal{C}^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}(\overline{N}_{0},V),

the first of which is
J
-equivariant.

Let
s>0
. Recall from
Proposition

2.21
that
J_{s}
admits a rooted Iwahori decomposition,
J_{s}=\overline{N}_{s}M_{s}N_{s}
.
For each
i\in\Sigma
, in
Section

2.4
we constructed an affinoid
L_{i}
-space
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{i,s}
whose set of
L_{i}
-points is
\overline{N}_{i,s}
.
In particular, we may view
\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}
as the set of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-points of the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-affinoid
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}:=\prod_{i\in\Sigma}\Res_{L_{i}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{i,s}
.
Given
s
and
s^{\prime}
, we will say that a locally analytic function
\overline{N}_{0}\longrightarrow\sigma
is
(s,s^{\prime})
-
\Sigma
-analytic if its restriction to each left
\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
-coset is
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
-analytic.
Let us also say that a function
f
in
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\text{la}}
is
(s,s^{\prime})
-
\Sigma
-analytic if its restriction to
\overline{N}_{0}
is.
We will write
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
for the subspace of
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
consisting of such functions.
Restriction to
\overline{N}_{0}
induces an isomorphism from
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
to the space of
(s,s^{\prime})
-
\Sigma
-analytic functions
\overline{N}_{0}\longrightarrow\sigma
, which we use to give the former the structure of a Banach
R
-module.
If
\sigma
is free over
R
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
is orthonormalisable as it is isomorphic to the completed base change to
R
of a Banach
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-module.
When
s=s^{\prime}
, we will speak of
s
-
\Sigma
-analytic functions instead and write
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}=\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s)-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
.
Note that
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
is isomorphic to the colimit
\varinjlim_{s}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
since any rigid analytic closed ball in
\overline{\mathbf{N}}
containing the identity is contained in some
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}
and vice versa (but the
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}
will not necessarily be closed balls if the
\mathbf{G}_{i}
aren’t split).

Lemma 4.6
.

If
s,s^{\prime}
are large enough, then
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
is stable under the action of
J
.

Proof.
As
\sigma
is finitely generated, we may choose
s
and
s^{\prime}
large enough so that the orbits of
M_{i,s}
in
\sigma
are analytic for
i\in\Sigma
(i.e. they come from rigid-analytic morphisms of
\mathbf{M}_{i,s}
, as defined in
Section

2.4
) and the orbits of

M_{i,s^{\prime}}

are constant for
i\not\in\Sigma
.
Let
y\in J
and
f\in\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
.
We will show that
(y^{-1}f)|_{\overline{N}_{0}}
is
(s,s^{\prime})
-
\Sigma
-analytic.
By writing
f
as a sum of functions with support in different cosets of
\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
, it’s enough to show that
(y^{-1}f)|_{\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}}
is
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
-analytic.
Write
y=\overline{n}_{0}m_{0}n_{0}
according to the Iwahori decomposition of
J
(
Lemma

2.9
), set
p_{0}=m_{0}n_{0}
, and let
\overline{n}\colon J_{\Sigma,s}\times J_{s^{\prime}}^{\Sigma}\longrightarrow\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
and
m\colon J_{\Sigma,s}\times J_{s^{\prime}}^{\Sigma}\longrightarrow M_{\Sigma,s}\times M_{s^{\prime}}^{\Sigma}
be the projections given by the Iwahori decomposition of
J_{s}
(
Proposition

2.21
).
Then, for all
x\in\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
one has

(4.7)

\displaystyle(y^{-1}f)(x)=m(p_{0}xp_{0}^{-1})^{-1}m_{0}^{-1}f(\overline{n}_{0}\cdot\overline{n}(p_{0}xp_{0}^{-1})).

By
Lemma

2.25
(i) and (ii), the map
x\longmapsto\overline{n}(p_{0}xp_{0}^{-1})\colon\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\longrightarrow\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}
and the map

x\longmapsto m(p_{0}xp_{0}^{-1})\colon

\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\longrightarrow M_{\Sigma,s}
are induced from rigid-analytic morphisms. We have similar continuous maps for
\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
. By our choice of
s
and
s^{\prime}
, this implies that
Equation

4.7
is
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
-analytic as a function of
x\in\overline{N}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
.
∎

We will write
s_{\sigma}
for the smallest
s
such that
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
is stable under the action of
J
, and when writing
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
we will always implicitly assume
s\geq s_{\sigma}
.
Similarly, when we consider
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
we will always assume that
s
and
s^{\prime}
are large enough that this is stable under the action of
J
.

Lemma 4.8
.

If
\widetilde{s}\geq s
and
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\geq s^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{s}^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
. If
\widetilde{s}>s
, then this inclusion is a compact map of Banach
R
-modules.

Proof.
The first statement is immediate. Note that there is an isomorphism of
R
-Banach modules

(4.9)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{C}^{\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}-\text{an}}(\overline{N}_{\Sigma,0},\mathbb{Q}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{|(\overline{N}_{0}^{\Sigma})/\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}|}\right)\cotimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\sigma\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}},

where
\mathcal{C}^{\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}-\text{an}}(\overline{N}_{\Sigma,0},\mathbb{Q}_{p})
denotes the space of functions
\overline{N}_{\Sigma,0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}
whose restriction to each
\overline{N}_{s}
coset is
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s}
-analytic.
The second statement follows from this isomorphism by
Lemma

2.8
and
[
Buz07
, Corollary 2.9]
.
∎

The
M_{0}
-equivariant injection
\sigma\xhookrightarrow{}V
given by
v\longmapsto[1,v]
induces
J
-equivariant maps
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\longrightarrow\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
. Composing with
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP)
we obtain a
J
-equivariant injection

\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\longrightarrow\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP)
.

Lemma 4.10
.

The image of this map is stable under the action of
\mathpzc{M}^{+}=(\mathpzc{M}^{-})^{-1}
.

Proof.
A function
f\in\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP)
is in the image of this map if and only if there exists some
F\in\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
such that
f(y)=[1,F(y)]
for all
y\in J
, or equivalently, for all
y\in\overline{N}_{0}
. Consider some such
f
and
F
, and let
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{+}
and
y\in\overline{N}_{0}
.
Then,
(\mu f)(y)=\mu f(\mu^{-1}y\mu)=[1,\mu F(\mu^{-1}y\mu)]
, so
\mu f
lies in the image.
∎

It follows from
Lemma

2.15
that
J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
is a submonoid of
G
, and
Lemma

4.10
implies that we may extend the action of
J
on
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
to an action of
J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
making the injection
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\longrightarrow\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP)
a
J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
-equivariant map.
Explicitly, if
y
is an element of
J
with Iwahori decomposition
y=\overline{n}mn
and
F\in\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
, then the action of
\mathpzc{M}^{+}
is given by
(\mu F)(y)=m^{-1}\mu F(\mu^{-1}\overline{n}\mu)
.
If
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{+}
, then
\mu
preserves
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
by
Lemma

2.25
(iii), and for a fixed norm on
\sigma
, the norm of
\mu
as an operator on
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
(with its norm induced by
Equation

4.9
and that of
\sigma
) is bounded above by the norm of
\mu
on
\sigma
.
If, moreover,
\mu^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}\mu\subseteq\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,\widetilde{s}}\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s^{\prime}}
for some
\widetilde{s}<s
(for example, if
\mu=a^{-1}
with
a\in A^{--}_{\Sigma}
), then the action of
\mu
on
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(\widetilde{s},s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
factors through the inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(s,s^{\prime})-\text{an}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(\widetilde{s},s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
, and therefore induces an
R
-compact endomorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{(\widetilde{s},s^{\prime})-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
by
Lemma

4.8
.
Moreover, applying
Lemma

4.4
and the results of
Section

2.6
, we obtain the following.

Lemma 4.11
.

The action of
\mathpzc{M}^{+}
on
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
induces a (left) action of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(J\mathpzc{M}^{-}J,J)_{R}
on
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}})
commuting with the action of
G
, where the characteristic functions
U_{\mu}=[J\mu J]
for
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
act as

U_{\mu}([g,F])=\sum_{x\mu\in J\mu J/J}[gx\mu,(x\mu)^{-1}F].

Moreover, for each
s
and
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
, the operator
U_{\mu}
preserves the subspace

\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
.

4.3.
A quasi-isomorphism

Write
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}=\mathcal{H}(J\mathpzc{M}^{-}J,J)_{R}
.
The
J
-equivariant embedding
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}\longrightarrow\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
induces a
G
-equivariant
R
-linear map

\varphi\colon\cInd_{J}^{G}(\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}})\longrightarrow\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}.

Explicitly, if
F\in\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
, then
\varphi([1,F])(y)=[1,F(y)]
for
y\in J
.
The source is a module for
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
, and we may regard
\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
as a
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
-module by letting
U_{\mu}=[J\mu J]
act trivially. Write
R(1)
for the
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
-module whose underlying
R
-module is
R
and where
U_{\mu}
acts trivially. The goal of this section is to show that
\varphi
induces an isomorphism in the derived category
D(\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}[G])
of
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}[G]
-modules

R(1)\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}.

To be more precise, we should be writing
R[G](1)\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}[G]}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}})
for the source instead.
However, the complexes are isomorphic, so we will use the shorter notation.
As explained in the introduction, we follow the strategy of
[
KS12
, §2]
.

Proposition 4.12
.

The map
\varphi
is surjective.

Proof.
We can write
G
as a union of finitely many translates of
JP
,
G=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}JP
. It is easy to see that we may write any element of
\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
as sum of a functions in
\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
whose support intersects only one of the sets
g_{i}JP
.
In particular,

\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}\cdot\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP),

so it’s enough to show that the image of
\varphi
contains
\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP)
. Let
f
be an element of
\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(JP)
.
Since
f
is locally
\Sigma
-analytic,
f|_{\overline{N}_{0}}
factors through some BH-space
U\xhookrightarrow{}V
.
It follows from the expression
Equation

4.5
of
V
as an LB-space and
[
Sch02
, Corollary 8.9]
that any BH-space
U\xhookrightarrow{}V
factors through
\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{M}(\sigma)(\Omega)
for some finite union
\Omega
of left cosets of
\mathpzc{M}
in
M
.
Hence,
f(\overline{N}_{0})
is contained in
\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{M}(\sigma)(\Omega)
, where
\Omega=\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}m_{i}\mathpzc{M}
for some integer
s\geq 0
and
m_{1},...,m_{s}\in M
. For any
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}_{0}
we have
f(\overline{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}[m_{i},F_{i}(\overline{n})]
, where we have set
F_{i}(\overline{n}):=f(\overline{n})(m_{i})
. Thus, we may assume that
s=1
and
f|_{\overline{N}_{0}}=[m,F]
for some
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma-\text{la}}(\overline{N}_{0},\sigma)
.
Choose
a\in A^{-}
such that
am^{-1}\overline{N}_{0}ma^{-1}\subseteq\overline{N}_{0}
, so that by changing
F
by
\overline{n}\longmapsto aF(\overline{n})
we may assume that
m^{-1}\overline{N}_{0}m\subseteq\overline{N}_{0}
.
As
(m^{-1}f)(m^{-1}\overline{n}m)=[1,F(\overline{n})]
, we see by replacing
f
with
m^{-1}f
and
F
with
y\longmapsto F(mym^{-1})
for
y\in m^{-1}\overline{N}_{0}m
that it suffices to consider the case when
f|_{\overline{N}_{0}}=[1,F]
. But in this case,
f=\varphi([1,F])
.
∎

Proposition 4.13
.

The kernel of
\varphi
is
\sum_{\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}}\Im(U_{\mu}-1)
. In fact, for
s\geq s_{\sigma}
, the kernel of the restriction of
\varphi
to
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
is
\sum_{\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}}(U_{\mu}-1)(\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}))
.

Proof.
The first statement follows from the second, so this is the one we will prove.
Let
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
. By
Lemma

2.17
, we can write
JP=J\mu JP=\coprod_{x}x\mu JP

where
x
runs over coset representatives in
\overline{N}_{0}/\mu\overline{N}_{0}\mu^{-1}
,
and
\varphi(U_{\mu}[1,F])=\sum_{x}\varphi([x\mu,(x\mu)^{-1}F])
, where
\varphi([x\mu,(x\mu)^{-1}F])
has support in
x\mu JP
.
Hence, for any
y\in JP
, there exists a unique coset representative
x
as above such that
y\in x\mu JP
, and we can write
y=x\mu\overline{n}p
with
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}_{0}
and
p\in P
. Then,

\displaystyle\varphi(U_{\mu}[1,F])(y)

\displaystyle=[x\mu,(x\mu)^{-1}F](x\mu\overline{n}p)

\displaystyle=p^{-1}[1,((x\mu)^{-1}F)(\overline{n})]

\displaystyle=p^{-1}[1,\mu^{-1}F(x\mu\overline{n}\mu^{-1})]

\displaystyle=p^{-1}\mu^{-1}[1,F(x\mu\overline{n}\mu^{-1})]

\displaystyle=\varphi([1,F])(y)

since we can write
y=(x\mu\overline{n}\mu^{-1})\cdot(\mu p)
as a product of an element of
\overline{N}_{0}
with an element of
P
.
Thus,
(U_{\mu}-1)[1,F]\in\ker\varphi
, and since the elements of the form
[1,F]
generate
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
, we see that the subspace
\mathcal{K}:=\sum_{\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}}(U_{\mu}-1)(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
is contained the kernel of
\varphi
.

We now show that the kernel of
\varphi
is contained in
\mathcal{K}
. For
g\in G
,
F\in\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
and
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
, we have

(4.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}[g,F]=~&(1-U_{\mu})[g,F]+U_{\mu}[g,F]\\
&\in\mathcal{K}+\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-\text{an}})(gJ\mu J).\end{split}

Recall that
\overline{N}_{0}\subseteq K\cap N
, so in particular for for
t\in\mathfrak{Z}^{-}
, we have

(A)

t\overline{N}_{0}t^{-1}\subseteq K\cap\overline{N}
.

Choose for each
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
a constant
c_{\alpha}\leq 0
that is small enough such that, defining
\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}
as in
Proposition

2.27
(iii), for all
t\in\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}
we have

(B)

t^{-1}(K\cap N)t\subseteq N_{0}
.

Then,
Equation

4.14
and
Proposition

2.27
(iii) show for all
g\in G,F\in\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
,

[g,F]\in\mathcal{K}+\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})(K\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}J).

Now, let
f\in\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})(K\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}J)
be an element of the kernel of
\varphi
. We may write
f=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}[k_{i,j}t_{i},F_{i,j}]
, where
k_{i,j}\in K
and the
t_{i}\in\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}
belong to different double cosets in
K\backslash G/J
. By
Proposition

2.27
(i),
Remark

2.16
and
Equation

4.14
we can further assume that if
i\neq i^{\prime}
then
Kt_{i}\mu J\neq Kt_{i^{\prime}}J
for any
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
. In fact, by
Lemma

2.18
, we may assume that the same is true for
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}
. We may also assume without loss of generality that if
k_{i,j}t_{i}J=k_{i,j^{\prime}}t_{i}J
, then
j=j^{\prime}
. We will show that any such
f
must be zero.

Let
k\in K
be any element, and write
\mathcal{I}_{k}
for the set of pairs of indices
(i,j)
such that
kP\subseteq k_{i,j}t_{i}JP
. For any
(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
, we may write
(k_{i,j}t_{i})^{-1}k=\overline{n}_{i,j}m_{i,j}n_{i,j}
for some

\overline{n}_{i,j}\in\overline{N}_{0},m_{i,j}\in M,n_{i,j}\in N
. In particular,
k_{i,j}^{-1}k=t_{i}\overline{n}_{i,j}t_{i}^{-1}\cdot t_{i}m_{i,j}\cdot n_{i,j}
, so by
Item

(A)
,
t_{i}m_{i,j}n_{i,j}\in K\cap P
. By
Lemma

2.11
and the Levi decomposition of
P
, this means that
t_{i}m_{i,j}\in K\cap M
and
n_{i,j}\in K\cap N
. In particular,
m_{i,j}(K\cap N)m_{i,j}^{-1}=t_{i}^{-1}(K\cap N)t_{i}\subseteq N_{0}
by
Item

(B)
.

The support of
\varphi([k_{i,j}t_{i},F_{i,j}])
is
k_{i,j}t_{i}JP
, and

\varphi([k_{i,j}t_{i},F_{i,j}])(k)=\varphi([1,F_{i,j}])((k_{i,j}t_{i})^{-1}k)=\varphi([1,F_{i,j}])(\overline{n}_{i,j}m_{i,j}n_{i,j})=[m_{i,j}^{-1},F_{i,j}(\overline{n}_{i,j})].

In particular,

(4.15)

\displaystyle 0=\varphi(f)(k)=\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[m_{i,j}^{-1},F_{i,j}(\overline{n}_{i,j})].

We claim that the supports of the summands in the right-hand side are pairwise disjoint.
Indeed, assume that
m_{i,j}^{-1}\mathpzc{M}\cap m_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}^{-1}\mathpzc{M}\neq\emptyset
. Then,
\mu:=m_{i,j}m_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{-1}\in\mathpzc{M}
and

Kt_{i}\mu J=Km_{i,j}^{-1}\mu J=Km_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}^{-1}J=Kt_{i^{\prime}}J,

which by assumption implies
i=i^{\prime}
. Moreover,
Corollary

2.28
implies
\mu\in M_{0}
. Now, as
k_{i,j}^{-1}=t_{i}\overline{n}_{i,j}m_{i,j}n_{i,j}k^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle t_{i}^{-1}k_{i,j}^{-1}k_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}t_{i}

\displaystyle=\overline{n}_{i,j}\cdot\mu\cdot m_{i,j^{\prime}}n_{i,j}n_{i,j^{\prime}}^{-1}m_{i,j^{\prime}}^{-1}\cdot\overline{n}_{i,j^{\prime}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\in\overline{N}_{0}\cdot M_{0}\cdot m_{i,j^{\prime}}(K\cap N)m_{i,j^{\prime}}^{-1}\cdot\overline{N}_{0}

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{N}_{0}\cdot M_{0}\cdot N_{0}\cdot\overline{N}_{0}\subseteq J,

or in other words,
k_{i,j^{\prime}}t_{i}J=k_{i,j}t_{i}J
. By assumption, this implies
j=j^{\prime}
, thus proving the claim.

In particular, we may deduce from
Equation

4.15
that
F_{i,j}(\overline{n}_{i,j})=0
for all
(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
. Now, let us fix any pair
(i,j)
. To show that
F_{i,j}=0
, it’s enough to show that
F_{i,j}(\overline{n})=0
for all
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}_{0}
. Fix such a
\overline{n}
, and take
k=k_{i,j}t_{i}\overline{n}t_{i}^{-1}
, which lies in
K
by
Item

(A)
. Then,
(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
,
\overline{n}_{i,j}=\overline{n},m_{i,j}=t_{i}^{-1}
and
n_{i,j}=1
. In particular,
F_{i,j}(\overline{n})=0
, as required.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

The restriction of
\varphi
to
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
is surjective.

Proof.
Let
s_{\sigma}\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
.
Choose
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
such that the operator
U_{\mu}
on
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
factors through

\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s^{\prime}-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
. There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s^{\prime}-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})}{\sum_{\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}}\Im(U_{\widetilde{\mu}}-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})}{\sum_{\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}}\Im(U_{\widetilde{\mu}}-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s^{\prime}-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})}{\sum_{\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}}\Im(U_{\widetilde{\mu}}-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})}{\sum_{\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}}\Im(U_{\widetilde{\mu}}-1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\mu}}
inclusion
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\mu}}
inclusion

where the vertical arrows are isomorphisms. Thus, the horizontal arrows are also isomorphisms. The result then follows by passing to the limit and
Proposition

4.12
.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\Sigma}
denote either
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
or
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
for some
s\geq s_{\sigma}
. Then,

\text{Tor}^{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}}_{q}(R(1),\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\Sigma}))=0
for all
q>0
.

Proof.
We will prove this by applying our results from
Section

3
, specifically
Proposition

3.6
. Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\mathpzc{M}/M_{0}
,
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}=\mathpzc{M}^{-}/M_{0}
and
\Lambda\subseteq A
inducing an isomorphism
\Lambda\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}A/A_{0}
and
\Lambda^{-}=\Lambda\cap A^{-}
.
Recall from
Remark

2.12
that
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is a free abelian group of finite rank and
\Lambda
can be seen as a finite index subgroup of it.

Lemma

2.18
implies that
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-})^{-1}
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}\Lambda
.
By
Corollary

2.33
(cf.
Remark

2.34
), there is an
R
-algebra isomorphism

R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
, which we use to regard
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma})
as an
R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]
-module.
The monoid
\Lambda^{-}
satisfies the condition of
Lemma

3.3
(i) by
Equation

2.14
.
Thus, to apply
Proposition

3.6
we need to check that
\mathcal{M}:=\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\Sigma})
admits a decomposition

\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\Sigma})=\mathcal{M}^{+}\oplus\bigoplus_{\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\mu}

such that

(a)

U_{\mu}\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{\mu}M_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mu\widetilde{\mu}M_{0}}
for
\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
,

(b)

for all
f\in\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\Sigma})
, there exists
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
such that
U_{\mu}f\in\bigoplus_{\widetilde{\mu}\in\Lambda^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\widetilde{\mu}}
.

Let
\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}
be as in the proof of
Proposition

4.13
. Choose a set
\Omega
of representatives in
\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim}
for each orbit of the (right) action of
\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathpzc{M}^{-}
on
\widetilde{W}^{K}\backslash\widetilde{W}/\widetilde{W}^{M_{0}}\simeq\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}/\widetilde{W}^{M_{0}}
(this is possible by
Proposition

2.27
(iii)). Set, for
\mu\in\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathpzc{M}^{-}
,

\mathcal{M}_{\mu M_{0}}:=\bigoplus_{t\in\Omega}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\Sigma})(Kt\mu J),~~~~~~~~~~\text{ and }~~~~~~~~~~\mathcal{M}^{+}:=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}\smallsetminus\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\mu}.

It then follows from
Proposition

2.27
(i) and
Corollary

2.28
that there is a decomposition
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{\Sigma})=\mathcal{M}^{+}\oplus\bigoplus_{\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}}\mathcal{M}_{\mu}
.
Condition
Item

(a)
above follows from the fact that for
k\in K,

t\in\mathfrak{Z}^{\sim},\mu,\widetilde{\mu}\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
,

\supp(U_{\mu}[kt\widetilde{\mu},F])\subseteq kt\widetilde{\mu}J\mu J\subseteq Kt\mu\widetilde{\mu}J,

where the second inclusion follows from
Remark

2.16
.

Let us move on to condition
Item

(b)
.
Let
\widetilde{t}\in\mathfrak{Z}
,
k\in K
.
We will show that there exists
a\in A^{-}
such that
\supp(U_{a}[k\widetilde{t},F])\subseteq\coprod_{\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-},t\in\Omega}Kt\mu J
for any
F
.
By
Proposition

2.27
(ii), there exist
\widetilde{t}_{1},...,\widetilde{t}_{r}\in\mathfrak{Z}
such that
\widetilde{t}Ja\subseteq\bigcup_{i}K\widetilde{t}_{i}aJ
for all
a\in A^{-}
.
Choose
\mu_{i}\in\mathfrak{N}\cap\mathpzc{M}
and
t_{i}\in\Omega
such that the action of
\mu_{i}
on
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\backslash\mathfrak{Z}
sends
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}t_{i}
to
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}\widetilde{t}_{i}
.
Writing
\mu_{i}=n_{i}t^{\prime}_{i}
with
n_{i}\in\mathfrak{N}\cap K
and
t^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathfrak{Z}
, this means that, multiplying
\widetilde{t}_{i}
by an element of
\mathfrak{Z}_{0}
on the left if necessary, we have
\widetilde{t}_{i}=n_{i}^{-1}t_{i}\mu_{i}
.
If we take
a\in A^{-}
with
v_{p}(\alpha(a))
small enough for all
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\smallsetminus\Phi_{M}
so that
\mu_{i}a\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
, then
U_{a}[k\widetilde{t},F]
has support in

K\widetilde{t}JaJ\subseteq\bigcup_{i}K\widetilde{t}_{i}aJ=\bigcup_{i}Kn_{i}^{-1}t_{i}\mu_{i}aJ=\bigcup_{i}Kt_{i}(\mu_{i}a)J\subseteq\coprod_{\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-},t\in\Omega}Kt\mu J,

as required.
∎

We are almost ready to state and prove the main theorem of the section.
Before that, we need to introduce a bit more notation.
Let
G_{0}
be an abstract group and let
U
be a representation of
G_{0}\times G
on a finite flat
R
-module.
We set
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}:=U\otimes_{R}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
and view it as a
G_{0}\times G
-submodule (with
G
acting diagonally) of
U\otimes_{R}\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{\Sigma-\text{la}}
.
There is a
G_{0}\times G
-equivariant isomorphism

(4.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U\otimes_{R}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})&\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})\\
u\otimes[g,F]&\longmapsto[g,g^{-1}u\otimes F].\end{split}

If we regard
U
as an
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
-module by letting the operators
U_{\mu}
act as 1 and let
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
act diagonally on the left-hand side, then
Equation

4.18
is also an
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
-module isomorphism.
The corresponding statements for
U\otimes_{R}\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}
also hold.
The map
\varphi
induces
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}[G_{0}\times G]
-linear maps

\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})\longrightarrow U\otimes_{R}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}})\longrightarrow U\otimes_{R}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}

Theorem 4.19
.

The map
\varphi
induces isomorphisms in the derived category
D(\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}[G_{0}\times G])
of
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}[G_{0}\times G]
-modules

\displaystyle R(1)\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}R(1)\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}}(U\otimes_{R}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}))

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}U\otimes_{R}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}.

Proof.
When
U
is the trivial representation, the theorem follows by putting together
Proposition

4.12
,
Proposition

4.13
,
Corollary

4.16
and
Proposition

4.17
.
For general
U
, the result follows from the fact that the functor
U\otimes_{R}{-}
on
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
-modules is exact, preserves projectives (as
U
is a projective
R
-module), and commutes with the functor
R(1)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}}{-}
.
∎

Next, assume that
\sigma=\sigma_{\Sigma}\otimes_{R}\sigma^{\Sigma}
where
\sigma_{\Sigma}
is a locally analytic representation of
G_{\Sigma}
and
\sigma^{\Sigma}
is a smooth representation of
G^{\Sigma}
.
We may choose
s\geq s_{\sigma}
such that
M_{s}^{\Sigma}
acts trivially on
\sigma^{\Sigma}
.
We can extend the action of
M_{0}^{\Sigma}
on
\sigma^{\Sigma}
to an action of
J_{0,s}^{\Sigma}
via the isomorphism described above
Corollary

2.23
, and even to an action of the monoid
J_{0,s}^{\Sigma}\mathpzc{M}^{\Sigma,+}J_{0,s}^{\Sigma}
.
In particular, we may consider the
G_{0}\times J_{0,s}\mathpzc{M}^{+}J_{0,s}
-module
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Sigma},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}\otimes_{R}\sigma^{\Sigma}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

The map

\displaystyle\xi\colon\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Sigma},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}\otimes_{R}\sigma^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}

\displaystyle F\otimes v

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\overline{n}mn\longmapsto F(\overline{n}mn)\otimes m^{-1}v)

for
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}_{0},m\in M_{0},n\in N_{0}
,
is an isomorphism of
G_{0}\times J_{0,s}\mathpzc{M}^{+}J_{0,s}
-modules.

Proof.
The map
\xi
is well-defined, since the image of an element
f\otimes v
as above is an element of
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}
whose restriction to
\overline{N}_{0}
is
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s}
-analytic.
Note that, under restriction to
\overline{N}_{0}
,
\xi
corresponds to the natural isomorphism of
R
-modules between
U\otimes_{R}\mathcal{C}^{\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s}-\text{an}}(\overline{N}_{0},\sigma)
and
U\otimes_{R}\mathcal{C}^{\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{\Sigma,s}\times\overline{N}^{\Sigma}_{s}-\text{an}}(\overline{N}_{0},\sigma_{\Sigma})\otimes_{R}\sigma^{\Sigma}
, and is thus an
R
-module isomorphism.
It is also clearly
G_{0}
-equivariant.

Let us check that
\xi
is
J_{0,s}
-equivariant. Let
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}_{0}
and
y\in J_{0,s}
, and let
m_{0}
be the projection of
y
to
M_{0}
under the Iwahori decomposition of
J_{0,s}
. It follows from the comment above
Corollary

2.23
that we can write
y^{-1}\overline{n}=\overline{n}_{1}m_{0}^{-1}m_{1}n_{1}
for some
\overline{n}_{1}\in\overline{N}_{0}
,
m_{1}\in M_{s},n_{1}\in N_{s}
, and we have

\displaystyle(y\xi(F\otimes v))(\overline{n})

\displaystyle=\xi(F\otimes v)(y^{-1}\overline{n})=F(y^{-1}\overline{n})\otimes m_{0}v,

\displaystyle\xi(y(F\otimes v))(\overline{n})

\displaystyle=\xi(yF\otimes yv)(\overline{n})=(yF)(\overline{n})\otimes yv=F(y^{-1}\overline{n})\otimes m_{0}v.

Similarly, if
\mu\in\mathfrak{M}^{+}
, then

\displaystyle(\mu\xi(F\otimes v))(\overline{n})

\displaystyle=\mu\cdot\xi(F\otimes v)(\mu^{-1}\overline{n}\mu)=\mu F(\mu^{-1}\overline{n}\mu)\otimes\mu v,

\displaystyle\xi(\mu(F\otimes v))(\overline{n})

\displaystyle=\xi(\mu F\otimes\mu v)(\overline{n})=(\mu F)(\overline{n})\otimes\mu v=\mu F(\mu^{-1}\overline{n}\mu)\otimes\mu v.\qed

4.4.
Untwisting

In this section we assume that
\mathpzc{M}\longrightarrow\mathpzc{M}/M_{0}
admits a multiplicative section
\mathpzc{M}/M_{0}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\widetilde{\Lambda}\subseteq\mathpzc{M}
such that
\widetilde{\Lambda}
acts on
\sigma
via a character
\chi\colon\widetilde{\Lambda}\longrightarrow R^{\times}
.
Note that this assumption is always satisfied if
\mathpzc{M}=AM_{0}
(with
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\Lambda
) and
\sigma
admits a central character.
Then, it follows from the Bruhat–Tits decomposition
[
BT72
, 7.4.15]
that the map
\widetilde{\Lambda}\longrightarrow J\backslash J\mathpzc{M}J/J
is a bijection. Let
\eta
be its inverse.
We will write
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}
to denote either
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
or
U\otimes_{R}\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(\sigma)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}
.

In this situation, we may modify the action of
J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
on
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}
by letting
\mu\in J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
act via
(\mu*F)(y):=\chi(\eta(\mu))^{-1}(\mu F)(y)
. In other words,

(\mu*F)(\overline{n})=F(\mu^{-1}\overline{n}\eta(\mu)).

for
\overline{n}\in\overline{N}_{0}
.
For clarity, we will write
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U,*}^{\Sigma}
when we want to consider
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}
as a
J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
-module with this action.
We will call this action of
J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
the
*
-action
or
untwisted action
to differentiate it from the action induced by
Lemma

4.10
.
It may appear more natural to call this action “twisted” as opposed to “untwisted”, so let us explain our choice of terminology. Assume for simplicity that
\mathpzc{M}=AM_{0}\simeq\Lambda\times M_{0}
, and that
\sigma=\chi\otimes_{R}\sigma_{0}
for some character
\chi
of
\Lambda
and representation
\sigma_{0}
of
M_{0}
on a finitely generated
R
-module. The
J
-module structure on
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}
depends only on
\sigma_{0}
and not
\chi
, but the action of
\mathpzc{M}^{+}
depends on both. However, this is not true for the
*
-action, which like the action of
J
depends only on
\sigma_{0}
. However, it of course depends on the choice of
\Lambda
.

As before, this action of
J\mathpzc{M}^{+}J
induces an action of
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
on
\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U,*}^{\Sigma})
. Let
R(\chi)
denote the
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
-module whose underlying
R
-module is
R
and such that
U_{\mu}
acts as
\chi(\mu)
for
\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
. The functor
\mathcal{M}\longmapsto R(\chi)\otimes_{R}\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}
-modules is exact, and

\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U,*}^{\Sigma})\simeq R(\chi)\otimes_{R}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}).

In this setting, we may restate
Theorem

4.19
as saying that there is an isomorphism in
D(\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}[G_{0}\times G])

R(\chi)\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{R}}\cInd_{J}^{G}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma,U,*}^{\Sigma})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}R(\chi)\otimes_{R}\left(U\otimes_{R}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V)^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}\right).

5.
S
-arithmetic (co)homology

5.1.
Generalities on
S
-arithmetic (co)homology

Let
F
be a number field and
\mathbf{G}
be a reductive group over
F
, and let
S
a finite set of non-archimedean primes of
F
. For any place
v
of
F
, we let
F_{v}
denote the completion of
F
at
v
, and we write
F_{\infty}=F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
. We will write
\mathbb{A}_{F}
for the ring of adeles of
F
,
\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\infty}
for the ring of finite adeles and
\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty}
for the ring of finite adeles away from
S
.

Let
K^{S}
be a compact open subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty})
. There exists a finite set of (non-archimedean) primes
S_{\text{ram}}(K^{S})
containing
S
such that we may write
K^{S}
as a product

K^{S}=\prod_{v\not\in S_{\text{ram}}(K^{S})}K_{v}\times K_{\text{ram}}

where
K_{\text{ram}}\subseteq\prod_{v\in S_{\text{ram}}(K^{S})\smallsetminus S}\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
is a compact open subgroup and
K_{v}
is a hyperspecial subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
for each
v\not\in S_{\text{ram}}(K^{S})
. We will assume that
K^{S}
is neat, i.e. that
\mathbf{G}(F)\cap gK^{S}g^{-1}
is neat for all
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty})
, where the intersection takes place in
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty})
. Write
\mathcal{K}^{S}=G_{\infty}\times G_{S}\times K^{S}
.

5.1.1.
Geometric definition

Fix a maximal compact subgroup
K_{\infty}
of
G_{\infty}:=\mathbf{G}(F_{\infty})
and let
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}:=\mathbf{G}(F_{\infty})/K_{\infty}
. For each
v\in S
, write
\mathscr{B}_{v}
for the Bruhat–Tits building of
\mathbf{G}_{F_{v}}
over
F_{v}
, and
G_{S}=\prod_{v\in S}\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
.
Consider the topological spaces
\mathscr{B}_{S}=\prod_{v\in S}\mathscr{B}_{v}
and
X_{S}=\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S}\times\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
, where we give the
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
factor the discrete topology.
We may equip this space with an action of the group
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
as follows:
\mathbf{G}(F)
acts diagonally on all factors of the product via the embeddings
\mathbf{G}(F)\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
, and
\mathcal{K}^{S}
acts trivially on
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S}
and by right translations on
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.

Let
M
be a (left)
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]
-module. Then
M
defines a local system on
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash X_{S}/\mathcal{K}^{S}
consisting of locally constant sections of the map

\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash(X_{S}\times M)/\mathcal{K}^{S}\longrightarrow\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash X_{S}/\mathcal{K}^{S}.

Then, the
S
-arithmetic cohomology
H^{*}(K^{S},M)
of
M
(of level
K^{S}
) is defined as the cohomology of this local system.
More explicitly, this is also the cohomology of the complex

C_{\text{ad}}^{\bullet}(K^{S},M):=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}(C_{\bullet}^{\text{sing}}(X_{S}),M),

where here
C^{\text{sing}}_{\bullet}(Y)
denotes the complex of singular chains on a topological space
Y
.
Similarly, we may define the
S
-arithmetic homology
H_{*}(K^{S},M)
as the homology of the complex

C^{\text{ad}}_{\bullet}(K^{S},M):=C_{\bullet}^{\text{sing}}(X_{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}M.

Let
\mathbf{A}
denote the maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split torus in the center of
\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{A}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbf{A}(\mathbb{R})
the connected component of the identity (for the Euclidean topology).
It acts on
X_{S}
by acting on the
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}
factor.
Then, the spaces
X_{S}
and
X_{S}/\mathbf{A}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}\times\mathbf{A}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
are
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-equivariantly homotopically equivalent, where the latter space is given the diagonal action where the second factor is given the trivial action.
In particular, when taking (co)homology as above, one can work with either of these spaces.

Usually,
M
will be a
\mathcal{K}^{S}
-module, which we will consider as a
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-module by letting the first factor act trivially.
If
M
is a
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
-module, then we may view it as a
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-module in two ways: by letting the first factor act by restriction of the
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
-action and the second factor act trivially, or by letting the first factor act trivially and the second act by restriction.
Let us denote
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
for the corresponding
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-modules respectively.
Then, there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}(C_{\bullet}^{\text{sing}}(X_{S}),M_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}(C_{\bullet}^{\text{sing}}(X_{S}),M_{2})

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\longmapsto((\sigma,g)\longmapsto g^{-1}\phi((\sigma,g))

for
\sigma\in C^{\text{sing}}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S}),g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.
In fact, the two corresponding local systems are isomorphic.
In particular, we see that
H^{*}(K^{S},M)
depends only on the action of
\mathbf{G}(F)
, up to isomorphism.
This justifies, for example, switching the action on the algebraic part of the coefficients from
\infty
to
p
in the introduction since the two actions of
\mathbf{G}(F)
through
\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R})
agree.
Similarly, the same phenomenon happens for homology.

5.1.2.
Definition using
S
-arithmetic groups

The double coset set
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/\mathcal{K}^{S}
is finite.
Let
g_{S,1},...,g_{S,n}
be a system of representatives for these cosets, and consider the
S
-arithmetic groups
\Gamma_{S,i}:=\mathbf{G}(F)\cap g_{S,i}K^{S}g_{S,i}^{-1}
, where the intersection is taken inside
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.
Let
M
be a
\mathcal{K}^{S}
-module, regarded as a
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-module by letting
\mathbf{G}(F)
act trivially as above.
For each
i
, write
M(g_{S,i})
for the
\Gamma_{S,i}
-module whose underlying abelian group is
M
and where the action of
\Gamma_{S,i}
is induced by the map
\Gamma_{S,i}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{S}:\gamma\longmapsto g_{S,i}^{-1}\gamma g_{S,i}
.
Then there are isomorphisms

\displaystyle H_{*}(K^{S},M)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i}H_{*}(\Gamma_{S,i},M(g_{S,i})),

\displaystyle H^{*}(K^{S},M)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i}H^{*}(\Gamma_{S,i},M(g_{S,i})),

where the right hand sides denote group homology and cohomology respectively.
Let us be more precise.
First, the
S
-arithmetic groups
\Gamma_{S,i}
act freely on the contractible space
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S}
, so we may compute the group (co)homology of
\Gamma_{S,i}
as the (co)homology of
\Gamma_{S,i}\backslash(\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S})
with local coefficients given by
M(g_{S,i})
.
Moreover, there are isomorphisms
\coprod_{i=1}^{n}\Gamma_{S,i}\backslash(\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash X_{S}/\mathcal{K}^{S}
given by
\Gamma_{S,i}(b_{\infty},b_{S})\longmapsto\mathbf{G}(F)(b_{\infty},b_{S},g_{S,i})\mathcal{K}^{S}
.
The corresponding local system on the target is the one described in
Section

5.1.1
.
Similarly, if
M
is instead a
\mathbf{G}(F)
-module, then we have isomorphisms
H_{*}(K^{S},M)\simeq\bigoplus_{i}H_{*}(\Gamma_{S,i},M)
and
H^{*}(K^{S},M)\simeq\bigoplus_{i}H^{*}(\Gamma_{S,i},M)
.

5.1.3.
Algebraic definition

Let
M
be a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]
-module again.
Then, there are isomorphisms

\displaystyle H_{*}(K^{S},M)

\displaystyle\simeq\text{Tor}^{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})],M),

\displaystyle H^{*}(K^{S},M)

\displaystyle\simeq\text{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}^{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})],M).

Here,
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})]
is given an action of
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
by letting the first factor act on the left and the second on the right.
Comparing with
Section

5.1.1
, it’s enough to show that
C_{\bullet}^{\text{sing}}(X_{S})
is a free
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]
-module resolution of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})]
.
It is clear that the homology is
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})]
since
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}
and
\mathscr{B}_{S}
are contractible, so it’s enough to show that
G(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
acts freely on
X_{S}
.
If
(\gamma,k)
stabilises a point
(b_{\infty},b_{S},g)\in\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S}\times\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
, then
\gamma=gk^{-1}g^{-1}\in\mathbf{G}(F)\cap g\mathcal{K}^{S}g^{-1}
and
\gamma
lies in the compact subgroup of
G_{\infty}\times G_{S}
stabilising
(b_{\infty},b_{S})
.
Thus,
\gamma
lies in the discrete subgroup
\mathbf{G}(F)
and in a compact subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
, so it must have finite order.
But the assumption that
K^{S}
is neat then implies that
\gamma
(and hence also
k
) is the identity element, which proves our claim.

In fact, it follows from the work of Borel–Serre
[
BS76
]
that
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})]
admits a resolution by
finite free

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]
-modules.
Indeed, let us write
\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{\infty}
for the Borel–Serre compactification of
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}
and
\overline{X}_{S}=\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S}\times\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.
By
[
BS76
, Remarque after Proposition 6.10]
1
1

1

To be precise, Borel–Serre work with
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}/\mathbf{A}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
instead of
\mathscr{B}_{\infty}
and similarly for the compactifications. However, the homotopy equivalence mentioned in
Section

5.1.1
allows us to translate their results to our setting.
and the isomorphism
\coprod_{i=1}^{n}\Gamma_{S,i}\backslash(\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{B}_{S})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\overline{X}_{S}/\mathcal{K}^{S}
analogous to the one in
Section

5.1.2
, there exists on
\overline{X}_{S}
a
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-equivariant triangulation with trivial stabilisers and finitely many orbits.
This triangulation gives rise to a finite free complex
Q_{S,\bullet}
of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]
-modules and a
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence
Q_{S,\bullet}\longrightarrow C^{\text{sing}}_{\bullet}(\overline{X}_{S})
, and the latter is
\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}
-equivariantly homotopically equivalent to
C^{\text{sing}}_{\bullet}(X_{S})
.
We make a choice of such triangulation and homotopy equivalences and write
C_{\bullet}(K^{S},M)=Q_{S,\bullet}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}M
and
C^{\bullet}(K^{S},M)=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]}(Q_{S,\bullet},M)
, which are homotopically equivalent to
C^{\text{ad}}_{\bullet}(K^{S},M)
and
C_{\text{ad}}^{\bullet}(K^{S},M)
respectively.
Following
[
Han17
]
, we call these
Borel–Serre complexes
.
Each of the
\mathbb{Z}
-modules
C_{q}(K^{S},M)
and
C^{q}(K^{S},M)
is isomorphic to a finite direct sums of copies of
M
, so if
M
is a topological module over some ring
R
, we may equip
C_{q}(K^{S},M)
and
C^{q}(K^{S},M)
with their direct sum topologies, and
H_{q}(K^{S},M)
and
H^{q}(K^{S},M)
with their subquotient topologies.
Note, however, that these complexes, and hence any topology we may put on them and the induced one on (co)homology, depend on the choices of triangulation.

Let us highlight that all the isomorphisms between the different definitions of
S
-arithmetic (co)homology we have provided extend to isomorphisms in the derived category of abelian groups (or of
R
-modules for a commutative ring
R
, if the action of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]
on
M
extends to an action of
R[\mathbf{G}(F)\times\mathcal{K}^{S}]
).
We will usually simply write
C_{\bullet}(K^{S},M)
and
C^{\bullet}(K^{S},M)
to denote the corresponding objects in the derived category (having fixed homotopy equivalences as above).

5.1.4.
Hecke operators

Next, we discuss the action of Hecke operators on
S
-arithmetic (co)homology.
We will do this following the (
S
-arithmetic analogue of the) description in
[
Han17
]
using the explicit complexes
C^{\text{ad}}_{\bullet}(K^{S},M)
.
However, we will shortly see in
Section

5.2
that they admit a more natural description.
Let
\Delta\subseteq\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty})
a submonoid containing
K^{S}
.
Let
R
be a commutative ring and
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty}),K^{S})_{R}
be the convolution algebra of
K^{S}
-biinvariant locally constant functions
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{S,\infty})\longrightarrow R
as in
Section

2.6
.
Assume now that
M
is an
R[G_{\infty}\times G_{S}\times\Delta^{-1}]
