\bigboxtimes_{v\in S}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{v,i}
, and as observed above we have already proven that
S
-arithmetic (co)homology is finite-dimensional in this case. If
\mathfrak{S}\geq 1
, then there exist some
v_{0}
and
i_{0}
such that
s_{v_{0},i_{0}}\geq 2
. In light of
Lemma

5.28
, there is an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\bigboxtimes_{v\in S}\prod_{i=1}^{r_{v}}Z_{s_{v,i}}(\rho_{v,i})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\left(\bigboxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in S\\
v\neq v_{0}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{r_{v}}Z_{s_{v,i}}(\rho_{v,i})\right)\otimes\left(\left(\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}Z_{s_{v_{0},i}}(\rho_{v_{0},i})\right)\times(Z_{s_{v_{0},i_{0}}-1}(\rho_{v_{0},i_{0}})\times\rho(s))\times\left(\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{r_{v_{0}}}Z_{s_{v_{0},i}}(\rho_{v_{0},i})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\left(\bigboxtimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in S\\
v\neq v_{0}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{r_{v}}Z_{s_{v,i}}(\rho_{v,i})\right)\otimes\left(\left(\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}Z_{s_{v_{0},i}}(\rho_{v_{0},i})\right)\times(\rho(s)\times Z_{s_{v_{0},i_{0}}-1}(\rho_{v_{0},i_{0}}))\times\left(\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{r_{v_{0}}}Z_{s_{v_{0},i}}(\rho_{v_{0},i})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\bigboxtimes_{v\in S}\prod_{i=1}^{r_{v}}Z_{s_{v,i}}(\rho_{v,i})\longrightarrow 0.

Applying
U\otimes_{E}\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,{-})
we get yet another exact sequence, which to avoid running over the margins we will write simply as

0\longrightarrow\Pi_{4}\longrightarrow\Pi_{3}\longrightarrow\Pi_{2}\longrightarrow\Pi_{1}\longrightarrow 0,

where
\Pi_{1}=\Pi_{4}
is the representation whose
S
-arithmetic homology we want to prove is finite-dimensional.
Moreover,
\Pi_{2}
and
\Pi_{3}
are obtained in the same way as
\Pi_{1}
, except the corresponding sums
\mathfrak{S}
are smaller than the one for
\Pi_{1}
.
By our induction hypothesis, both representations have finite-dimensional
S
-arithmetic homology.
This short exact sequence induces a converging spectral sequence

E_{i,j}^{1}=H_{i}(K^{S},\Pi_{j})\implies 0.

Assume for the sake of contradiction that
H^{*}(K^{S},\Pi_{1})
is infinite-dimensional. Let
i_{0}
be the greatest index
i
for which
H_{i}(K^{S},\Pi_{1})
is infinite-dimensional. Thus,
H_{i}(K^{S},\Pi_{j})
is finite-dimensional for all
j
and all
i>i_{0}
. In particular, the entry
E_{i_{0},4}
will be infinite-dimensional in all pages starting in the
E^{1}
page, which contradicts the fact that the spectral sequence converges to 0. The same argument works for cohomology.
∎

Corollary 5.30
.

Every locally algebraic representation of
G
of finite length has finite-dimensional
S
-arithmetic homology at level
K^{S}
, and similarly for the cohomology of its (abstract) dual.

Remark 5.31
.

This result is false without the finite length assumption, even for smooth admissible representations.
Indeed, this fails already for
F=\mathbb{Q}
,
S=\{p\}
and
\mathbf{G}=\text{GL}_{1}
.
Choose a sequence
\psi_{n}
of unitary Hecke characters of conductors
p^{r_{n}}
with
r_{n}\rightarrow\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, and let
\psi_{n,p}
be their components at
p
.
Let
K^{p}=\prod_{\ell\neq p}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}^{\times}
.
Then,
\Pi:=\bigoplus_{n}\psi_{n,p}
is an admissible smooth representation of
\text{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(of infinite length) and

H^{0}(K^{p},\Pi^{\prime})=\prod_{n}H^{0}(K^{p},\psi_{n,p}^{-1})

is infinite-dimensional, since each term in the product is non-zero by
Theorem

5.20
.

5.10.
p
-arithmetic and completed cohomology

We will now prove a result relating the degree zero
p
-arithmetic (i.e.
S
-arithmetic when
S=\mathcal{S}_{p}
) cohomology groups of duals of locally analytic representations to completed cohomology when
\mathbf{G}
is
F
-anisotropic.
We hope to investigate the general relation between
p
-arithmetic and completed cohomology in the future.
We assume that
S=\mathcal{S}_{p}
and write
K^{p}
and
\mathbb{T}^{p}(K^{p})
instead of
K^{S}
and
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
respectively.
Recall the definition of (rational) completed cohomology,

\widetilde{H}^{i}(K^{p}):=\left(\varprojlim_{s}\varinjlim_{K_{p}}H^{i}(K^{p}K_{p},\mathcal{O}_{E}/p^{s}\mathcal{O}_{E})\right)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}E,

where
K_{p}
runs through the compact open subgroups of
G=\mathbf{G}(F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Proposition 5.32
.

Assume that
\mathbf{G}
is
F
-anisotropic.
Let
V
be a locally analytic representation of
G
on a locally convex
E
-vector space of compact type.
There is a
\mathbb{T}^{p}(K^{p})
-equivariant isomorphism
H^{0}(K^{p},V^{\prime})\simeq\text{Hom}_{G,\text{cts}}(V,\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})^{\text{la}})
.

Proof.
Let
W
be a locally convex space with a continuous action of
G
.
If
W
is a locally convex space with a continuous action of
G
, we let
\widetilde{S}(K^{p},W)
be the space of continuous functions
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\infty})/K^{p}\longrightarrow W
.
This space is equipped with a natural action of
G
(via

(gf)(x):=gf(xg)
) and of
\mathbb{T}^{p}(K^{p})
.
Unwinding the definition of
H^{0}(K^{p},W)
shows that it is isomorphic as a
\mathbb{T}^{p}(K^{p})
-module to
\widetilde{S}(K^{p},W)^{G}
.
On the other hand, if
W=V^{\prime}
, the example at the end of
[
Sch02
, §17]
and
[
Sch02
, Corollary 18.8]
show that there are topological isomorphisms
\widetilde{S}(K^{p},V^{\prime})\simeq\widetilde{S}(K^{p},E)\cotimes_{E}V^{\prime}\simeq\text{Hom}_{\text{cts}}(V,\widetilde{S}(K^{p},E))
(note that the space
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\infty})/K^{p}
is compact by our anisotropy assumption).
Moreover,
\widetilde{S}(K^{p},E)
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})
.
Thus, the result follows from the fact that, as
V
is locally analytic, any
G
-equivariant continuous homomorphism
V\longrightarrow\widetilde{S}(K^{p},E)
factors through
\widetilde{S}(K^{p},E)^{\text{la}}
.
∎

6.
Eigenvarieties

In this section we will use the results from
Section

5
to construct eigenvarieties.
We will start by proving the spectral theorem that powers the construction, and then give a first definition of eigenvarieties.
Then, we will give a different construction using overconvergent homology and determine the relation between both constructions, and use it to give some of their properties.
Finally, in the case of definite unitary groups, we will discuss the relation between these eigenvarieties and the Bernstein eigenvarieties studied by Breuil–Ding
[
BD21
]
.

6.1.
A spectral theorem

If
R
is a commutative Banach
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra and
M_{\bullet}
is a complex of Banach
R
-modules, by a chain operator we mean an
R
-linear, graded map
\phi\colon M_{\bullet}\longrightarrow M_{\bullet}
that commutes with the differentials and is continuous in each degree.
We say such a map is simultaneously
R
-compact if there exist chain operators
\{\psi_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
on
M_{\bullet}
that are of finite
R
-rank at each degree and converge to
\phi
in each degree as operators on a Banach space.
We will need the following spectral theorem generalising
[
Eme06a
, Proposition 2.2.6]
for orthonormalisable modules.
Note that, unlike in
[
Eme06a
, Proposition 2.2.6]
, we have to work with algebraic (as opposed to completed) tensor products.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
R
be a commutative Noetherian Banach
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra,
(M_{\bullet},d)
a complex of orthonormalisable
R
-Banach modules,
\phi_{1},...,\phi_{n},\phi
continuous chain operators on
M_{\bullet}
. Assume that
\phi
is simultaneously
R
-compact on
M_{\bullet}
,
\phi_{1},...,\phi_{n}
commute in
H_{*}(M_{\bullet})
, and that the composition
\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n}
agrees with
\phi
on
H_{*}(M_{\bullet})
. We let
R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]
act on
H_{*}(M_{\bullet})
by letting
T_{i}
act as
\phi_{i}
. Let
R^{\prime}
be a flat commutative Noetherian Banach
R
-algebra and
t_{1},...,t_{n}\in(R^{\prime})^{\times}
. Then, for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}_{q}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}{(T_{1}-t_{1},...,T_{n}-t_{n})},H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\text{Tor}^{R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}_{q}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}{(T_{1}-t_{1},...,T_{n}-t_{n})},H_{s}(M_{\bullet})\right).

is a finitely generated
R^{\prime}
-module.

Proof.
We will prove the theorem by induction on
n
, starting with the case
n=1
. We may replace
\phi_{1}
by
\phi
, and we drop the subindices for notational convenience and assume for simplicity that
s=0
. Write
N
for the kernel of the map
M_{0}\longrightarrow M_{-1}
and
N^{\prime}
for the kernel of
M_{0}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}\longrightarrow M_{-1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}
.
We will write
\phi
instead of
\phi\cotimes 1\colon M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}\longrightarrow M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}
.
In this case, the Tor groups can be non-zero only in degrees 0 and 1, and

\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[T]}_{0}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)},H_{0}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)\simeq\frac{N^{\prime}}{(\phi-t)N^{\prime}+d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})},

and
\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[T]}_{1}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)},H_{0}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)
is isomorphic to the subspace
(N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}))^{\phi=t}
of
N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})
consisting of classes of elements
n
such that
\phi(n)=tn+d(m)
for some
m\in M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}
.
On the other hand,

\displaystyle\text{Tor}^{R[T]}_{0}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)},H_{0}(M_{\bullet})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[T]}_{0}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)},H_{0}(M_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{(\phi-t)(N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})+d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})},

and
\text{Tor}^{R[T]}_{1}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)},H_{0}(M_{\bullet})\right)
is isomorphic to
(N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}))^{\phi=t}
.

Choose
a\in R^{\times}
such that
a\phi,at
and
at^{-1}
are power-bounded on
M_{0}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}
and on
M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}
. Since
\phi
is compact, we may find a chain operator
\psi
on
M_{\bullet}
of finite
R
-rank in each degree such that
a^{-1}(\phi-\psi)
is topologically nilpotent. Since
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are orthonormalisable, there is a commutative diagram whose rows compose to
\psi

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}

for some
r
. Indeed, we can apply
[
Eme06a
, Lemma 2.2.1]
to show the existence of each row with
\Im(\psi^{\prime\prime})=\Im(\psi)
, and choosing preimages for a set of generators for the map
\psi^{\prime}
in the bottom row gives the diagonal map after possibly increasing
r
. Moreover, if we set
P:=\ker d_{0}
, we have
\psi^{\prime\prime}(P)\subseteq N
and hence a commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}

where the diagonal map exists by the same argument as before (after possibly increasing
r
).
Moreover, there is also a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R^{\prime})^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R^{\prime})^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R^{\prime})^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}

Consider the chain operator

\displaystyle\theta\colon M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{i\geq 0}t^{-i-1}(\phi-\psi)^{i}(m).

Note that
t^{-i-1}(\phi-\psi)^{i}=t^{-1}(at^{-1})^{i}(a^{-1}(\phi-\psi))^{i}
is a topologically nilpotent operator, hence the sum on the right converges and defines a continuous map. The map
\theta
commutes with
\phi-\psi
and we also have
\theta\circ(t-\phi+\psi)=(t-\phi+\psi)\circ\theta=\text{id}
.
From this, we see that, for all
m
in
M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle(\psi\circ\theta)(m)

\displaystyle=m+(\phi-t)\theta(m),

\displaystyle(\theta\circ\psi)(m)

\displaystyle=m+\theta(\phi-t)(m).

From the first equality, we see that the composition

\displaystyle N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\frac{P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N^{\prime})}

is just projection onto the quotient.
In particular, the map

\frac{P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N^{\prime})}

is surjective, so the target is finitely generated. Similarly, from the second equality we see that the composition

\displaystyle\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}

\displaystyle\xhookrightarrow{}N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\frac{P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}N^{\prime}/d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})

is simply the inclusion, which shows that the map
\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}/d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})
is injective, so the source is finitely generated.

Note that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\psi^{\prime\prime}}

In particular, the upper right vertical injection factors through
\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}
, so the bent vertical arrows are injective.

Moreover, the inclusion
\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}\xhookrightarrow{}\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}
factors through the
middle-top right
\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}
.
Let
y
be the image by
\theta\psi
in
N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
of some lift to
N^{\prime}
of an element of
\left(\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}
.
Thus,
y
is a lift in
N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
of the same element.
We can see from the top square of the diagram that
\theta\psi(y)=y+d(m)
for some
m\in M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
.
But also
\theta\psi(y)=y+\theta(\phi-t)(y)
, so

(\phi-t)(y)=\theta^{-1}d(m)=(t-\phi+\psi)d(m)\in d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}).

Thus,
y\in\left(\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}\right)^{\phi=t}
. Hence, the bent vertical maps are also surjective, so

\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[T]}_{1}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)},H_{0}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)\simeq\text{Tor}^{R[T]}_{1}\left(\frac{R[T]}{(T-t)},H_{0}(M_{\bullet})\right).

The argument for the zeroth Tor space is very similar. In this case, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{P\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}{d_{1}((R^{\prime})^{r})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{N^{\prime}}{d(M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N^{\prime})}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime\prime}}

The lower left vertical surjection factors through the bent vertical maps, so these must be surjective. Let
y\in N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
be a lift of an element in the kernel of the bent map, i.e.
y=d(m)+(\phi-t)(n^{\prime})
for some
m\in M_{1}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime},n^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}
. Using that

\psi\theta(\phi-t)=\psi\theta\psi-\psi=(\phi-t)\theta\psi,

we see that

\psi\theta y=\psi\theta d(m)+\psi\theta(\phi-t)n^{\prime}=d\psi\theta m+(\phi-t)\theta\psi n^{\prime}.

Since the image of
\psi
is contained in
M_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
, we see that the right hand side lies in
d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})
. Since
y-\psi\theta y\in d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})
by the top square of the diagram above, this shows that

y\in d(M_{1}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})+(\phi-t)(N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}),

so the bent maps are also injective. This completes the case
n=1
.

Assume now that
n>1
. The first step is to reduce to the case where
\phi_{1}
is compact.
Write
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
\Lambda^{-}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
.
Let
e_{1},...,e_{n}
denote the canonical basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and let
v=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{n}
.
Write
\Lambda^{\prime}
for the (free abelian) submonoid of
\Lambda^{-}
generated by
v,e_{2},...,e_{n}
.
We can identify
R[\Lambda^{-}]
with
R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]
by identifying
T_{k}
with
e_{k}
,
and then
v
is identified with
Z:=T_{1}\cdots T_{n}
.
Write
z:=t_{1}\cdots t_{n}
. Note that
Z
acts on
H_{*}(M\bullet)
as
\phi
, which is compact operator on
M_{\bullet}
.

Lemma

3.1
(ii) implies that

\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}_{q}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}{(T_{1}-t_{1},...,T_{n}-t_{n})},H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)

is isomorphic to

\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[Z,T_{2},...,T_{n}]}_{q}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[Z,T_{2},...,T_{n}]}{(Z-z,T_{2}-t_{2},...,T_{n}-t_{n})},H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right),

and similarly if we replace
H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})
by
H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})
.
Thus, we may assume without loss of generality that
\phi_{1}
is compact.
Write for brevity
R^{\prime}_{k}=R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{k}]
and
\mathfrak{m}_{k}
for the ideal
(T_{1}-t_{1},...,T_{k}-t_{k})
. By
[
Bou06
, X.9.5, Corollaire 2]
, if
\mathcal{M}
denotes either
H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})
or
H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})
, then there is an exact sequence

(6.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\longrightarrow R^{\prime}_{k+1}/(T_{k+1}-t_{k+1})\otimes_{R^{\prime}_{k+1}}\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}_{k}}_{q}(R^{\prime}_{k}/\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathcal{M})\\
&\longrightarrow\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}_{k+1}}_{q}(R^{\prime}_{k+1}/\mathfrak{m}_{k+1},\mathcal{M})\\
&\longrightarrow\text{Hom}_{R^{\prime}_{k+1}}(R^{\prime}_{k+1}/(T_{k+1}-t_{k+1}),\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}_{k}}_{q-1}(R^{\prime}_{k}/\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathcal{M}))\longrightarrow 0\end{split}

so it follows by induction on
k
that the middle module is finitely generated, and the 5-lemma implies the isomorphism in the statement of the theorem.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

In the setting of the statement of
Theorem

6.1
, if
t_{1},...,t_{n}
belong to the image of
R
in
R^{\prime}
, writing also
t_{1},...,t_{n}
for some of their preimages in
R
it follows from the theorem that

\displaystyle\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}_{q}\left(\frac{R^{\prime}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}{(T_{1}-t_{1},...,T_{n}-t_{n})},H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\text{Tor}^{R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}_{q}\left(\frac{R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}{(T_{1}-t_{1},...,T_{n}-t_{n})},H_{s}(M_{\bullet})\right)\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}.

Remark 6.4
.

Let us show how
Theorem

6.1
(i) implies
[
Eme06a
, Proposition 2.2.6]
for orthonormalisable modules. Let
M,K,A,\phi,x
and
t
be as in
loc. cit.
, and assume that
M
is orthonormalisable.
Applying
Theorem

6.1
to
M
(seen as a complex concentrated in degree zero) with
R=A
and
R^{\prime}=A\langle xt,xt^{-1}\rangle
, we see that
\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)}\otimes_{R[T]}M
is a finitely generated
R^{\prime}
-module.
In fact, the proof of
Theorem

6.1
realises this
topologically
as a quotient of a finite
R^{\prime}
-module, and hence its topology is the Banach
R^{\prime}
-module topology.
In particular,
\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)}\otimes_{R[T]}M
is isomorphic to the completed tensor product
\frac{R^{\prime}[T]}{(T-t)}\cotimes_{R[T]}M
. Finally, this is isomorphic to
K\langle xt,xt^{-1}\rangle\cotimes_{K[t]}M\simeq K\langle xt,xt^{-1}\rangle\cotimes_{K\langle xt\rangle}M
.

In this context, we will also need an analogue of
[
Eme06a
, Proposition 3.2.28]
.
The statement is a bit technical, but informally it will say that if we can write
R^{\prime}=R^{\prime\prime}\{\!\{t_{i_{1}},...,t_{i_{r}}\}\!\}
, then the (higher) part of the higher Tor groups above corresponding to the operators
\phi_{i_{1}},...,\phi_{i_{r}}
vanishes, and the corresponding part of the zeroth Tor groups is especially nice.
Before we state the precise result, it will be useful to consider the following lemma.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
R
be an affinoid algebra and let
m\geq 1
.
Given
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let us write

R^{(h)}=R\langle p^{h}y_{1},p^{h}y_{1}^{-1},...,p^{h}y_{m},p^{h}y_{m}^{-1}\rangle
and
R^{(\infty)}:=R\{\!\{y_{1},y_{1}^{-1},...,y_{n},y_{n}^{-1}\}\!\}=\varprojlim_{h}R^{(h)}
. Then,
R^{(h)}
and
R^{(\infty)}
are flat over
R[y_{1},...,y_{n}]
,
R^{(h^{\prime})}
is flat over
R^{(h)}
for
h^{\prime}\leq h
, and
R^{(\infty)}
is a Frechet–Stein algebra.

Proof.
That
R^{(h^{\prime})}
is flat over
R^{(h)}
follows from the fact that
\text{Sp}(R^{(h^{\prime})})
is an affinoid subdomain of
\text{Sp}(R^{(h)})
.
This implies that
R^{(\infty)}=\varprojlim_{h}R^{(h)}
is a Frechet–Stein algebra since the transition maps have dense images.
That
R^{(h)}
and
R^{(\infty)}
are flat over
R
follows from the argument in
[
Fu21
, Corollary 4.4]
applied to the subring
R^{\circ}\subseteq R
of power-bounded elements (instead of
\mathcal{O}_{K}\subseteq K
).
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
R,M_{\bullet},\phi_{1},...,\phi_{n}
be as in
Theorem

6.1
and
t_{1},...,t_{n}\in R^{\times}
, and assume that
R
is an affinoid algebra. Let
\psi_{1},...,\psi_{m},\psi
be chain operators on
M_{\bullet}
with
m\geq 1
,
\psi
simultaneously compact and which commute in homology such that
\psi
agrees in homology with
\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{m}
.
View
H_{*}(M_{\bullet})
as an
R[T_{1},...,T_{n},Y_{1},...,Y_{m},Z]
-module by letting
T_{i}
(resp.
Y_{i}
, resp.
Z
) act by
\phi_{i}
(resp.
\psi_{i}
, resp.
\psi
).
Define
R^{(h)}
and
R^{(\infty)}
as in
Lemma

6.5
.
We view these as
R[Y_{1},...,Y_{n},Z]
-modules by letting
Y_{i}
act as
y_{i}
and
Z
as
y_{1}\cdots y_{m}
.
Write
R^{(h)}(t_{*})
for the
R^{(h)}[T_{1},...,T_{n},Y_{1},...,Y_{m},Z]
-module whose underlying module is
R^{(h)}
, and where
T_{i}
acts by multiplication by
t_{i}
, and define an
R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]
-module
R(t_{*})
similarly.
Then, there is an isomorphism of
R^{(\infty)}[T_{1},...,T_{n},Y_{1},...,Y_{m},Z]
-modules

(6.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varprojlim_{h}\text{Tor}^{R^{(h)}[T_{1},...,T_{n},Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}_{i}(R^{(h)}(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{(h)}))\\
&\simeq\varprojlim_{h}R^{(h)}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}\text{Tor}^{R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}_{i}(R(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))\\
&\simeq\varprojlim_{h}R\langle p^{h}z,p^{h}z^{-1}\rangle\otimes_{R[Z]}\text{Tor}^{R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]}_{i}(R(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))\end{split}

(in particular, the last term admits a structure of
R^{(\infty)}[T_{1},...,T_{n},Y_{1},...,Y_{m},Z]
-module).
Moreover, these modules can be equipped with locally convex topologies that make them coadmissible modules over
R^{(\infty)}
and over
R\{\!\{z,z^{-1}\}\!\}
.

Proof.
To simplify notation, write
S=R[T_{1},...,T_{n}]
and
S^{(h)}=R^{(h)}[T_{1},...,T_{n}]
for any

h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Let
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Let us start by showing the first isomorphism.
In fact, we will show that the terms in both limits are isomorphic.
By
Theorem

6.1
, the left-hand side of
Equation

6.7
is isomorphic to the inverse limit of
\text{Tor}^{S[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}_{i}(R^{(h)}(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))
.

As
R^{(h)}(t_{*})\simeq R(t_{*})\otimes_{R}R^{(h)}
as a module over
S[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]\simeq S\otimes_{R}R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]
and all of the terms in the tensor products are flat over
R
, there is a spectral sequence

E^{2}_{i,j}=\text{Tor}^{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}_{i}\left(R^{(h)},\text{Tor}^{S}_{j}(R(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))\right)\implies\text{Tor}^{S[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}_{i+j}(R^{(h)}(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet})).

As
R^{(h)}
is flat over
R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]
, this spectral sequence collapses and we obtain isomorphisms

(6.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Tor}^{S[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}_{i}(R^{(h)}(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))&\simeq R^{(h)}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}\text{Tor}^{S}_{i}(R(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet})).\\
&\simeq\text{Tor}^{S}_{i}(R(t_{*}),R^{(h)}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})).\end{split}

This proves the first isomorphism in
Equation

6.7
.
The proof of
Theorem

6.1
realises the
R^{(h)}
-module
R^{(h)}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})
topologically as the quotient of a finitely generated one, so it is a finitely generated Banach
R^{(h)}
-module isomorphic to the completed tensor product
R^{(h)}\cotimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})
.
In particular, if
h^{\prime}<h
then

R^{(h^{\prime})}\cotimes_{R^{(h)}}\left(R^{(h)}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})\right)\simeq R^{(h^{\prime})}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet}).

Thus,
\text{Tor}^{S[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}_{i}(R^{(h)}(t_{*}),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))
is also finitely generated over
R^{(h)}
and

R^{(h^{\prime})}\cotimes_{R^{(h)}}\text{Tor}^{S}_{i}(R(t_{*}),R^{(h)}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))\simeq\text{Tor}^{S}_{i}(R(t_{*}),R^{(h^{\prime})}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})).

Here, we have given the Tor groups their unique Banach
R^{(h)}
and
R^{(h^{\prime})}
-module topologies – thus, the completed tensor product is the same as the non-completed tensor product.
Therefore, the limit over all
h
of the terms in
Equation

6.8
is a coadmissible
R^{(\infty)}
-module.

It remains to describe the
S^{(\infty)}
-action on the last term of
Equation

6.7
and to prove the second isomorphism and coadmissibility over
R\{\!\{z,z^{-1}\}\!\}
.
There is a natural homomorphism

(6.9)

\displaystyle R\langle p^{mh}z,p^{mh}z^{-1}\rangle\otimes_{R[Z]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})\longrightarrow R^{(h)}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})

sending
z
to
y_{1}\cdots y_{m}
.
The left-hand side is a finitely generated Banach
R\langle p^{mh}z,p^{mh}z^{-1}\rangle
-module by
Theorem

6.1
, which is also a module over
R\langle p^{mh}z,p^{mh}z^{-1}\rangle[Y_{1},Y_{1}^{-1},...,Y_{m},Y_{m}^{-1}]
:
Y_{i}
acts via
1\otimes\psi_{i}
and
Y_{i}^{-1}
acts via
z^{-1}\otimes(\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{i-1}\psi_{i+1}\cdots\psi_{m})
.
Considering the norms of these operators, we see that this action extends to an action of
R^{(h^{\prime})}
for some
h^{\prime}\geq h
, and is compatible with that of
R\langle p^{mh}z,p^{mh}z^{-1}\rangle
, in the sense that
(Y_{1}\cdots Y_{m})^{\pm 1}
acts as
z^{\pm 1}
. This allows us to construct an
R^{(h^{\prime})}
-module map

R^{(h^{\prime})}\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})\longrightarrow R\langle p^{mh}z,p^{mh}z^{-1}\rangle\otimes_{R[Z]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})

whose composition with
Equation

6.9
on either side gives the transition maps for the inverse systems
R\langle p^{mh}z,p^{mh}z^{-1}\rangle\otimes_{R[Z]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})
and
R^{(h)}(y_{*})\otimes_{R[Y_{1},...,Y_{m}]}H_{s}(M_{\bullet})
.
Via
Equation

6.8
and its analogue for
Z
instead of
Y_{1},...,Y_{m}
, we obtain the second isomorphism in
Equation

6.7
, and coadmissibility over
R\{\!\{z,z^{-1}\}\!\}
also follows.
∎

Finally, let us state the version of the spectral theorem that we will actually use in practice.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
R
be a commutative Noetherian Banach algebra and
M_{\bullet}
a complex of orthonormalisable
R
-Banach modules. Let
\widetilde{\Lambda},\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-},\Lambda,\Lambda^{-}
and
\chi
be as in
Proposition

3.6
. Assume that for each
\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
we are given a chain operator
\phi_{\lambda}
on
M_{\bullet}
and that
\phi_{\mu}
is
R
-compact for some
\mu\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
. Assume that for any
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
, the operators
\phi_{\lambda_{1}}\phi_{\lambda_{2}}
and
\phi_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}
agree on
H_{*}(M_{\bullet})
. Then:

(i)

The space

\text{Tor}^{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}_{q}\left(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet})\right)

is a finitely generated
R
-module for all
q
and
s
, where
\lambda
acts on
H_{s}(M_{\bullet})
via
\phi_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

If
R\longrightarrow R^{\prime}
is a flat continuous map of Noetherian Banach algebras, then

\displaystyle\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}_{q}\left(R^{\prime}(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})\right)=\text{Tor}^{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}_{q}\left(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet})\right)\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}.

Proof.
Note that if
\phi_{\mu}
is
R
-compact, we may assume that for any given
\lambda\in\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}
the operator
\phi_{\mu\lambda}
is also
R
-compact (by replacing it with the operator
\phi_{\mu}\phi_{\lambda}
if necessary).
In particular, we may assume that
\mu\in\Lambda^{-}
.
Let us start by proving (i) and (ii) for
\Lambda^{-}
instead of
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}

This follows from a similar argument as in the end of the proof of
Theorem

6.1
. By
Lemma

3.3
(i) there exists a free abelian submonoid
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
such that
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\prime}(\Lambda^{\prime})^{-1}
. Writing
\mu=\lambda\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}
with
\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda}\in\Lambda^{\prime}
and replacing
\mu
with
\lambda=\mu\widetilde{\lambda}
we may assume that
\mu\in\Lambda^{\prime}
.

Let
e_{1},...,e_{n}
be a basis of
\Lambda^{\prime}
.
We may not apply
Theorem

6.1
yet, as we can’t guarantee that
\phi_{e_{1}\cdots e_{n}}
is
R
-compact.
If
n=1
, we can replace
\Lambda
by the subgroup generated by
\mu
so that this holds.
If
n\geq 2
, we may write
\mu=e_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots e_{n}^{a_{n}}
for some non-negative integers
a_{1},...,a_{n}
, and changing the order of the basis elements and multiplying
\mu
by
e_{1}
or
e_{2}
and replacing
\mu
by
\mu e_{1}
or
\mu e_{2}
as in the previous paragraph, we may assume that
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are coprime.
In particular, there exist integers
c_{1},c_{2}\geq 0
such that
a_{1}c_{2}-a_{2}c_{1}=1
. Let
\lambda=e_{1}^{c_{1}}e_{2}^{c_{2}}\in\Lambda^{\prime}
, and consider the (free abelian) submonoid of
\Lambda^{\prime}
generated by
\mu,\lambda,e_{3},...,e_{n}
. Then,
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\prime\prime}(\Lambda^{\prime\prime})^{-1}
. By
Lemma

3.1
(ii),

\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))=\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{\prime\prime}]}_{q}(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))

and in
Theorem

6.1
we showed that the latter is finitely generated over
R
and commutes with completed tensor products with flat
R
-modules.

Next, we will prove the result for
\widetilde{\Lambda}
. For this, we use
Lemma

3.5
, which says that there is a natural map

\text{Tor}^{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))\longrightarrow\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))

that identifies the source with a direct summand of the target. Since the target is finitely generated over
R
, so is the source, and if
R^{\prime}
is as in (ii), then there is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Tor}^{R[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Tor}^{R[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}))\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[\widetilde{\Lambda}^{-}]}_{q}(R^{\prime}(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Tor}^{R^{\prime}[\Lambda^{-}]}_{q}(R^{\prime}(\chi),H_{s}(M_{\bullet}\cotimes_{R}R^{\prime})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the bent horizontal arrows are sections of the straight ones, and the vertical maps commute with the straight (resp. bent) horizontal arrows.
This implies that the left vertical arrow must also be an isomorphism.
∎

6.2.
Character spaces

Let
E
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
In this section we gather some facts about character spaces of locally analytic groups.
For example, they exist:

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
G
be a topologically finitely generated
p
-adic locally analytic group. There exists a rigid
E
-analytic strictly quasi-Stein space
\widehat{G}
representing the functor that associates to a rigid
E
-analytic space
X
to the set of continuous characters
G\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\times}
.
In fact, there exist non-negative integers
d_{1},d_{2}
such that
\widehat{G}
is isomorphic to a union of finitely many copies of the product of
\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}^{d_{1}}
(where
\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
is the rigid
E
-analytic multiplicative group) and open unit
d_{2}
-dimensional balls.

Proof.
Let
\overline{[G,G]}
be the closure of the commutator subgroup of
G
. Any continuous character
G\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\times}
is trivial on
\overline{[G,G]}
, so if
H:=G/\overline{[G,G]}
, then we can identify the set of continuous characters
G\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\times}
with the set of continuous characters
H\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\times}
. Since
H
is a topologically finitely generated locally analytic abelian group, by
[
Eme17
, Proposition 6.4.5]
there exists a rigid analytic space
\widehat{H}
as claimed representing this functor, so we can set
\widehat{G}:=\widehat{H}
.
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
G
be a topologically finitely generated
p
-adic locally analytic group, and
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\widehat{G}(E)
a finite group of characters. Then,
\widehat{G}
can be admissibly covered by affinoid subspaces that are stable under the action of
\mathcal{T}
by multiplication.

Proof.
Let
\widehat{G}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}X_{n}
realise
\widehat{G}
as a strictly quasi-Stein space. For each
n
, define
Y_{n}=\bigcap_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}X_{n}\tau
. Then
\{Y_{n}\}
is an admissible covering satisfying our requirements.
∎

Let us now place ourselves in our familiar setting of reductive groups over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. More concretely,
\mathbf{G}
is a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with parabolic subgroup
\mathbf{P}
,
\mathbf{M}
is a Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
,
\mathbf{A}
is a maximal
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-split torus in the center of
\mathbf{M}
, etc. Write
\mathbf{Z}
for the center of
\mathbf{M}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
H_{0}\subseteq M
be a compact open subgroup and
H
an open subgroup of
M
containing
AH_{0}
with finite index. Then, the morphism of rigid spaces
\widehat{M}\longrightarrow\widehat{H}
induced by restriction of characters is finite flat.

Proof.
It follows from the constructions of
\widehat{M}
and
\widehat{H}
and a case by case analysis that it’s enough to show that the homomorphism
H/\overline{[H,H]}\longrightarrow M/\overline{[M,M]}
has finite kernel and cokernel. We proved this in
Lemma

2.35
.
∎

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
H_{0}\subseteq M
be a compact open subgroup.
Then,
\dim(\widehat{H_{0}})=\dim(\mathbf{Z})
.

Proof.
Let
Z_{0}
be the maximal compact subgroup of
\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
The homomorphisms

Z_{0}\simeq\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})/\Lambda\rightarrow(M/\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))/\Lambda\leftarrow(M/\overline{[M,M]})/\Lambda\leftarrow\Lambda H_{0}/\Lambda\overline{[H_{0},H_{0}]}\simeq H_{0}/\overline{[H_{0},H_{0}]}

all have finite kernel and cokernel by the proof of
Lemma

2.35
, so all the corresponding character spaces have the same dimension. Thus, the proposition follows from the fact that, as
Z_{0}
is compact, we have

\dim(\widehat{Z_{0}})=\dim(Z_{0})=\dim(\mathbf{Z})
.
∎

Recall that a subset
S
of a rigid
E
-analytic space
X
is called Zariski-dense if the smallest Zariski closed subvariety of
X
containing
S
is
X
itself, that
S
accumulates at a point
x
of
X
if any affinoid open neighbourhood of
x
contains an affinoid subdomain
U
such that
S\cap U
is Zariski-dense in
U
, and that
S
is an accumulation subset of
X
if it accumulates at every point of
S
.

Assume now that
\mathbf{G}=\Res_{L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
for some finite extension
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
such that
E
contains the images of all the embeddings of
L
into
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
split
reductive group
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
over
L
.
Then,
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{T}
admit similar descriptions.
The group
\mathbf{G}
is quasi-split, so we may fix a Borel subgroup
\mathbf{B}\subseteq\mathbf{P}
.
Let
\Delta_{E}
(resp.
\Delta_{M,E}
) be the set of simple positive roots of
\mathbf{G}_{E}
(resp.
\mathbf{M}_{E}
) with respect to
\mathbf{B}_{E}
(resp.
\mathbf{M}_{E}\cap\mathbf{B}_{E}
).
Fix an algebraic character
\lambda
of
\mathbf{T}_{E}
that is dominant with respect to
\mathbf{M}_{E}\cap\mathbf{B}_{E}
.
If
H
is closed subgroup of
M
, by an algebraic (resp. dominant regular) weight of
\widehat{H}
we mean the restriction to
H
of an algebraic character of
\mathbf{M}_{E}
(resp. an algebraic character
\psi
of
\mathbf{M}_{E}
such that
\lambda\psi
is dominant regular with respect to
\mathbf{B}_{E}
).

Proposition 6.15
.

With the assumptions above, fix
a\in A^{--}
and let
H_{0}
be a compact open subgroup of
M
such that
H_{0}\cap Z=Z_{0}
is the maximal compact subgroup of
Z=\mathbf{Z}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
Fix
h>0
.
Then, the set of dominant regular weights
\psi
such that

(6.16)

\displaystyle v_{p}((s_{\alpha}\cdot(\lambda\psi))(a))-v_{p}((\lambda\psi)(a))>h

for all
\alpha\in\Delta_{E}\smallsetminus\Delta_{M,E}
accumulates at any algebraic weight of
\widehat{H_{0}}
and are Zariski-dense in the connected components of
\widehat{H_{0}}
containing an algebraic weight.

Proof.
The proof of
Proposition

6.14
shows that the restriction map
\widehat{H_{0}}\longrightarrow\widehat{Z_{0}}
is finite flat.
By
[
Che04
, Lemme 6.2.8]
, it suffices to prove the statement with
H_{0}
replaced by
Z_{0}
.
Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{Z}}
be the center of
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}
its identity component, or equivalently its maximal subtorus.
Let
\widetilde{A}:=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(L)
and let
\widetilde{A}_{0}
be its maximal compact subgroup.
Then,
\widetilde{A}_{0}
has finite index in
Z_{0}
, so
[
Che04
, Lemme 6.2.8]
again shows that it’s enough to prove the statement of the proposition for
\widehat{\widetilde{A}_{0}}
instead of
\widehat{Z_{0}}
(or
\widehat{H_{0}}
).
The proof in this setting is standard.
∎

6.3.
Eigenvarieties from
S
-arithmetic homology

We are finally ready to tackle our construction of eigenvarieties.
We fix throughout a base field
E
that is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

We place ourselves in the setting of
Section

5.3
, except we assume that
\sigma
and
U
are
E
-vector spaces instead of
R
-modules.
If
\mathcal{U}
is an open affinoid subspace of
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
,
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)
, the inclusion
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
induces a continuous character
\delta_{\mathcal{U}}\colon M_{\Sigma}\longrightarrow R^{\times}
. For any such affinoid, we let
\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}=\sigma\otimes_{E}\delta_{\mathcal{U}}
and
V_{\mathcal{U}}=\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{M}(\sigma_{\mathcal{U}})\simeq V\cotimes_{E}\delta_{\mathcal{U}}
. These give rise to representations

\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}}):=U\otimes_{E}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V_{\mathcal{U}})^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}\simeq(U\otimes_{E}R)\otimes_{R}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V_{\mathcal{U}})^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}},

\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{{\Sigma-\text{la}}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
, etc. (this is the same notation as in
Section

5.3
, except we write
U
instead of
U\otimes_{E}R
).

Proposition 6.17
.

The association
\mathcal{U}\longmapsto H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}}))
defines a coherent sheaf
\mathscr{H}
on
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
.

Proof.
We need to check that if
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)
, then
H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}}))
is finitely generated over
R
and that if
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is an admissible open affinoid,
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R^{\prime})
, then
H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}))\simeq H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}}))\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
.
In view of the spectral sequence in
Corollary

5.9
, it’s enough to prove the same statements for
\text{Tor}_{i}^{\mathcal{H}_{S,R}^{-}}(R(1),H_{j}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}))
and its analogue over
R^{\prime}
for all
i,j
.
This follows from
Corollary

6.10
since
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
is a complex of orthonormalisable Banach
R
-modules
(the term in each degree being isomorphic to a finite direct sum of copies of
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}
),
the operators
U_{\mu}
give a well-defined action of
\mathcal{H}_{S,R}^{-}
on homology (recall also
Corollary

2.33
),
and if
a\in A^{--}_{\Sigma}
then the operator
U_{a}
is
R
-compact.
∎

The action of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
on
H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}}))
induces a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\text{End}_{\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}}(\mathscr{H})
, so we obtain a coherent sheaf
\mathscr{T}
of
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}}
-algebras on
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
assigning to
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)\subseteq\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
the
R
-subalgebra
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{U}}
of
\text{End}_{R}(H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}})))
generated by the image of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
under the above homomorphism.

We write
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
for the relative spectrum of
\mathscr{T}
over
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
. As
V
may be isomorphic to some of its twists by characters of
M_{\Sigma}
, it is possible for the same systems of eigenvalues for
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})\otimes\mathcal{H}_{S,E}^{-}
to be found on the fibers of several characters (note that we are including the Hecke operators at
p
). This can be solved by taking the quotient of
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
by the group of such characters.
The following lemma shows that we can actually do this in most cases of interest.

Lemma 6.18
.

Assume that
V_{\Sigma}
has a central character.
The group
\mathcal{T}_{M}
of continuous characters
\delta\colon M_{\Sigma}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
such that
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\otimes_{E}V\simeq V\otimes\delta
is finite. If all such characters are defined over
E
and
\text{End}_{G}(V)=E
, this group acts compatibly on the spaces
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
and
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
, which have (compatible) admissible affinoid coverings which are stable under this action.

Proof.
Comparing the central characters of
V_{\Sigma}
and
V_{\Sigma}\otimes\delta
for a character
\delta
as above we see that the restriction of
\delta
to the center of
M_{\Sigma}
is trivial. It is also trivial on
\overline{[M_{\Sigma},M_{\Sigma}]}
, which has finite index in the group of points of the derived group,
\mathbf{M}_{\Sigma}^{\text{der}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Thus,
\delta
factors through the (finite) quotient of
M_{\Sigma}
by the subgroup generated by its center and
\overline{[M_{\Sigma},M_{\Sigma}]}
. In particular, there are only finitely many possibilities for
\delta
.

Assume that all such
\delta
are defined over
E
. By
Lemma

6.12
, this group acts on
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
, which admits an admissible covering
\{\mathcal{U}_{i}\}_{i}
by open affinoids that are stable under this action. Assume that
\text{End}_{G}(V)=E
. This implies that the isomorphisms
V\simeq V(\delta)
for
\delta
as above are uniquely defined up to a constant. The pullback
\delta^{*}\mathscr{H}
sends
\mathcal{U}_{i}
to
H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}_{i}}\otimes\delta))
, which is isomorphic to
H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}_{i}}))
canonically-up-to-scalar. This determines a canonical isomorphism
(\delta^{*}\mathscr{T})(\mathcal{U}_{i})\simeq\mathscr{T}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
that is compatible with multiplication of characters and with restriction to (invariant) affinoid subspaces. This gives rise to an action on
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
as claimed in the statement.
∎

Definition 6.19
.

If all the assumptions in (both parts of)
Lemma

6.18
are satisfied, we call the quotient
\mathscr{Y}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
of
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
by the action of
\mathcal{T}_{M}
the eigenvariety of tame level
K^{S}
associated to
(V,U)
(with variation at places in
\Sigma
).

Let us remark that this space does not depend on the choice of
\sigma
giving rise to
V
up to canonical isomorphism, and depends on
U
only up to isomorphism, which is canonical if
U
is an irreducible algebraic representation of
G_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}
(or more generally, if
U
has no non-trivial automorphisms). Moreover, twisting
U
or
V
by a locally analytic character of
M^{\Sigma}
gives canonically isomorphic eigenvarieties.

It is usually more convenient to work directly with
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
and pass to the quotient
\mathscr{Y}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
afterwards if necessary, so we will not make use of
\mathscr{Y}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
very often.
Note that the construction of
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
can be carried out verbatim if one works over
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
instead of
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
, the only difference being that it does not make sense to say that
V_{\mathcal{U}}:=\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{M}(\sigma_{\mathcal{U}})\simeq V\otimes_{E}\delta_{\mathcal{U}}
for
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)\subseteq\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
an open affinoid with associated character
\delta_{\mathcal{U}}
(since
V\otimes_{E}\delta_{\mathcal{U}}
is not a representation of
M
).
Thus, this construction depends on
\sigma
and
\mathpzc{M}
and not just on
V
.
We will write
\mathscr{H}_{\mathpzc{M}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathpzc{M},\mathcal{U}}
for the analogues of
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{U}}
.
Write
\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M},\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
for the resulting variety over
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
.

Proposition 6.20
.

The inclusion
\mathpzc{M}\xhookrightarrow{}M
induces an isomorphism

\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M},\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\times_{\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}}\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}

Proof.
By
Lemma

6.13
, the natural morphism
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
is finite flat. Let
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)
be an open affinoid subset of
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
. The preimage
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{U}
in
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
is then an affinoid open subset of
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
,
\mathcal{V}=\text{Sp}(B)
, where
B
is a finite flat
R
-algebra. Write
\delta_{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}\longrightarrow R^{\times}
and
\delta_{\mathcal{V}}\colon M_{\Sigma}\longrightarrow B^{\times}
for the associated characters. The restriction
\delta_{\mathcal{V}}|_{\mathpzc{M}}
is simply the composition of
\delta_{\mathcal{U}}
and the morphism
R\longrightarrow B
. In particular, it follows from
Corollary

6.10
(ii) together with the spectral sequence from
Corollary

5.9
that there is a
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
-equivariant isomorphism

H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{V}}))\simeq H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V_{\mathcal{U}}))\otimes_{R}B

This shows the coherent sheaf
\mathscr{H}
on
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
is the pullback of its analogue on
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
. The same is then true for the coherent sheaves of
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}}
-algebras and
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}}
-algebras obtained from the action of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
. Taking relative spectra, we obtain the result.
∎

Proposition 6.21
.

(i)If
\mathpzc{M}\subseteq\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M}^{\prime},\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}}(\sigma),U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M},\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\times_{\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}}\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}}.

(ii)

If
\mathpzc{M}\subseteq\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
and
\sigma
extends to a representation of
\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
, then there is a natural closed immersion

\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M}^{\prime},\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\xhookrightarrow{}\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M},\sigma|_{\mathpzc{M}},U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\times_{\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}}\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}}

Proof.
Statement (i) follows from the transitivity of compact induction from
\mathpzc{M}
to
\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathpzc{M}^{\prime}
to
M
and the same arguments as in the proof of
Proposition

6.20
.
Let us prove (ii). Let

\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)\subseteq\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
be an admissible open affinoid and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\text{Sp}(R^{\prime})
be its preimage in
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}^{\prime}}
.
The result will follow if we show that for all
i
and
j
,
\text{Tor}_{i}^{\mathcal{H}_{S,\mathpzc{M}^{\prime},R^{\prime}}^{-}}(R^{\prime}(1),H_{j}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}))
is a direct summand of
\text{Tor}_{i}^{\mathcal{H}_{S,\mathpzc{M},R}^{-}}(R(1),H_{j}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}))\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}
,
which by
Corollary

6.10
is isomorphic to
\text{Tor}_{i}^{\mathcal{H}_{S,\mathpzc{M},R^{\prime}}^{-}}(R^{\prime}(1),H_{j}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}))
. This follows from
Lemma

3.5
.
∎

In preparation for the next section, let us introduce two other auxiliary variants of these eigenvarieties.
Consider instead of the coherent sheaf on
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
corresponding to
S
-arithmetic cohomology as above the coherent sheaves
\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\mathpzc{M}}
and
\mathscr{H}^{\infty}_{\mathpzc{M}}
determined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\mathpzc{M}}(\mathcal{U}):=\bigoplus_{j}E^{2}_{0,j}=R(1)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}_{S,\mathpzc{M},R}^{-}}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathscr{H}^{\infty}_{\mathpzc{M}}(\mathcal{U}):=\bigoplus_{j}E^{\infty}_{0,j},

where
E^{r}_{i,j}
denotes the spectral sequence from
Corollary

5.9
.
We will write
\mathbb{T}_{\mathpzc{M},\mathcal{U}}^{2}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathpzc{M},\mathcal{U}}^{\infty}
for the
R
-algebra generated by the image of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
in the algebra of
R
-module endomorphisms of the right hand sides and
\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M}}^{2}(K^{S})
and
\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M}}^{\infty}(K^{S})
for the varieties formed by gluing these
R
-algebras (since these varieties will only play an auxiliary role, we will suppress
\Sigma,\sigma
and
U
from the notation).
The edge maps in the spectral sequence from
Corollary

5.9
determine maps

\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\mathpzc{M}}(\mathcal{U})\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{H}^{\infty}_{\mathpzc{M}}(\mathcal{U})\xhookrightarrow{}\mathscr{H}_{\mathpzc{M}}(\mathcal{U})

and hence surjections

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathpzc{M},\mathcal{U}}^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\mathpzc{M},\mathcal{U}}^{\infty}\twoheadleftarrow\mathbb{T}_{\mathpzc{M},\mathcal{U}}.

In conclusion, there are closed immersions

\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M}}^{2}(K^{S})\mathrel{{\mathop{\hookleftarrow}\limits}}\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M}}^{\infty}(K^{S})\mathrel{{\mathop{\xhookrightarrow{}}\limits}}\mathscr{X}_{\mathpzc{M},\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S}).

Proposition 6.23
.

(i)
\iota_{2}
and
\iota
become isomorphisms after passing to nilreductions.

(ii)

Assume that
\mathpzc{M}=AM_{0}
and that
\Lambda
acts on
\sigma
by a character. If
\Sigma=S
, then
\iota_{2}
and
\iota
are isomorphisms.

Proof.
For (i), since affinoid algebras are Jacobson, it’s enough to show that for all open affinoids
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)
of
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
the
R
-algebra affinoid epimorphisms
Equation

6.22
induce bijections on maximal ideals. Injectivity is immediate, so we only need to show that the induced maps on maximal ideals are surjective.
For
\iota_{2}
, this is equivalent to showing that any system of Hecke eigenvalues appearing in the
i=0
column of the
E^{\infty}_{i,j}
page of the spectral sequence from
Corollary

5.9
appears also in the same column for the
E^{2}
page.
For
\iota
, it’s enough to show that any system of eigenvalues contributing to the
E^{\infty}_{i,j}
page appears in the column
i=0
.
Therefore, it suffices to show that any system of eigenvalues contributing to the
E^{\infty}
page appears in a term of the
i=0
column in the
E^{2}_{i,j}
page whose localisation at the corresponding maximal ideal is stable.
Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a maximal ideal in
\mathbb{T}(K^{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}R
such that the localisation
(E^{\infty})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is non-zero.
The intersection of
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathcal{H}_{S,R}^{-}
is the ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
generated by
U_{\mu}-1
with
\mu\in\mathpzc{M}^{-}
.
Consider the spectral sequence of
\mathbb{T}(K^{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}R
-modules obtained by localising at
\mathfrak{m}
, with
E^{2}
page

E^{2}_{i,j}=\text{Tor}^{\mathcal{H}_{S,R}^{-}}_{i}(R(1),H_{j}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{\Sigma})_{\mathfrak{m}}).

Let
j_{0}
be the smallest degree for which
H_{j}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U}^{\Sigma})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is non-zero (it exists since
(E^{\infty})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is non-zero). Then the
E^{2}_{0,j_{0}}
-term in the spectral sequence above is also non-zero.
This term is stable, which proves (i).
The proof of (ii) essentially follows from
Proposition

6.6
, which implies that the
E^{2}_{i,j}
page above can be non-zero only in the
i=0
column.
We defer the details of this proof to the next section.
∎

6.4.
Eigenvarieties from overconvergent homology

Next, we will define analogues of some of the varieties from the previous section using overconvergent homology.
We will relate both constructions and study the latter to deduce some properties of the former. We change the setting slightly for now and assume that
\sigma
is a representation of
M_{0}
on a finite-dimensional
E
-vector space that decomposes as
\sigma=\sigma_{\Sigma}\otimes_{E}\sigma^{\Sigma}
as usual. Consider the rigid
E
-analytic space
\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}
parametrising continuous characters of
M_{\Sigma,0}
. For any open affinoid
\Omega=\text{Sp}(B)
of
\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}
with associated character
\delta_{\Omega}\colon M_{\Sigma,0}\longrightarrow B^{\times}
consider the
B[M_{0}]
-module
\sigma_{\Omega}:=\sigma\otimes_{E}\delta_{\Omega}
and the Banach
B
-modules
\mathcal{A}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}
defined in
Section

5.4
. Fix
a\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{--}
.

The construction using overconvergent homology follows the same steps as in
[
Han17
, Section 4]
, and we refer to this reference for the details.
Let
F_{\Omega}(X)=\det(1-U_{a}X)
be the characteristic power series of the operator
U_{a}
on
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})
.
This is well-defined, since the complex
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})
consists of orthonormalisable Banach
B
-modules and the operator
U_{a}
is compact, and independent of
s
by
Proposition

5.7

Item

(v)
.
Moreover, by
Proposition

5.7

Item

(v)
again, if
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
is an open affinoid,
\Omega^{\prime}=\text{Sp}(B^{\prime})
, then
F_{\Omega^{\prime}}(X)=F_{\Omega}(X)\otimes_{B}B^{\prime}
.
Thus, we may glue the power series
F_{\Omega}
to form a Fredholm series
F\in\mathcal{O}(\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}})\{\!\{X\}\!\}
.
Let
\mathscr{Z}\subseteq\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
be the corresponding Fredholm hypersurface, i.e. the vanishing locus of
F
in
\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
, where
\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
denotes the rigid
E
-analytic multiplicative group.
The open affinoids
\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h}=\mathscr{Z}\cap(\Omega\times\{p^{-h}\leq|t|\leq 1\})
such that
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})
has a slope-
\leq h
decomposition with respect to
U_{a}
form an admissible cover of
\mathscr{Z}
.
For such
\Omega=\text{Sp}(B)
and
h
, the slope decomposition induces a factorisation
F_{\Omega}(X)=Q(X)R(X)
, where
Q(X)
is the characteristic polynomial
\det(1-U_{a}X)
on the slope-
\leq h
part, which is multiplicative and has slope
\leq h
, and
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h})\simeq B[X]/Q(X)
.
Let
\chi^{\Sigma}\colon\Lambda^{\Sigma}\longrightarrow E^{\times}
be a character and let
\mathfrak{m}_{\chi^{\Sigma},E}^{\Sigma}
be the ideal of
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S\smallsetminus\Sigma,AM_{0},E}
generated by
U_{\widetilde{a}}-\chi^{\Sigma}(\widetilde{a})
for
\widetilde{a}\in(A^{\Sigma})^{-}
.
The module
H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}
does not depend on the choice of
s
, so we will write
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}=\mathcal{A}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}
.
We can view
H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}
as an
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h})
-module by letting
X
act via
U_{a}^{-1}
, and the assignment
\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h}\longmapsto H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}/\mathfrak{m}_{\chi^{\Sigma},E}^{\Sigma}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}
defines a coherent sheaf
\mathscr{H}_{*}
on
\mathscr{Z}
with an action of the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
.
Taking the relative spectrum of the coherent subsheaf of
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h})
-algebras of
\text{End}(\mathscr{H}_{*})
induced by this action as in the previous section, we obtain a rigid
E
-analytic space
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma,U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
.

The main difference between this construction and that of the previous section (apart from working with the untwisted version of overconvergent homology) is the way in which we truncate slopes or pass to finite slope subspaces.
While on this construction this is achieved by taking slope decompositions, in the previous one (or rather, its untwisted analogue) it was done by taking (the derived functors of)
R(\chi)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S,E}}{-}
.
Working with slope decompositions is easier and gives more information (for example, it shows that eigenvarieties are locally finite over weight space
\widehat{\mathpzc{M}_{\Sigma}}
), but the gluing of the local pieces is more subtle.
In order to compare eigenvarieties, we will have to compare both of these procedures.
We will do this in two steps: constructing the analogues of the open affinoids
\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h}
for the varieties in
Section

6.3
, and showing that these do indeed give an admissible cover of such varieties.

Recall that the product map
\Lambda_{\Sigma}\times M_{\Sigma,0}\longrightarrow A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}
is an isomorphism. Extend the action of
\sigma
to an action of
AM_{0}
by letting
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
act trivially and letting
\Lambda^{\Sigma}
act via the character
\chi^{\Sigma}
.
We have
\widehat{A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}}\simeq\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}
. Since
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
is a free abelian group of rank
d=\rank(\mathbf{A}_{\Sigma})
, the choice of a basis of
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
determines an isomorphism
\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}^{d}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}=\Omega\times\Theta\subseteq\widehat{A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}}
be an admissible open affinoid,
\mathcal{U}=\text{Sp}(R)
, with
\Omega=\text{Sp}(B)\subseteq\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}
and
\Theta\subseteq\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}
.
As remarked when defining the
*
-action in
Section

5.4
, there is an isomorphism of
JA^{+}J
-modules
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U,*}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}\cotimes_{B}R
.
Note that
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U,*}
is defined in
Section

4.4
, whereas
\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}
is defined in
Section

5.4
. For the former, we are indeed in the situation described in that section, since
\Lambda
acts on
\sigma_{\mathcal{U}}
via
\chi\otimes\delta_{\Theta}
, where
\delta_{\Theta}
is the inflation to
\Lambda
of the character of
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
corresponding to
\Theta
.

By
Lemma

3.3
(i) there exists a free abelian submonoid
\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{-}
such that
\Lambda_{\Sigma}=\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{\prime}(\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{\prime})^{-1}
, and by the arguments in the proof of
Corollary

6.10
we may assume that
\Lambda^{\prime}
admits a basis
a_{1},...,a_{d}
such that
a_{1}\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{--}
. Thus, we may assume that the element
a\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{--}
fixed in the beginning of the section is
a_{1}
. From now on, whenever we identify
\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}
with
\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}^{d}
, we will do so with respect to this basis. Write
T_{1},...,T_{d}
for the coordinates of
\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}^{d}
.

Given
\Omega\subseteq\widehat{M_{0}},\Theta\subseteq\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}
, let
R=\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta)
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta}

\displaystyle:=R\cdot\Im\left(\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\text{End}_{R}(R(\chi^{\Sigma}\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta})\otimes_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S,AM_{0},R}}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta},U,*}))\right)

(a priori, this may depend on the degree of analyticity
s
since we are not taking slope
\leq h
parts, but we omit it from the notation nonetheless). In particular, by
Lemma

5.11
there is an isomorphism
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta}\simeq R(\chi^{\Sigma}\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta})\otimes_{R}\mathbb{T}^{2}_{AM_{0},\Omega\times\Theta}
of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathcal{H}_{S,R}^{-}
-algebras, and hence there is an isomorphism
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta}\simeq\mathbb{T}^{2}_{AM_{0},\Omega\times\Theta}
of affinoid
R
-algebras.
Note that the character
\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta}
sends
a_{i}\in\Lambda
to
T_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(\Theta)
.
Thus,
\mathscr{X}_{AM_{0}}^{2}(K^{S})
may be seen as being formed by gluing the
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta}
for different
\Omega,\Theta
.
If
h\geq 0
is such that
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})
admits a slope-
\leq h
decomposition, let

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h})\cdot\Im\left(\mathbb{T}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\text{End}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h})}\left(\frac{H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}{\mathfrak{m}_{\chi^{\Sigma},E}^{\Sigma}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}\right)\right).

Hence, the space
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
is forming by gluing
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h}
as we vary over
\Omega
and
h
.

Given
\Omega
and
h
as above, for
i=1,...,d
, the image of
U_{a_{i}}
in

\mathcal{E}:=\text{End}_{\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h})}\left(\frac{H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}{\mathfrak{m}_{\chi^{\Sigma},E}^{\Sigma}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}\right)

is a unit, so there exists
h_{i}
such that
p^{-h_{i}}\leq|U_{a_{i}}^{-1}|_{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}\leq 1
(recall that the operators
U_{a}
are norm-decreasing).
For example, we may take
h_{1}=h
.
In any case, we assume that
h_{1}\leq h
.
Let
\mathbf{h}=(h_{1},...,h_{d})
.
Define an affinoid subset of
\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}^{d}
by

\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h}):=\{(T_{1},...,T_{d}):p^{-h_{i}}\leq|T_{i}|\leq 1\text{ for all }i\}.

Note that by
[
Han17
, Proposition 2.3.4]

H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})},U,*})
admits a slope-
\leq h
decomposition, and by

Lemma

5.10
applied to this decomposition (and the assumption that
h_{1}\leq h
) we could take the slope-
\leq h
part of homology in the definition of
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})}
without altering it. By definition of
\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})
, there is a morphism of affinoid algebras

\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h}))\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h}

sending
T_{i}
to the image of
U_{a_{i}}
, so we may regard the target as an
\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h}))
-algebra.
The following lemma and its proof complete the first step outline above, and give a comparison of the two methods we have to truncate slopes.

Lemma 6.24
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h}))
-algebras
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h}
.

Proof.
We will show that we may canonically identify
\frac{H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}{\mathfrak{m}_{\chi^{\Sigma},E}^{\Sigma}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}
with
R(\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})}\chi^{\Sigma})\otimes_{\mathcal{H}_{S,AM_{0},R}^{-}}H_{j}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})},U,*})

as
\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h}))\otimes\mathbb{T}(K^{S})
-modules.
Let
R=\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h}))
and
B=\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
.
We first claim that the natural map

H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}R\otimes_{B[T_{1},...,T_{d}]}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}.

is an isomorphism of
R
-modules (where we consider the target as an
R
-module by letting
R
act on either of the terms in the tensor product, the action on the second being induced by
R\longmapsto\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h}
) whose inverse is given by the
R
-algebra structure on
H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}
. Indeed, the fact that
H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}
is finitely generated over
B[T_{1},...,T_{d}]\subseteq R
(since it is over
B
) and
B[T_{1},...,T_{d}]
is dense in
R
implies that

\displaystyle R\otimes_{B[T_{1},...,T_{d}]}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}

\displaystyle\simeq R\cotimes_{B[T_{1},...,T_{d}]}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}

\displaystyle\simeq R\cotimes_{R}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}

\displaystyle\simeq H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}.

By
Lemma

3.1
(ii), we can rephrase this as saying that there is an
R\otimes\mathbb{T}(K^{S})
-module isomorphism

(6.25)

\displaystyle\frac{H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}{\mathfrak{m}_{\chi^{\Sigma},E}^{\Sigma}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})_{\leq h}}\simeq R(\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})}\chi^{\Sigma})\otimes_{\mathcal{H}_{S,AM_{0},R}^{-}}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})},U,*})_{\leq h}.

As
|\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})}(a_{1})^{-1}|=|T_{1}^{-1}|_{\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})}\leq p^{h}
,

Lemma

5.10
(and the comment above it) shows that the right-hand side is isomorphic to
R(\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta}\chi^{\Sigma})\otimes_{\mathcal{H}_{S,AM_{0},R}^{-}}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})},U,*})
, thus proving our claim and the lemma.
∎

The second step in our comparison of eigenvarieties is a consequence of
Proposition

6.6
, as the following proposition shows.

Proposition 6.26
.

The morphism
\widehat{A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}}\longrightarrow\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
induced by the morphism

\widehat{A_{\Sigma}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\langle a\rangle}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
induces a finite morphism
\mathscr{X}^{2}_{AM_{0}}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
that factors through a closed subvariety of the target whose underlying analytic subset is a Zariski closed subset of
\mathscr{Z}
, and such that the preimage of
\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h}
is
\text{Sp}(\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})})
.
In particular, the affinoid open subspaces
\text{Sp}(\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})})
form an admissible cover of
\mathscr{X}^{2}_{AM_{0}}(K^{S})
.

Proof.
Consider the pushforward of
\mathscr{H}^{2}_{AM_{0}}
along the morphism
\widehat{A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}}\longrightarrow\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
. By
Proposition

6.6
and the way we have chosen
a_{1},...,a_{d}
following the strategy of
Corollary

6.10
, it is a coherent sheaf that sends
\Omega\times\text{Sp}(E\langle p^{h}T_{1},p^{h}T_{1}^{-1}\rangle)
to

E\langle p^{h}T_{1},p^{h}T_{1}^{-1}\rangle\otimes_{E[T_{1}]}\mathcal{O}(\Omega)(\chi^{\Sigma})\otimes_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S\smallsetminus\Sigma,\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}).

Thus, the morphism
\mathscr{X}^{2}_{AM_{0}}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
is indeed finite and factors through the schematic support of this coherent sheaf.
This is contained in the analytic subset of
\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
corresponding to the support of the coherent sheaf sending the affinoid
\Omega\times\text{Sp}(E\langle p^{h}T_{1},p^{h}T_{1}^{-1}\rangle)
to

E\langle p^{h}T_{1},p^{h}T_{1}^{-1}\rangle\otimes_{E[T_{1}]}H_{*}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}).

Thus, it’s enough to show that this is precisely the underlying analytic subset of
\mathscr{Z}
.
This follows from the same argument as in
[
BHS17
, Lemme 3.9]
, and the statement on preimages follows easily.
∎

Proposition 6.27
.

The maps
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h}
induce an isomorphism

\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathscr{X}^{2}_{AM_{0}}(K^{S}).

Proof.
This is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

6.24
and
Proposition

6.26
.
∎

We can extend these results to include the case where
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
acts on
\sigma
through a non-trivial character.
If
\chi_{\Sigma}
is a character of
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
with coefficients in
E
, we may consider
\chi_{\Sigma}
as a character of
A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}=\Lambda_{\Sigma}\times\Lambda^{\Sigma}M_{0}
by imposing
\chi(\Lambda^{\Sigma}M_{0})=1
. Multiplication by
\chi_{\Sigma}
induces an automorphism
m_{\chi_{\Sigma}}
of
\widehat{\Lambda^{\Sigma}}
, and the description of
\mathscr{X}^{2}_{AM_{0},\sigma}(K^{S})
via the
*
-action shows that

(6.28)

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}^{2}_{AM_{0},\sigma\otimes\chi_{\Sigma}}(K^{S})\simeq\mathscr{X}^{2}_{AM_{0},\sigma}(K^{S})\times_{\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}},m_{\chi_{\Sigma}}}\widehat{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}.

We can now prove
Proposition

6.23
(ii).

Proof of
Proposition

6.23

(ii)
.

Assume that
\Sigma=S
.
By the previous paragraph we may assume that
\Lambda
acts trivially on
\sigma
.
Let
\Omega\subseteq\widehat{M_{0}},\Theta\subseteq\widehat{\Lambda}
, and
R=\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta)
.
By
Proposition

6.6
,

\displaystyle\text{Tor}^{\mathcal{H}_{S,AM_{0},R}^{-}}_{i}(R(\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta}),H_{j}(K^{p}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta},U,*}))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}R(\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta})\otimes_{\mathcal{H}_{S,AM_{0},\mathcal{O}(\Omega)}^{-}}H_{j}(K^{p}J,\mathcal{A}^{\Sigma}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*})&\text{ if }i=0,\\
0&\text{ if }i\neq 0.\end{cases}

Thus, the spectral sequence in
Corollary

5.9
collapses at the
E^{2}
page.
As commented upon when proving part (i), this implies the result.
∎

Putting everything together, we have proven the following.

Theorem 6.29
.

Let
\mathscr{X}_{AM_{0},\sigma,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
be the eigenvariety constructed in
Section

6.3
using
S
-arithmetic cohomology and
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
the eigenvariety constructed in this section using overconvergent cohomology.
The underlying reduced varieties are isomorphic and, if
\Sigma=S
, then the varieties themselves are isomorphic.

6.5.
Some properties of eigenvarieties

Throughout this section, we assume that
\Lambda
acts on
\sigma
by a character
\chi
.
Fix
a\in\Lambda_{\Sigma}^{--}
as in
Section

6.4
.

Corollary 6.30
.

The variety
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
admits an admissible covering by open affinoids that are finite over their image in
\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}
. In particular,
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
and
\mathscr{Y}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
(when defined) have dimension at most
\dim(\mathbf{Z}_{\Sigma})
, where
\mathbf{Z}_{\Sigma}
is the center of
\mathbf{M}_{\Sigma}
.

Proof.
The statement on admissible covers follows from
Proposition

6.20
,
Proposition

6.21
,
Equation

6.28
, and the corresponding fact for
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
, where such an admissible cover is given by the preimages of the
\mathscr{Z}_{\Omega,h}
.
Thus,
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
and
\mathscr{Y}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
have dimension bounded by that of
\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}
, which is
\dim(\mathbf{Z}_{\Sigma})
by
Proposition

6.14
.
∎

Proposition 6.31
.

Let
\delta
be a point of
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. Then, the fiber of
\delta
on
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
is natural bijection with systems of Hecke eigenvalues for the action of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
on
S
-arithmetic homology
H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd(U,V\otimes\delta))_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
(or on
H^{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd(U,V\otimes\delta)^{\prime})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
).

Proof.
The point
\delta
is defined over some finite extension
E^{\prime}
of
E
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, and by base change we may assume that
E^{\prime}=E
.
We will also write
\delta
for the restriction of
\delta
to
A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}
, i.e. for the image of
\delta
under the map
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}\longrightarrow\widehat{A_{\Sigma}M_{\Sigma,0}}
. Let
a\in A_{\Sigma}^{--}
.
Choose an affinoid open
\Omega\subseteq\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}
containing the image of
\delta
in
\widehat{M_{\Sigma,0}}
and
h\geq v_{p}(\chi\delta(a))
such that
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
admits a slope-
\leq h
decomposition with respect to
U_{a}
.
If we let
\mathcal{U}
be the preimage in
\widehat{M_{\Sigma}}
of
\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})
for some
\mathbf{h}
as in the previous section, then
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\mathcal{U}},U,*}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})
admits a slope decomposition.
Set
R=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{U})
and
\Theta=\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})
.
By
Lemma

5.10
(and the paragraph above it) and
Lemma

5.11
, there are
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
-equivariant quasi-isomorphisms

\displaystyle C_{\bullet}(K^{S},\SigmaInd(U,V_{\Omega\times\Theta}))\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{R}E

\displaystyle\simeq R(\chi\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S,R}}C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta},U,*}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})_{\leq h}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{R}E

\displaystyle\simeq R(\chi\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S,R}}C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma\otimes\delta,U,*}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})_{\leq h}

\displaystyle\simeq E(\chi\delta)\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S,E}}C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma\otimes\delta,U,*}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})_{\leq h}

\displaystyle\simeq C_{\bullet}(K^{S},\SigmaInd(U,V\otimes\delta)),

where the map
R\longrightarrow E
corresponds to the point
\delta
in
\Omega\times\Theta
.
Here, the second line follows from
Proposition

5.7
(iii) and the fact that
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Omega\times\Theta},U,*}^{s-{\Sigma-\text{an}}})_{\leq h}
is a projective
R
-module,
and the third line follows from the fact that we can find a resolution
P_{\bullet}
of
