R(\chi\delta_{\Omega\times\Theta})
by projective
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{S,R}
-modules such that
P_{\bullet}\otimes_{R}E
is a resolution of
E(\chi\delta)
(this follows by twisting from the corresponding result for the trivial character, where it is true since such a resolution can be defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
).
Thus, we obtain a spectral sequence relating the
S
-arithmetic homologies of
\SigmaInd(U,V_{\Omega\times\Theta})
and of
\SigmaInd(U,V\otimes\delta)
, and the same analysis as in
[
Han17
, Theorem 4.3.3]
gives the result.
∎

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{\Sigma},\lambda^{\Sigma})
be a dominant regular weight of
\mathbf{M}_{\Sigma,E}\times\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{S}_{p}\smallsetminus\Sigma,E}
.
We also assume that
U=V_{G_{\mathcal{S}_{p}\smallsetminus\Sigma}}(\lambda^{\Sigma})
and
\sigma_{\Sigma}=\sigma_{\Sigma,\text{sm}}\otimes_{E}V_{M_{\Sigma}}(\lambda_{\Sigma})^{\prime}
, where
\sigma_{\Sigma,\text{sm}}
is smooth. Let us set
V_{\text{sm}}=\cInd_{\mathpzc{M}}^{M}(\sigma_{\Sigma,\text{sm}}\otimes\sigma^{\Sigma})
.

Definition 6.32
.

Let
x
be a point of
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
.

(i)

We say that
x
has locally algebraic (resp. dominant regular) weight if its image in
\widehat{M}
is a locally algebraic character
\delta=\delta_{\text{sm}}\delta_{\text{alg}}
(resp. if moreover
\lambda_{\Sigma}\delta_{\text{alg}}
is a dominant regular of
\mathbf{G}_{\Sigma}
).

(ii)

We say that
x
is
classical
if it has dominant regular weight
\delta=\delta_{\text{sm}}\delta_{\text{alg}}
and the localisation of
H_{*}(K^{S},V_{G_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}}(\lambda\delta_{\text{alg}})^{\prime}\otimes_{E}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V_{\text{sm}}\otimes\delta_{\text{sm}})^{\text{sm}})
at the maximal ideal of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
corresponding to
x
is non-zero.

(iii)

We say that
x
is
non-critical
if it is classical of weight
\delta=\delta_{\text{sm}}\delta_{\text{alg}}
and the natural homomorphism

H_{*}(K^{S},V_{G_{\mathcal{S}_{p}}}(\lambda\delta_{\text{alg}})^{\prime}\otimes_{E}\Ind_{P}^{G}(V_{\text{sm}}\otimes\delta_{\text{sm}})^{\text{sm}})\longrightarrow H_{*}(K^{S},\SigmaInd_{P}^{G}(U,V\otimes\delta))

is an isomorphism after localisation at the maximal ideal above.

We can make analogous definitions for points of
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
.

Theorem

5.26
gives a numerical criterion on a locally algebraic character
\delta
for its fiber in
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
to consist of non-critical classical points.
We will call the points satisfying this criterion
numerically non-critical
.

Proposition 6.33
.

Assume that
G_{\infty}
is compact,
\Sigma=S
and that for all
v\in\Sigma
,
\Res_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbf{G}_{v}
is the restriction of scalars of a
split
reductive group over a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Then, the set of (numerically) non-critical classical points is a Zariski dense in
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
and
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
and accumulates at points of locally algebraic weight.
Moreover,
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
and
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
are equidimensional of dimension
\dim(\mathbf{Z})
, where
\mathbf{Z}
is the center of
\mathbf{M}
.

Proof.
This follows a standard argument.
Since
G_{\infty}
is compact, the topological space
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\infty}\times\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{F})/\mathcal{K}^{S}J
is a disjoint union of points and the complexes
C_{\bullet}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma\otimes\delta,U,*}^{s-\text{an}})
are concentrated in degree 0 for any continuous character
\delta
of
M_{0}
.
Let
\Omega\subseteq\widehat{M_{0}}
be an irreducible admissible affinoid open subspace and
h\geq 0
such that
C_{0}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}^{s-\text{an}})
admits a slope-
\leq h
decomposition.
Then
H_{0}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}^{s-\text{an}})_{\leq h}
is a finite projective
\mathcal{O}(\Omega)
-module.
By
[
Che04
, Lemme 6.2.10]
, the map
\text{Sp}(\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h})\longrightarrow\Omega
is surjective after restricting it to any irreducible component.
Thus, all irreducible components of
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
have the same dimension as
\widehat{M_{0}}
, that is
\dim(\mathbf{Z})
.
If
\mathcal{U}
is the preimage of
\Omega\times m_{\chi_{\Sigma}}^{-1}\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h})
on
\widehat{M}
(cf.
Equation

6.28
), then by
Proposition

6.20
,
Proposition

6.21
,
Lemma

6.24
and their proofs, the value at
\mathcal{U}
of coherent sheaf
\mathscr{H}
on
\widehat{M}
is a direct summand of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{U})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\Omega\times\Theta(\Omega,\mathbf{h}))}H_{0}(K^{S}J,\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{\Omega},U,*}^{s-\text{an}})_{\leq h}
(with a twisted action of
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
). In particular, the same analysis applies to
\mathscr{X}_{V,U}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
instead of
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
.

For the Zariski density and accumulation statement, it’s enough to show that every irreducible component has a point of locally algebraic weight, and any such point has arbitrarily small affinoid neighbourhoods where non-critical classical points are dense.
For simplicity, let us focus only on the case of
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
.
The previous paragraph implies that the morphism
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\mathscr{Z}
sends irreducible components to irreducible components.
Since the image of an irreducible component of
\mathscr{Z}
in
\widehat{M_{0}}
is a Zariski-open subset, the same is true for the images of irreducible components of
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
.
Fix a connected component
C
of
\widehat{M_{0}}
and a smooth character
\phi
lying on it.
By
Proposition

6.15
, the product of
\phi
and dominant regular weights in
C
are Zariski dense and accumulation.
In particular, their intersection with the image of an irreducible component of
\mathscr{X}_{\sigma|_{M_{0}},U,*}^{\Sigma}(K^{S})
lying above
C
is nonempty.
Therefore, every such irreducible component contains a point with locally algebraic weight.
Now, let
x
be such a point and
\delta=\delta_{\text{sm}}\delta_{\text{alg}}
its image in
\widehat{M_{0}}
.
Fix
h\geq 0
and
\Omega\subseteq\widehat{M_{0}}
a connected affinoid open such that
x
lies in
\text{Sp}(\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h})
.
By
Proposition

6.15
again, the weights of the form
\delta_{\text{sm}}\psi
with
\psi\lambda_{\Sigma}
dominant regular and
v_{p}((w\cdot\psi\lambda_{\Sigma})(a))-v_{p}((\psi\lambda_{\Sigma})(a))>h
are Zariski dense in
\Omega
and accumulate at
\delta
.
Their preimages in
\text{Sp}(\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h})
are thus also Zariski dense and accumulate at
x
by finiteness of the morphism
\text{Sp}(\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,h})\longrightarrow\Omega
and
[
Che04
, Lemme 6.2.8]
.
The condition that
v_{p}((w\cdot\psi\lambda_{\Sigma})(a))-v_{p}((\psi\lambda_{\Sigma})(a))>h
implies that these points are numerically non-critical.
∎

6.6.
Comparison with the work of Breuil–Ding

We will now compare our construction of eigenvarieties with that of
[
BD21
]
.
We need to make additional assumptions to place ourselves in the setting of
loc. cit.

Assume that
F
is a totally real number field and
\widetilde{F}/F
is a totally imaginary quadratic extension of
F
over which all primes in
\mathcal{S}_{p}
split.
We will assume that
\mathbf{G}
is a unitary group over
F
that becomes isomorphic to
\text{GL}_{n}
after base change to
\widetilde{F}
and that
G_{\infty}
is compact.
For each
v\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
, fix a prime
\widetilde{v}
in
\widetilde{F}
dividing it, as well as an isomorphism
\mathbf{G}_{F_{v}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbf{G}_{{\widetilde{F}}_{\widetilde{v}}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\text{GL}_{n/{\widetilde{F}}_{\widetilde{v}}}
.
We will use these isomorphisms to identify
\mathbf{G}_{F_{v}}
and
\text{GL}_{n/{\widetilde{F}}_{\widetilde{v}}}
, and we will do so without comment.
Assume also that
S=\Sigma=\mathcal{S}_{p}
, and that for each
v\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
the subgroups
\mathbf{T}_{v}
and
\mathbf{S}_{v}
correspond to the subgroups of diagonal matrices, and that the Borel subgroup
\mathbf{B}_{p,E}
is of the form
E\times_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\Res_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbf{B}_{v}
, where
\mathbf{B}_{v}
corresponds to the subgroup of upper-triangular matrices.
The parabolics
\mathbf{P}_{v}
contain
\mathbf{B}_{v}
, so
\mathbf{M}_{v}
corresponds to a subgroup of block-triangular matrices, with block sizes
n_{v,1},...,n_{v,r_{v}}
, say.
Moreover,
\mathbf{A}_{v}
is the full center of
\mathbf{M}_{v}
, consisting of diagonal matrices with identical entries in each block.
Fix a uniformiser
\varpi_{v}
for all
v\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
, and let
\Lambda_{v}
consist of diagonal matrices with entries

(\overbrace{\varpi_{v}^{e_{1}},...,\varpi_{v}^{e_{1}}}^{n_{v,1}},...,\overbrace{\varpi_{v}^{e_{r_{v}}},...,\varpi_{v}^{e_{r_{v}}}}^{n_{v,r_{v}}}).

The assumption that
G_{\infty}
is compact implies that arithmetic (co)homology occurs only in degree 0, and similarly for completed cohomology (cf.
Section

5.10
).
Moreover, the systems of Hecke eigenvalues in the
p
-arithmetic (co)homology groups considered in
Section

5
occur all in degree 0, as they are in bijection with systems of Hecke eigenvalues in the corresponding arithmetic (co)homology groups, which will already be detected by the zeroth Tor and Ext groups over the Hecke algebra at
p
.
Thus, we may (and will) always focus our discussion to (co)homology in degree 0, where we can apply
Proposition

5.32
.

Let us recall the construction of eigenvarieties in
[
BD21
]
.
Assume that
E
contains the
m
-th roots of unity in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
for any
m\leq n
.
Fix a cuspidal Bernstein component
\Omega
for
\mathbf{M}
(defined over
E
) and a dominant weight
\lambda
for
\mathbf{M}
. Let
\pi
be a point of
\Omega
, corresponding to a supercuspidal representation of
\mathbf{M}
over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
that we also denote by
\pi
, and write
\pi=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\otimes_{E}\cInd_{AM_{0}}^{M}(\sigma)
for some representation
\sigma
of
AM_{0}
for
M_{0}:=\prod_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{p}}\text{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{v}})
.
For any admissible locally analytic representation
W
of
G
over
E
, set

\displaystyle J_{P}(W)_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\text{Hom}_{\mathfrak{m}_{E}^{\text{der}}}(V_{M}(\lambda),J_{P}(W)),

\displaystyle B_{\Omega,\lambda}(W)

\displaystyle:=\text{Hom}_{M_{0}}(\sigma,J_{P}(W)_{\lambda}\cotimes_{E}\mathcal{C}^{\text{la}}(A_{0},E)),

where
\mathfrak{m}^{\text{der}}_{E}
is the Lie
E
-algebra of
\mathbf{M}^{\text{der}}
and
J_{P}(W)
denotes Emerton’s locally analytic Jacquet functor.
The representation
B_{\Omega,\lambda}(W)
is endowed with an action of
A=\Lambda\times A_{0}
by letting
\Lambda
act on
J_{P}(W)_{\lambda}
and
A_{0}
act on
\mathcal{C}^{\text{la}}(A_{0},E)
, and these actions make
B_{\Omega,\lambda}(W)
an essentially admissible locally analytic representation of
A
.
In particular, it gives rise to a coherent sheaf
\mathcal{H}
on
\widehat{A}
endowed with an action of
\mathbb{T}^{p}(K^{p})
.
When
W=\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})
, we will write
\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
for its relative spectrum.
In fact, the action of
\Lambda
factors through the action of the Bernstein spectrum
\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega}=\text{End}_{M}(\cInd_{M_{0}}^{M}(\sigma))
on the first argument of

\text{Hom}_{M}(\cInd_{M_{0}}^{M}(\sigma),J_{P}(W)_{\lambda}\cotimes_{E}\mathcal{C}^{\text{la}}(A_{0},E))\simeq B_{\Omega,\lambda}(W).

If
\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\text{un}}
denotes the group of unramified characters
\delta
of
A
such that
\pi\otimes(\delta\circ\det)\simeq\pi
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega}\simeq E[\Lambda]^{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\text{un}}}
, and so
(\Spec\mathcal{Z}_{\Omega})^{\text{an}}\simeq\widehat{\Lambda}/\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\text{un}}
.
Hence, the coherent sheaf on
\widehat{A}
associated to
B_{\Omega,\lambda}(W)
in fact descends to one on
\widehat{A}/\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\text{un}}
.
When
W=\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})
, we call the resulting relative spectrum of the Hecke algebra
\mathcal{E}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
.

Note that the block-wise determinant map
\det_{M}\colon M\longrightarrow A
sending a block-diagonal matrices
(m_{v,i})_{v,i}
to the diagonal matrix with entries

(\overbrace{\det(m_{v,1}),...,\det(m_{v,1})}^{n_{v,1}},...,\overbrace{\det(m_{v,r_{v}}),...,\det(m_{v,r_{v}})}^{n_{v,r_{v}}})_{v}

induces isomorphisms
\widehat{A}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\widehat{M},\widehat{A_{0}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\widehat{M_{0}}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}(\widehat{M})^{\text{un}}
, where
(\widehat{M})^{\text{un}}\subseteq\widehat{M}
denotes the (closed) subspace of unramified characters.
Moreover, the subgroup
\mathcal{T}_{M}^{\text{un}}
of
\mathcal{T}_{M}
(cf.
Lemma

6.18
) consisting of unramified characters is isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\text{un}}
in the same way. In particular, we have morphisms
\widehat{A}/\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\text{un}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\widehat{M}/\mathcal{T}_{M}^{\text{un}}\longrightarrow\widehat{M}/\mathcal{T}_{M}
.
Write
q
for the composition of these morphisms and the multiplication by
\delta_{P}^{-1}
morphism
\widehat{M}/\mathcal{T}_{M}\longrightarrow\widehat{M}/\mathcal{T}_{M}
.

Let
V_{\text{sm}}=\cInd_{M_{0}}^{M}(\sigma)
and
V=V_{M}(\lambda)\otimes_{E}V_{\text{sm}}
, and let
U=1
be the trivial representation.
To make comparisons with
[
BD21
]
easier, we will slightly modify our conventions.
Namely, when taking locally analytic inductions, we will induce from the opposite parabolic
\overline{P}
instead of
P
.
As a consequence of this change, we will sometimes have to replace the simple positive roots
\Delta_{E}
of
E\times_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\Res_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbf{G}
by their negations
\overline{\Delta}_{E}
, and
\Lambda^{-}
by
\Lambda^{+}
.
We will also denote by
\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})
and
\overline{\mathscr{Y}}_{V}(K^{p})
the analogues of
\mathscr{X}_{V,1}^{\mathcal{S}_{p}}(K^{p})
and
\mathscr{Y}_{V,1}^{\mathcal{S}_{p}}(K^{p})
respectively.
Note that the algebraic part of
V
,
V_{M}(\lambda)
is the dual of the irreducible algebraic representation of
M
of highest weight
-\lambda

with respect to the opposite Borel

\overline{\mathbf{B}}
.

Before stating and proving the main result of the section comparing the two constructions, let us sketch roughly how the comparison goes.
Recall from
[
BD21
, Proposition 3.2.2]
that a
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-point
x
of
\mathcal{E}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
corresponds to a triple
(\eta_{x},\pi_{x},\chi_{x})
where
\eta_{x}\colon\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is a system of Hecke eigenvalues,
\pi_{x}
is a supercuspidal representation of
M
in the Bernstein component
\Omega
and
\chi_{x}\colon A_{0}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
is a continuous character.
On the other hand,
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-points
y
of
\overline{\mathscr{Y}}_{V}(K^{p})
correspond to
(\nu_{y},\delta_{y})
where
\nu_{y}\colon\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is a system of eigenvalues and
\delta_{y}
is an equivalence class of characters of
M
modulo those characters which twist
\pi=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\otimes V_{\text{sm}}
into itself.
Given such a point
x
, we may write
\pi_{x}=\pi\otimes\psi
for some unramified character
\psi
of
M
, and the point
x
will then correspond to the point
y
with
\nu_{y}=\eta_{x}
and
\delta_{y}=\psi(\chi_{x}\circ\det_{M})\delta_{P}^{-1}
.
The only subtlety in making this precise is how one reconciles the fact that one construction relies Emerton’s Jacquet functor whereas the other relies on parabolic induction, and these are not adjoint in general.
However, the adjunction theorem of Bergdall–Chojecki
[
BC18
, Theorem A]
can be applied at numerically non-critical points, and using their Zariski density one can extend the comparison to all points by an interpolation argument.

Theorem 6.34
.

There is an isomorphism of reduced varieties

\mathcal{E}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})^{\text{red}}\simeq\overline{\mathscr{Y}}_{V}(K^{p})^{\text{red}}\times_{\widehat{M}/\mathcal{T}_{M},q}(\widehat{A}/\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\text{un}})

(here, the superscript
red
is used to denote the underlying reduced varieties).

Proof.
Let us write
\delta_{P}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
for the variety
\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})\times_{\widehat{M},\delta_{P}}\widehat{M}
over
\widehat{M}
(of course, this is isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
but not as varieties over
\widehat{M}
).
We will start by showing that the images of
\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})
and
\delta_{P}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
in
\widehat{M}
have the same underlying analytic subset.
For this, we will use that numerically non-critical points are Zariski-dense in
\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})
.
Let us unwind the definition of numerical non-criticality,
Definition

5.23
, in this case.
Write
\omega
for the central character of
V_{\text{sm}}
, so that the central character of
V\otimes\delta
is
\chi_{\delta}:=\omega\lambda\delta
for any locally algebraic character
\delta=\delta_{\text{sm}}\delta_{\text{alg}}\colon M\longrightarrow E^{\times}
.
Write
V_{\text{sm}}=\bigboxtimes_{v,i}V_{\text{sm},v,i}
according to the decomposition
M\simeq\prod_{v,i}\text{GL}_{n_{i}}(F_{i})
and similarly for
V
, and write
\omega=(\omega_{v,i})_{v,i}
(with each
\omega_{v,i}
a character of
F_{v}^{\times}
) and
\delta=(\delta_{v,i})_{v,i}
accordingly.
We will also write
\mu_{v,i}=(\mu_{v,\tau,i})_{\tau}
for the weights of
\delta_{v,i}
.
As
\delta
factors through the block-wise determinant
M\subseteq A
, we will sometimes consider each
\delta_{v,i}
as a character on
F_{v}^{\times}
.
Write also
\lambda=(\lambda_{v,\tau,j})_{v,\tau,j}
, where
\tau
runs through the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra embeddings of
F_{v}
into
E
and
1\leq j\leq n
, to mean
\lambda(\mathbf{t})=(\prod_{j=1}^{n}t_{v,\tau,j}^{\lambda_{v,\tau,j}})_{v,\tau}
for
\mathbf{t}=(t_{v,\tau,j})_{v,\tau,j}\in\mathbf{T}(E)\simeq\prod_{v,\tau,j}E^{\times}
.
For such
\tau
and
v
, set
s_{v,i}=n_{v,1}+\cdots+n_{v,i}
and
\alpha_{v,\tau,j}
for the simple negative root of
\mathbf{M}_{v}
given by
(t_{v,\tau,k})_{v,\tau,k}\longmapsto t_{v,\tau,j}^{-1}t_{v,\tau,j+1}
.
Then
\overline{\Delta}_{E}\smallsetminus\overline{\Delta}_{M,E}=\bigcup_{v,\tau}\{\alpha_{v,\tau,s_{v,1}},...,\alpha_{v,\tau,s_{v,r_{v}-1}}\}
.
The condition
Equation

5.24
from
Definition

5.23
applied to
V\otimes\delta
, the root
\alpha_{v,\tau,s_{v,k}}
, and the diagonal element
a_{v,k}\in\Lambda_{v}^{+}
with entries

(\overbrace{\varpi_{v},...,\varpi_{v}}^{s_{v,k}},1,...,1)

asks that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{p}(\omega_{v,i}(\varpi_{v})\delta_{\text{sm},v,i}(\varpi_{v}))<\frac{1}{[F_{v}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]}\Bigg(\Bigg.

\displaystyle-\left(\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}\colon F_{v}\xhookrightarrow{}E}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{s_{v,k}}\lambda_{v,\widetilde{\tau},j}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{j}\mu_{v,\widetilde{\tau},i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+(\lambda_{v,\tau,s_{v,k}}+\mu_{v,\tau,k}-\lambda_{v,\tau,s_{v,k}+1}-\mu_{v,\tau,k+1}+1)\Bigg.\Bigg)

By
Proposition

6.33
, the analytic subset underlying the image of
\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})
in
\widehat{M}
is the Zariski closure of the locally algebraic characters
\delta
such that
-\lambda\delta_{\text{alg}}
is dominant with respect to
\overline{\mathbf{B}}
and that satisfy this equation.
But this exactly the numerical classicality condition from
[
BD21
, Proposition 3.2.9]
for the point corresponding to the character
\delta_{P}\delta
:
note that we are using different normalisations for the valuations and that,
writing
\delta_{?}=\delta_{?,\text{un}}\delta_{?,\text{ram}}
, where
?\in\{\emptyset,\text{sm},\text{alg}\}
,
\delta_{?,\text{un}}
is trivial on
A_{0}
and
\delta_{?,\text{ram}}
is trivial on
\Lambda
, the representation
\pi_{x}
in
loc. cit.
(associated to the image of
x
in
\widehat{M}
) is
V_{\text{sm}}\otimes\delta_{\text{un}}\simeq(V_{\text{sm}}\otimes\delta_{\text{sm},\text{un}})\otimes\delta_{\text{alg},\text{un}}
, and hence the restriction of its central character to
\Lambda
is the same as that of
(V_{\text{sm}}\otimes\delta_{\text{sm}})\otimes\delta_{\text{alg}}
.
Using the density of numerically non-critical points in the image of
\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})
in
\widehat{M}
, and the analogous result
[
BD21
, Theorem 3.2.11]
for
\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
, our claim follows.

In particular, the underlying analytic subsets of the images of
\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})
and
\delta_{P}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
in
\widehat{M_{0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
under the composition
r
of the maps
\widehat{M}\longrightarrow\widehat{M_{0}}\times\widehat{\Lambda}\longrightarrow\widehat{M_{0}}\times\mathbb{G}_{\text{m}}
, where the last map is given by restriction to the subgroup generated by some
a\in\Lambda^{--}
, also agree, and by
Proposition

6.26
is contained in the Fredholm hypersurface
\mathscr{Z}
from
Section

6.4
.
To finish the proof of the theorem, we will use the interpolation theorem
[
Han17
, Theorem 5.1.6]
.
In the notation of
[
Han17
]
,
\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})
is the eigenvariety associated to the eigenvariety datum
(\widehat{M_{0}},\mathscr{Z},r_{*}\overline{\mathscr{H}},\mathbb{T}(K^{p}),\psi_{1})
, where
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
is the analogue for
\overline{P}
of
\mathscr{H}
and
\psi_{1}
is the natural morphism
\psi_{1}\colon\mathbb{T}(K^{p})\longrightarrow\text{End}_{\mathscr{Z}}(r_{*}\mathscr{H})
, and
\delta_{P}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})
is the eigenvariety associated to the eigenvariety data
(\widehat{M_{0}},\mathscr{Z},r_{*}(\delta_{P})^{*}\mathcal{H},\mathbb{T}(K^{p}),

\psi_{2})
for
\psi_{2}
as
\psi_{1}
.
Thus, in order to apply
[
Han17
, Theorem 5.1.6]
to obtain mutually inverse morphisms

\overline{\mathscr{X}}_{V}(K^{p})^{\text{red}}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}_{\Omega,\lambda}(K^{p})^{\text{red}}\times_{\widehat{M},\delta_{P}}\widehat{M}

it’s enough to show that for all numerically non-critical characters
\delta
, the fibers of
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
and
(\delta_{P})^{*}\mathcal{H}
at

\delta
are isomorphic as
\mathbb{T}^{S}(K^{S})
-modules.
Once this is shown, the theorem will follow by taking quotients.
According to
[
BD21
, Proposition 3.1.8]
, the fiber of
(\delta_{P})^{*}\mathcal{H}
at
\delta
is dual to
\text{Hom}_{M}(V\otimes\delta_{P}\delta,J_{P}(\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})^{\text{la}}))
.
On the other hand, the fiber of
\overline{\mathscr{H}}
on
\delta
is isomorphic to
H_{0}(K^{p},\Ind_{\overline{P}}^{G}(V\otimes\delta)^{\text{la}})
(this can be deduced from the proof of
Proposition

6.23
(ii)),
which by
Proposition

5.32
is dual to
\text{Hom}_{G}(\Ind_{\overline{P}}^{G}(V\otimes\delta)^{\text{la}},\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})^{\text{la}})
.

Thus, it’s enough to check that for numerically non-critical
\delta
, there is a Hecke equivariant isomorphism

\text{Hom}_{G}(\Ind_{\overline{P}}^{G}(V\otimes\delta)^{\text{la}},\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})^{\text{la}})\longrightarrow\text{Hom}_{M}(V\otimes\delta_{P}\delta,J_{P}(\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})^{\text{la}})).

This will follow from Bergdall–Chojecki’s adjunction theorem
[
BC18
, Theorem A]
(or rather, the analogous result products of groups of the form considered in
[
BC18
]
) once we check that its assumptions are satisfied.
We need to check that
\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})^{\text{la}}
is f-
\mathfrak{p}
-acyclic (cf.
[
BC18
, Definition 4.1]
), which follows from
[
BC18
, Example 4.2]
, and the pair
(V_{M}(\lambda\delta_{\text{alg}}),V_{\text{sm}}\otimes\delta_{P}\delta_{\text{sm}})
is non-critical with respect to
\widetilde{H}^{0}(K^{p})^{\text{la}}
(cf.
[
BC18
, Definition 4.4]
).
As explained in
[
BC18
, Remark 4.6]
, it’s enough to show that
\omega\delta_{P}\delta\lambda
is of non-critical slope in the sense of
[
Eme06a
, Definition 4.4.3]
.
This is equivalent to
\delta
being numerically non-critical.
∎

Appendix A
Orlik–Strauch functors

In this appendix we will define and study the basic properties of the Orlik–Strauch functors
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}
for reductive groups over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
that are not necessarily the restriction of scalars of a split reductive group over a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Let
\mathbf{G}
be a reductive group over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mathbf{P}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-parabolic, and let
\mathbf{N}
be its unipotent radical,
\mathbf{M}
a Levi subgroup and
\overline{\mathbf{N}}
the unipotent radical of the opposite parabolic.
Choose a maximal torus
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{M}
defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and a finite extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
containing a Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
over which
\mathbf{T}
splits.
We will write
\mathbf{G}_{E}=E\times_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbf{G}
,
G=\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
,
G_{E}=\mathbf{G}_{E}(E)
,
\mathfrak{g}
for the Lie
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra of
\mathbf{G}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{E}
for the Lie
E
-algebra of
\mathbf{G}_{E}
, and
U(\mathfrak{g}),U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})
for their universal enveloping algebras, and similarly for
\mathbf{P}_{E}
,
P
, etc.
We will also fix a special point in the Bruhat–Tits building of
G
that lies in the image of the building of
M
, and let
J
be its stabiliser in
G
.
It is also a special point for
M
, and we will use some of the groups from
Section

2
for the facets
\mathscr{F}_{M}\supseteq\mathscr{F}
for
M
and
G
in some fixed apartment for
M
that contain it.
Note that
J
is the pointwise stabiliser of
\mathscr{F}
in
G
and
M_{0}=J\cap M
is the pointwise stabiliser of
\mathscr{F}_{M}
in
M
.
More concretely, we will consider
\overline{N}_{s}
and the analogue
\overline{J}_{0,s}
of
J_{0,s}
defined by swapping
N
and
\overline{N}
in the definition of the latter, and also the corresponding subgroups
\overline{N}_{E,s}:=\overline{N}^{\prime}_{s},\overline{J}_{E,0,s}:=\overline{J}^{\prime}_{0,s}
of
G_{E}
(defined as in
Section

2.4
with
L^{\prime}=E
), as well as their rigid-analytic variants.
We will follow the arguments in
[
OS14
]
very closely (as all of the arguments generalise), but will not discuss the irreducibility of representations in the image of the functor
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}
.

Recall that the category
\mathcal{O}^{\mathfrak{p}_{E}}_{\text{alg}}
is the full subcategory of the category of representations
X
of
\mathfrak{g}_{E}
over
E
such that

(i)

X
is finitely generated over
U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})
,

(ii)

X
is a direct sum of finite-dimensional irreducible
U(\mathfrak{m}_{E})
-modules that are obtained from finite-dimensional irreducible representations of
M_{E}
,

(iii)

the action of
U(\mathfrak{p}_{E})
is locally finite (i.e. the orbits of this action are finite-dimensional).

Let
X\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathfrak{p}_{E}}_{\text{alg}}
and let
V
be an admissible smooth representation of
M
. Choose a finite-dimensional
\mathfrak{p}_{E}
-subrepresentation
W
that generates
X
as a
U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})
-module and let
\mathfrak{d}
be the kernel of the natural surjection
U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{p}_{E})}W\longrightarrow X
.
By
[
OS14
, Lemma 3.2]
,
W
extends to an
E
-linear (algebraic) representation of
P_{E}
, and we may restrict it to a representation of
P\subseteq P_{E}
.
Let
D(G,E)
(resp.
D(P,E)
) be the locally analytic distribution algebra of
G
(resp.
P
) over
E
.
Recall that there is a natural injective algebra homomorphism
U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})\xhookrightarrow{}D(G,E)
.
The pairing

\displaystyle(D(G,E)\otimes_{D(P,E)}W)\times\Ind_{P}^{G}(W^{\prime}\otimes_{E}V)^{\text{la}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\text{la}}(G,V)

\displaystyle(\delta\otimes w,f)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\delta\otimes w)\cdot f\colon g\longmapsto\delta(h\longmapsto f(gh)(w))

gives rise to a pairing
(U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{p}_{E})}W)\times\Ind_{P}^{G}(W^{\prime}\otimes_{E}V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\text{la}}(G,V)
.
Define

\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,W,V):=\Ind_{P}^{G}(W^{\prime}\otimes_{E}V)^{\mathfrak{d}}:=\{f\in\Ind_{P}^{G}(W^{\prime}\otimes_{E}V)^{\text{la}}\colon\delta\cdot f=0\text{ for all }\delta\in\mathfrak{d}\}.

Lemma A.1
.

Assume that
V=E
is the trivial representation.
Then, the dual of
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,W,E)
is isomorphic to the following:

(i)

(D(G,E)\otimes_{D(P,E)}W)/D(G,E)\mathfrak{d}
as a
D(G,E)
-module, and
D(G,E)\mathfrak{d}
is the submodule of elements
D(G,E)\otimes_{D(P,E)}W
of that annihilate
\Ind_{P}^{G}(W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}
under the pairing
\cdot
.

(ii)

D(G,E)\otimes_{D(\mathfrak{g}_{E},P,E)}X
as a
D(G,E)
-module, where
D(\mathfrak{g}_{E},P,E)=U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})D(P,E)
is the subalgebra of
D(G,E)
generated by
U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})
and
D(P,E)
.

(iii)

\varprojlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}((U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})_{n}\otimes_{E}W)/\mathfrak{d}_{n})
as a
D(J,E)
-module, where
\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}
denotes ordinary induction,
U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})_{n}
is the completion of
U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})
with respect to the norm
|{-}|_{n}
described below
and
\mathfrak{d}_{n}
is the closure of
\mathfrak{d}\subseteq U(\mathfrak{g}_{E})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{p}_{E})}W\simeq U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})\otimes_{E}W
in
U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})_{n}\otimes_{E}W
.

Proof.
Statement (i) follows from the same arguments as
[
OS14
, Proposition 3.3 (ii)]
and similarly for (ii) and
[
OS14
, §3.4]
.
Let us prove (iii) in more detail.
The proof is essentially the same as that in
[
OS14
, §3.8]
except that the groups
U_{P}^{-,n}
and
P^{n}
in
loc. cit.
must be replaced by
\overline{N}_{n}
and
\overline{J}_{0,n}
(or their analogues over
E
).
The Iwasawa decomposition
G=JP
shows that
\Ind_{P}^{G}(W^{\prime})^{\text{la}}\simeq\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(W^{\prime})^{\text{la}}
, and we may define a subspace
\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}
of the latter as above, which is therefore isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,W,E)
as a
D(J,E)
-module.
We see as in
[
OS14
, Lemma 3.9]
that

\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(W^{\prime})^{\text{la}}\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}(\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}W^{\prime})\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}(\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,s})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime}).

The algebra
U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})
acts on
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})
by taking derivatives as usual, and hence also on
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,s})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime}
, so it makes sense to define

(\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,s})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}
.
There are isomorphisms

\Ind_{J\cap P}^{J}(W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}(\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{s})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}(\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,s})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}.

Choose coordinates
X_{1},...,X_{r}
of the root subgroups of
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E}
that identify
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n}
with the closed ball of radius
p^{-n}
centered at the origin. This is possible, as if this holds for one
n
then it holds for all.
Let
\overline{n}_{1},...,\overline{n}_{r}
be the basis of
\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E}
corresponding to this choice, so that
\overline{n}_{i}
can be seen as taking derivatives in the direction of
X_{i}
, i.e.
\overline{n}_{i}X_{j}
is 1 if
i=j
and 0 otherwise.
Then, the norm
|{-}|_{n}
on
U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})
is defined by

\left|\sum_{(i_{1},...,i_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{r}}a_{i_{1},...,i_{r}}\overline{n}_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots\overline{n}_{r}^{i_{r}}\right|_{n}:=\sup_{(i_{1},...,i_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{r}}|(i_{1})!\cdots(i_{r})!a_{i_{1},...,i_{r}}|p^{-n(i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r})}.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{b}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})
denote the ring of bounded functions on
\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n}
,

\mathcal{O}_{b}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})=\left\{f=\sum_{(i_{1},...,i_{r})}a_{i_{1},...,i_{r}}X_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots X_{r}^{i_{r}}:|f|:=\sup_{(i_{1},...,i_{r})}|a_{i_{1},...,i_{r}}|p^{-n(i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r})}<\infty\right\},

which is a Banach space with norm
f\longmapsto|f|
.
The pairing
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E})\times U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})\longrightarrow E
given by
(f,\mathfrak{r})\longmapsto(\mathfrak{r}\cdot f)(1)
extends to a pairing
\mathcal{O}_{b}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})\times U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})_{n}\longrightarrow E
that realises
\mathcal{O}_{b}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})
as the continuous dual of
U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})_{n}
, and similarly
\mathcal{O}_{b}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the dual of
U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})_{n}\otimes_{E}W
.
Now, the proof is completed in the same way as in
[
OS14
, §3.4]
by observing that

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}(\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}

\displaystyle\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}(\mathcal{O}_{b}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}}

\displaystyle\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}(\mathcal{O}_{b}(\overline{\mathbf{N}}_{E,n})\otimes_{E}W^{\prime})^{\mathfrak{d}_{n}}

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\varprojlim_{n}\Ind_{\overline{J}_{0,n}}^{J}((U(\overline{\mathfrak{n}}_{E})_{n}\otimes_{E}W)/\mathfrak{d}_{n})\right)^{\prime}.\qed

Theorem A.2
.

The locally analytic representation
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,W,V)
does not depend on
W
up to canonical isomorphism. Thus, we may write simply
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,V)
, and it satisfies the following properties:

(i)

\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}
is a bi-functor that is contravariant in the first argument and covariant in the second.

(ii)

\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}
is exact on both arguments.

(iii)

\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,V)
is an admissible locally analytic representation, and if
V
is of finite length then it is strongly admissible.

(iv)

Assume that
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathbf{P}
is a parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
defined over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with Levi subgroup
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathbf{M}
such that
X\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}_{E}}_{\text{alg}}
.
Then,
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,V)\simeq\mathcal{F}_{P^{\prime}}^{G}(X,\Ind_{M^{\prime}\cap P}^{M^{\prime}}(V)^{\text{sm}})
.

Proof.
All of these statements follow exactly as the corresponding analogues in
[
OS14
]
:
that
\mathcal{F}_{P}^{G}(X,W,V)
does not depend on
W
up to canonical isomorphism follows as in
[
OS14
, §4.6]
from
Lemma

A.1
(ii),
(i) follows as in
[
OS14
, Proposition 4.7]
,
(ii) and (iv) follow as in
[
OS14
, Proposition 4.2 and Proposition 4.9]
(here one needs to use
Lemma

A.1
(ii) and (iii))
and (iii) follows as
[
OS14
, Proposition 4.8]
.
∎
